Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G1.1, G1.2, G1.3, G1.4, G1.5, G1.6, G1.7, G1.8, G1.10, G1.11). The report states Anthony's age as 55; the fact find [P4] records his age as 56. The date of birth is 06/01/1970. If the fact find was completed on 06/08/2025 (as recorded in [P2]), Anthony would be 55 until 06/01/2026, making age 55 correct as at the advice date. However, the fact find itself records age 56, which is inconsistent with the DOB of 06/01/1970 at the stated completion date of 06/08/2025. The report does not state Anthony's date of birth; the fact find records 06/01/1970.   Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   Anthony Barnes, aged 55, married to Yvette, aged 59. Both in good health. Three adult children (all financially independent) and three grandchildren. No date of birth stated in the report.

The policy research [P23] confirms client name Anthony Barnes. The suitability report itself [P12] states 'You are currently aged 55 and married to Yvette, aged 59.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states Anthony's age as 55; the fact find [P4] records his age as 56. The date of birth is 06/01/1970. If the fact find was completed on 06/08/2025 (as recorded in [P2]), Anthony would be 55 until 06/01/2026, making age 55 correct as at the advice date. However, the fact find itself records age 56, which is inconsistent with the DOB of 06/01/1970 at the stated completion date of 06/08/2025.

    The primary discrepancy is the age of Anthony Barnes. The report states 55; the fact find [P4] records 56. The DOB in [P4] is 06/01/1970. The fact find completion date is recorded as 06/08/2025 [P2]. As at 06/08/2025, Anthony would be 55 years old (his 56th birthday falls on 06/01/2026), so the report's age of 55 is arithmetically correct relative to the DOB. The fact find's own recorded age of 56 therefore appears to be an error in the fact find itself — the DOB and the advice-date age in the report are mutually consistent. This is a data quality issue in the fact find rather than a report error, but the discrepancy between the fact find's stated age (56) and the report's stated age (55) must be noted. The guard permits a 1-year tolerance where the fact find predates the report, and given the DOB confirms 55 is correct, this does not rise to a material concern about the report's accuracy. Yvette's age of 59 cannot be corroborated from evidence outside the report, as no fact find data for Yvette's DOB or independently recorded age appears in the retrieved passages; however, the absence of a KYC record for a spouse who is a co-client (she is named but not the primary advised client for pensions) is a mild gap. Marital status (Married) is confirmed by [P4]. The dependants (three adult children, three grandchildren, all financially independent) and health status are stated only in the suitability report [P12] with no independent corroboration in the fact find passages retrieved — the fact find does not appear to contain a section recording dependants or health in the retrieved passages, so this is unsupported but not contradicted. The income cessation point (client stopped working) is noted in the report but the fact find still shows active employment income, creating a minor temporal inconsistency that reflects the fact find being completed before cessation rather than a contradiction per se.

  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts that the client has not triggered the MPAA and does not have a lower annual allowance. The report is silent on income tax band, annual allowance amount, and territorial profile — these paths are absent from the extracted model. The report does include a general capital gains tax table showing rates for non-taxpayer, basic rate, higher rate and additional rate taxpayers, and discusses ISA and pension tax treatment generically.
    File:     The fact find (P21) records the client as UK domiciled and UK resident, not an expatriate, with National Insurance number NS371401B. The fact find (P9) records total gross annual earnings of £14,000 and net basic monthly income of £890. No document in the pack records the client's income tax band, MPAA status, or annual allowance as a client-specific figure. The transaction history (P3, P6) shows the existing Aviva pension is a pre-retirement arrangement with ongoing charges and fund switches, with no crystallisation events visible. The LOA response (P11) confirms no guarantees and that flexi-access drawdown is available, but makes no reference to MPAA triggering. No HMRC, pension scheme or fact-find field records a specific MPAA trigger event or confirms the standard annual allowance applies to this client.
    Discrepancies:

      - The report is silent on the client's income tax band; the fact find records £14,000 gross annual earnings and £890 net monthly income, which arithmetically suggests basic rate or possibly non-taxpayer status, but no tax band is explicitly recorded in any document.
      - The report states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' of £3,600 gross / £2,880 net, yet also states the client has stopped working altogether — the earnings figure underpinning the pension contribution relief claim is not evidenced in any document.
    The sole MPAA assertion in the report ('You have not yet triggered the MPAA, and you do not have a lower annual allowance') is an [I] passage and cannot self-corroborate. No external document — fact find, provider LOA, transaction history — independently confirms that no crystallisation or flexi-access drawdown trigger has occurred. The transaction history shows pre-retirement charges and small fund sales but no UFPLS or drawdown commencement entries; however, absence of a transaction is not positive confirmation that MPAA has not been triggered, particularly as the LOA response explicitly notes UFPLS is 'not applicable to this plan' but does not address whether flexi-access drawdown has ever been taken elsewhere. The income tax band is not recorded as a client-specific figure anywhere in the pack; the fact find's £14,000 gross earnings figure (P9) is consistent with basic rate but is not labelled as such and predates the advice date. The territorial profile (UK domicile and residence confirmed at P21) means the Scottish rate guard does not apply. The earnings sufficiency assertion for the pension contribution — material because it determines whether the £3,600 gross contribution is permissible at all — is unsupported: the report simultaneously states the client has stopped working and that earnings are sufficient, without quantifying them. This is an internal tension already identified in the extraction's internalInconsistencies, and no external document resolves it. The MPAA non-trigger claim is the most material: it directly supports the recommendation to make a pension contribution, and no evidence file corroborates it.

    Several discrepancies arise between the suitability report and the fact-find evidence. First, the report describes Anthony's career as a 'factory role after 35 years of shift work', whereas the fact find [P1] records his occupation simply as 'Driver' with no reference to factory work or length of service — the 35-year figure is unmatched anywhere in the file. The fact find does not record cessation of employment, creating a data quality concern about whether the file was updated to reflect his retirement from the driving role. Third, Yvette's employment details (Medical Case Handler, £28,000 gross, intention to reduce hours, planned retirement at Anthony's age 60) are stated in the report but no corroborating evidence — no fact find entry, no income table row — appears in the retrieved passages. This is an absence of supporting evidence for a materially stated financial fact (Yvette's income feeds into household income and cashflow modelling). The guard notes that intended retirement age in a fact find may be superseded by a later file note; [P4] corroborates the target retirement age of 60 for Anthony, and [P16] confirms the £2,200 net monthly income target — these are consistent. Anthony's £890 net monthly income is corroborated by [P13]. However, the absence of Yvette's income from the fact find, the mismatch between 'factory worker' and 'Driver', the unrecorded employment cessation, and the unmatched £28,000 figure collectively represent material gaps in the evidential base for employment and income facts that the report relies on.

    Three material issues are identified. First and most significant: the report states Yvette earns £28,000 gross p.a. in full-time employment, but [P9] is headed 'Yvette reduced inc calcs' and shows a gross income of £16,800 p.a. / £1,400/month, with take-home of £1,301.30/month. The existence of this document in the file as a supporting calculation suggests the cashflow model may have been run (or at least tested) on a reduced income figure of £16,800, while the report continues to present £28,000 as the current figure. However the fact find [P1] records total net monthly income as £2,590, and [P2] (cashflow current situation) shows 2025 total money in as £38,800 (= £22,000 DB pension + £16,800 reduced Yvette salary, not £28,000). The fact find [P1] records total monthly expenditure as £2,206.67 — this is the cashflow model input — but it contradicts the narrative essential expenditure of £2,493 stated in the same suitability report. Third: the fact find total net monthly income of £2,590 only reconciles if Yvette's income is approximately £16,800 p.a. (£1,400/month net approx £890 take-home at lower tax... actually £1,301 per [P9]) rather than £28,000, and Anthony's DB pension of £1,700/month, totalling approximately £3,001 — but £2,590 is closer to £1,700 (Anthony DB) + £890 (ceased employment) = £2,590, suggesting the fact find captured income including the now-ceased £890 part-time work rather than Yvette's income. This creates further ambiguity about which income figures underpin the cashflow model. The gross/net guard is respected throughout — all comparisons are net-to-net or gross-to-gross. The discrepancy on Yvette's income is material because the cashflow model (£38,800 total income in 2025) is consistent with £16,800 not £28,000, and this income figure drives the affordability and surplus conclusions in the report.

    Discrepancies:

      - The cashflow model [P6] shows the Aviva GIA opening value as £52,326, whereas the report recommends a GIA investment of £57,120. These differ by £4,794.       - The fact-find [P18] records 'MoneyBox Cash ISA 4.5%' with value £20,000 under Anthony Barnes (C1), whereas the report attributes the £20,000 Cash ISA with Moneybox to Yvette (C2). The fact-find lists both a 'MoneyBox Cash ISA 4.5%' and a 'Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA' each at £20,000 under Anthony Barnes — ownership labelling in fact-find is ambiguous (all assets listed under Anthony Barnes as the primary client), but the report correctly identifies one as Yvette's.       - The report states the client's Cash ISA of £20,000 is a separate asset from Yvette's Moneybox ISA. In the cashflow model [P6] the 'Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA' is shown at £20,000 but the Aviva ISA (stocks and shares) is shown at £20,000 — implying the client's Cash ISA has been converted/transferred into the Aviva ISA for cashflow modelling purposes, which is consistent with the recommendation but the opening-value description has changed from Cash ISA to ISA Stocks & Shares without explicit reconciliation.
      - The report's narrative states 'remaining £60,000' of the inheritance to be invested in the GIA after £40,000 renovations, but the actual GIA recommendation is £57,120 — a difference of £2,880 — and the cashflow model uses £52,326. Three figures (£60,000 intended, £57,120 recommended, £52,326 modelled) do not reconcile and are not explained in the report.
    The core asset values in the suitability report (£400,000 property, £100,000 inheritance, £40,000 Chartered savings, £20,000 client's ISA, £20,000 Yvette's Moneybox ISA, £10,000 emergency fund, £0 liabilities) are all corroborated by the fact-find [P18] and [P11]. The Assets & Liabilities table total of £669,092 is arithmetically consistent with its components. No liability discrepancy exists.
    
    However, two material discrepancies remain after the guards. First, the pension value used in the report (£79,092 as at 2025-11-23) differs from the cashflow model opening value of £82,692 [P6] by exactly £3,600 — the gross contribution amount. The report does not state the cashflow uses a post-contribution value, so this is an unexplained inconsistency between a figure the report relies on in the assets table and the figure used in cashflow modelling. The derived figures confirm that £82,692 is the implied base for the new plan charges, not the £79,092 pre-contribution value — the report uses two different pension values in different sections without reconciliation.
    
    Second, the GIA investment amount is stated as £57,120 in the recommendation, as £60,000 (remaining inheritance) in the narrative, and as £52,326 in the cashflow model opening values [P6]. Three different figures appear across the report for the same asset without explanation. The pension value discrepancy and the three-way GIA figure inconsistency are genuine findings that are not explained by any guard.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Both clients (Anthony and Yvette Barnes) expect to receive the full State Pension of £11,973 per annum gross from age 67, not yet in payment.
    File:     Cashflow models [P2], [P7], [P9] all show 'State Pension of £11,973 (inflated by 2.5%) from age 67' for Anthony. Cashflow files for Yvette [P5], [P6] record STATE PENSION AGE as 67. The £11,973 figure and age 67 are therefore corroborated for at least one client. The fact-find [P15] leaves all state pension entitlement fields blank (State Pension Retirement Age, Basic State Pension, Additional State Pension, BR19 Projection all blank). No HMRC state pension forecast or Check Your State Pension statement is present in the file for either client. The cashflow model repeats the report's own figure rather than providing independent evidence of entitlement.
    Discrepancies:
      - The fact-find [P15] has all state pension entitlement fields blank — no state pension age, no basic or additional state pension amount, no BR19 projection recorded for Anthony. No equivalent fact-find entry for Yvette's state pension is visible in the retrieved passages.
      - No independent HMRC forecast, Check Your State Pension document or BR19 projection is present in the file for either client to corroborate the £11,973 figure or full-entitlement claim.
      - The figure-match check notes that the second instance of '67,' (Yvette's state pension age) was not matched in the passages — only Anthony's is directly corroborated by [P2].
      - The cashflow models are category [F] documents that repeat the adviser's own inputs; they are not independent evidence of actual state pension entitlement.
    The report asserts that both clients will receive the full State Pension of £11,973 per annum from age 67. The cashflow models ([P2], [P7], [P9]) repeat the same figure and the same age 67, and the cashflow inputs for Yvette [P5], [P6] record STATE PENSION AGE as 67. However, cashflow models are adviser-constructed documents that merely reflect what the adviser entered; they are not independent corroboration of actual state pension entitlement. The fact-find [P15] is the natural place to record state pension details but every relevant field is blank. No HMRC forecast, Check Your State Pension printout or BR19 projection is present for either client. The full-entitlement claim (£11,973 at current full new State Pension rate) and the age-67 commencement are therefore unsupported by any independent evidence. The state pension amount is a material income figure used directly in the cashflow modelling to assess retirement adequacy, so the absence of supporting documentation is a Potential Concern rather than merely presentational. The guard about state pension age versus retirement age does not apply here — the report uses 67 for both and the cashflow models confirm 67 as the state pension age specifically.

  [G1.7] Existing products (pensions, investments and protection plans) are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   Existing arrangement EA1: provider Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd, policy number AV2075926-001, plan type SIPP, current value and transfer value £79,092.32 as at 23/11/2025, no ongoing contributions, holdings: Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity 35.02%, Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity 64.95%, Cash 0.03%.
    File:     [P12] (Aviva client report dated 23/11/2025) shows account AV2075926-001 'Pension Portfolio Pre-Retirement' with closing value £79,092.32, holdings VANGUARD LIFESTRATEGY 60% EQUITY - ACC 35.02% / £27,694.85, VANGUARD LIFESTRATEGY 80% EQUITY - ACC 64.95% / £51,371.06, CASH 0.03% / £26.41, investment holdings total £79,092.32. [P1] (Fact Find) records policy AV2075926-001, provider 'Aviva Platform', contract type 'Personal Pension Plan', value £88,590.88 (a later date figure — policy start date shown as 09/03/2026 which appears to be a data-entry anomaly). The Fact Find labels the plan type as 'Personal Pension Plan' not SIPP. The Aviva LOA document [P3] describes the plan as 'Pension Portfolio' with flexi-access drawdown available, no annuity rates, consistent with a SIPP-style arrangement. [P4] confirms closing value £79,092.32 for period ending 23/11/2025.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report describes plan type as SIPP; Fact Find [P1] records it as 'Personal Pension Plan' (Contract Type field).
      - Fact Find [P1] records a fund value of £88,590.88 against policy AV2075926-001, which differs from the report's stated value of £79,092.32 — however the Fact Find's 'Policy Start Date' shows 09/03/2026, suggesting this value may be post-advice or post-contribution, not the pre-advice value used in the report. The as-at dates are not reconciled in the Fact Find.
      - The report states the provider as 'Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd'; the Fact Find [P1] records the provider as 'Aviva Platform' — these are different legal entity / platform labels.
      - Holdings allocations (35.02%, 64.95%, 0.03%) sum to 100.00% — no arithmetic issue, confirmed by [P12].
    The core value assertion (£79,092.32 as at 23/11/2025) is strongly corroborated by [P12] and [P4], which are Aviva-generated client reports for the same account number and same date. Holdings (35.02% / 64.95% / 0.03%) are confirmed to the decimal by [P12]. The transfer value equals the current value, which is consistent with [P10] ('The transfer value of this plan is the same as the current value'). Three differences remain after reviewing the guards. First, the plan-type label: the report calls it a SIPP; the Fact Find calls it a 'Personal Pension Plan'. The Aviva LOA [P3] describes a 'Pension Portfolio' with SIPP-compatible features (flexi-access drawdown, no GAR, UFPLS not applicable). This is a labelling difference and does not affect any figure that drives the recommendation, so it is a low-severity inconsistency. Second, the provider name: 'Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd' (report) vs 'Aviva Platform' (Fact Find) — these refer to the same underlying arrangement by the same policy number, so this is a presentation difference rather than a material discrepancy. Third, and more significant, the Fact Find records £88,590.88 against the same policy number AV2075926-001. The 'Policy Start Date' of 09/03/2026 in the Fact Find is anomalous (it is after the advice date) and strongly suggests the Fact Find was updated post-recommendation (e.g. to reflect the new plan value after the recommended contribution and transfer). The as-at date of this figure is not stated, which prevents a clean reconciliation. The Aviva client report [P12] is the authoritative pre-advice valuation at 23/11/2025 and matches the report exactly. The £88,590.88 figure in the Fact Find is unreconciled and its date is unclear; it could represent the projected post-transfer value (£79,092.32 + £3,600 gross contribution + growth ≈ £82,692 in charges calculations, or approximately £88,590 at a later date). The report does not reference £88,590.88 anywhere, and the Fact Find provides no as-at date for this value. This is a data quality issue in the Fact Find rather than a contradiction of the report, but because the Fact Find is a supporting evidence document and the figure is materially different (£9,498.56 / 12%), it requires clarification. Overall: the report's key assertions (value, holdings, policy number) are corroborated by [P12]. The £88,590.88 in the Fact Find is an unreconciled figure of uncertain date that warrants a note but does not directly contradict the report's use of the 23/11/2025 valuation. The plan-type label difference (SIPP vs Personal Pension Plan) is a minor labelling inconsistency.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report contains several internal tensions: (1) Existing plan charges: 0.32% + 0.22% = 0.54% / £427 on a base of ~£79,092 — consistent in percentage terms; new plan charges 0.62% / £512.69 use an implied base of ~£82,692 (post-contribution), not the £79,092 pre-contribution value used for the existing plan comparison. (2) Existing pension risk level stated as 6 (Existing Arrangements table) but individual Vanguard funds described as risk 5 and risk 7 in the Alternative Solutions section. (3) Target retirement income stated as ~£2,200 net/month (Objectives), cashflow modeller input records ~£2,206/month expenditure, but current expenditure stated as £2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + ~£5,000 annual holidays ≈ £3,710/month — the modelled figure is materially inconsistent with stated current expenditure. (4) Client intends to invest 'remaining £60,000' of inheritance after £40,000 renovations, but GIA investment recommended is £57,120 — a £2,880 shortfall not explicitly reconciled in the report. (5) Client described as having 'stopped working altogether' yet the report simultaneously states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' of £2,880 net / £3,600 gross, while also admitting 'We are unsure of your earnings this tax year'.
    File:     This requirement compares the report against itself. No external supporting documents were searched. The internal inconsistencies are assessed solely from the report's own assertions.
    Discrepancies:
      - Charge comparison bases differ: existing plan charges (0.54%, £427) calculated on ~£79,092 pre-contribution value; new plan charges (0.62%, £512.69) calculated on ~£82,692 post-contribution value — monetary figures are not directly comparable on a like-for-like basis.
      - Existing pension risk level stated as 6 in Existing Arrangements table, but the same holding is described as two funds at risk 5 and risk 7 in the Alternative Solutions section — two different characterisations of the same arrangement.
      - Cashflow modeller expenditure input (~£2,206/month) is materially lower than stated current expenditure (£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + ~£417/month holiday allocation ≈ £3,710/month equivalent), with no reconciliation provided.
      - Target retirement income (~£2,200/month) appears to be used interchangeably with the cashflow modeller expenditure figure (~£2,206/month), conflating future income target with current expenditure.
      - GIA investment amount (£57,120) does not match the stated intention to invest the 'remaining £60,000' of inheritance — a £2,880 difference that is not explicitly reconciled in the report.
      - Report states client 'stopped working altogether' but simultaneously asserts 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' — contradictory statements about employment status and earnings, compounded by the admission 'We are unsure of your earnings this tax year'.
    Six discrepancies are identified, all internal to the report. Working through them in order of materiality: (1) The charge base inconsistency: the switch charges table comparison uses £79,092 as the base for both existing (£427 at 0.54%) and the monetary difference (£63 at 0.08%), but the new plan Annual Recurring Charges table uses ~£82,692 (post £3,600 contribution) to arrive at £512.69 at 0.62%. This means the £63 monetary difference in the switch table and the £85.69 implied difference from the two monetary figures (£512.69 - £427.00) are not directly comparable — the report does not flag this. This is a data inconsistency in the charge presentation, though the percentage difference (0.08%) is correctly stated. (2) The risk level discrepancy (6 overall vs. 5 and 7 for individual funds) is internally inconsistent — the report uses different characterisations of the same holding in different sections. A blended 6 is arithmetically plausible from 5 and 7 (equal split), but the report does not explain this, creating an apparent contradiction. (3) The cashflow modeller expenditure input of £2,206/month is materially inconsistent with stated current expenditure of approximately £3,710/month equivalent. No reconciliation is offered. The report appears to use the target income figure (£2,200) as the modeller input rather than actual expenditure — these are different concepts and should not be conflated. This is a material internal inconsistency that affects the cashflow modelling inputs. (4) The £60,000 vs £57,120 GIA discrepancy: the £2,880 difference exactly equals the net pension contribution, making the arithmetic plausible (inheritance remainder minus pension contribution = GIA amount), but the report does not state this explicitly. This is an unreconciled figure. (5) The employment/earnings contradiction: stating the client has stopped working while simultaneously asserting earnings sufficiency and admitting uncertainty is a direct internal contradiction on a figure (the £3,600 gross contribution) that drives the pension recommendation. The 'as at date' guard does not apply — both statements purport to describe the same current position. The charge base inconsistency and the cashflow modeller expenditure vs. stated expenditure mismatch are the most material findings. The employment/earnings contradiction also bears on a recommendation-driving figure. The GIA amount discrepancy and risk level presentation are lower-severity internal inconsistencies.

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report states the following income and expenditure figures: (1) Anthony's DB pension from Aon: £22,000 gross per annum / £1,700 net per month. (2) Anthony's prior part-time employment: £890 net per month (has since stopped). (3) Yvette's employment: £28,000 gross per annum. (4) Both expect full State Pension of £11,973 per annum from age 67. (5) Essential expenditure: £2,493 per month. (6) Discretionary entertainment: £800 per month. (7) Holidays: £5,000 annually (canonical model derives £416.67/month). The report also states cashflow modeller input expenditure of approximately £2,206 per month, which it presents alongside a stated current expenditure of ~£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + £5,000 annual holidays (approx. £3,710/month equivalent). A target retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month is stated in the objectives section.
    File:     The fact find (category B) records: Anthony's employed basic income as £890 net per month (Basic Income, Employed) and his private pension (Aon Final Salary) as £1,833 gross per month / £1,700 net per month. Total income for Anthony: £2,999.67/month (combined). The fact find records total monthly expenditure as £2,206.67. The fact find expenditure breakdown includes: Holidays £5,000 annually and Entertainment £800 monthly. The fact find total net monthly income is £2,590.00. A separate calculation file (Yvette reduced inc calcs, category F) shows Yvette's gross income as £16,800 per annum / £1,400 per month, not £28,000 per annum. The cashflow models (category F) show opening liquid assets + pensions of £225,018 in 2025. The fact find assets table records cash and property consistent with the report's liquid asset figures.
    Discrepancies:
      - Yvette's salary: report states £28,000 gross per annum; the Yvette reduced inc calcs file shows gross income of £16,800 per annum (£1,400/month) — a difference of £11,200 per annum.
      - Anthony's DB pension gross monthly: fact find records £1,833/month gross (implying ~£21,996/annum), report states £22,000 gross per annum — consistent within rounding, not a discrepancy.

      - Cashflow modeller expenditure input: report states ~£2,206/month; fact find records £2,206.67/month — consistent within rounding.
      - Stated current expenditure in narrative: ~£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + £416.67 holidays (£5,000/12) = ~£3,710/month; this materially exceeds the cashflow modeller input of £2,206/month and the fact find total of £2,206.67/month — the narrative expenditure figure does not reconcile with the cashflow modeller figure.
      - State Pension: report states £11,973 per annum for both; no corroborating evidence of this figure in the fact find (state pension entitlement fields are blank in the fact find).
      - Holidays expenditure: report states £5,000 annually; at /12 = £416.67/month; fact find corroborates £5,000 annually — consistent. The canonical model's derived monthly equivalent of £416.67 is arithmetically correct.
      - GIA investment: report states intention to invest 'remaining £60,000' of inheritance but recommends £57,120 — a £2,880 difference not explicitly reconciled in the report.
      - Target retirement income of ~£2,200/month (objectives) versus cashflow modeller input of ~£2,206/month — effectively the same figure used interchangeably; not a material discrepancy.
    The most material discrepancy is Yvette's stated salary. The suitability report asserts £28,000 gross per annum, but the supporting calculation file [P12] (category F, Yvette reduced inc calcs) shows a gross income of £16,800 per annum. This is a £11,200 difference — not explained by rounding, frequency conversion, or gross/net distinction, and the file figure cannot be reconciled to the report figure by any of the stated guards. This is a data inconsistency between the report assertion and a supporting evidence file. The second material issue is the internal inconsistency between the narrative expenditure (~£3,710/month combining essential £2,493 + discretionary £800 + holidays £416.67) and the cashflow modeller input of £2,206/month. The £2,206 figure matches the fact find total [P2] exactly, but the narrative breakdown sums to approximately £3,710/month — nearly £1,500/month more. The report uses these two different expenditure figures in different sections without reconciliation. The £2,206 figure appears to exclude discretionary and holiday expenditure items that the narrative section includes, but this is not stated. This is an internal inconsistency that also contradicts the cashflow evidence. The State Pension figure of £11,973 per annum appears in the report but the fact find state pension fields are entirely blank [P6], providing no corroboration. The £22,000 gross Aon DB pension is corroborated by the cashflow model [P13] showing £22,000 in-flow from 2030 onwards. The £1,700 net/month and £890 net/month figures are corroborated by [P1] and [P4] respectively. The Yvette salary discrepancy is the most significant finding as it could affect income adequacy assessment, cashflow projections and the overall financial position presented. The expenditure inconsistency is also material as the cashflow modelling appears to understate expenditure relative to the narrative by approximately £1,500/month. The £57,120 vs £60,000 GIA investment gap (£2,880) is noted but the report's own extraction flags this as a known internal tension; the arithmetic suggests the pension contribution accounts for it but this is not stated explicitly in the report — a disclosure shortfall.

  [G1.11] Facts used in calculations, cashflow modelling, projections and assumptions match the facts stated in the report narrative
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts cashflow was modelled (wasCashflowModelled=true) with the following key assumptions: general expenditure inflation 2%, savings growth 1.25%, state pension escalation 2.5%, property growth 4.7%, lending rate 4%, Aviva Platform investments growth 4.5%, surplus income saved 0%, low/mid/high critical yield growth rates 0.00%/2.94%/5.88%, retirement age used in critical yield 60, ongoing adviser charge 1% p.a. Cashflow modelled expenditure is stated as £2,206 per month. Yvette's employment income is included. Six scenarios are described including baseline (no shortfall), high inflation stress test at 6%, market crash (30% loss in 2028 with no recovery), Yvette predeceases in 2040, Anthony predeceases in 2040, and life expectancy adequacy. The report also states that Anthony is age 55 (DOB 06/01/1970) and Yvette is age 59. The report states current expenditure in the 'Your current situation' section as approximately £2,493/month essential plus £800/month discretionary plus ~£5,000 annual holidays, totalling approximately £3,710/month equivalent. The report also states the client 'stopped working altogether' but simultaneously states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level'. The GIA investment amount is £57,120, whereas the narrative states intention to invest 'remaining £60,000' of inheritance.
    File:     The cashflow model files confirm: general inflation 2%, property 4.7%, state pension 2.5%, lending 4%, surplus income saved 0%, market crash return override of -30% in 2028, and Anthony's age as 55 (DOB 06/01/1970). The market crash scenario file [P6] confirms the -30% override in 2028. The cashflow files for 'Tony Predeceases in 2040' and 'Yvette Predeceases in 2040' are present [P7,P8,P9,P10]. Anthony's retirement event is set at age 67 in 2037 across the cashflow files. The cashflow files do not record a savings growth rate of 1.25% — only general (2%), school fees (4%), property (4.7%), long-term care (4.5%), state pension (2.5%), lending (4%) appear in the inflation assumptions sections. The cashflow files do not show a separate line for 'Aviva Platform investments 4.5%' growth rate. The cashflow files do not record the £2,206/month expenditure input figure. The pension switch report [P18] corroborates the critical yield rates (2.94% mid, 5.88% high), retirement age 60 for critical yield, and the 1% ongoing adviser charge. Life expectancy figures of 84 (male aged 55) and 87 (female) are corroborated [P16].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states cashflow modelled expenditure as £2,206/month; the cashflow model files do not show this figure, and the report's own narrative states current expenditure of approximately £2,493/month essential + £800/month discretionary + ~£5,000/year holidays, totalling approximately £3,710/month — neither figure matches £2,206/month and the cashflow files do not confirm which figure was used as the input.
      - Report states a savings growth rate assumption of 1.25%; this rate does not appear in any of the cashflow model files — the files list only general, school fees, property, long-term care, state pension and lending rates.
      - Report states Aviva Platform investments growth rate of 4.5%; the cashflow model files do not show this as a named assumption in the inflation/return assumptions sections.
      - Report narrative states the client intends to invest 'remaining £60,000' of inheritance, but the recommended GIA investment is £57,120 — a £2,880 shortfall that is not explicitly reconciled in the report (though arithmetically equal to the net pension contribution).
      - Report states Anthony's age as 55 and Yvette's age as 59; Yvette's age is not corroborated in the cashflow files — only Anthony's age of 55/DOB 06/01/1970 appears.
      - Report states client 'stopped working altogether' but simultaneously asserts 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level', creating an internal contradiction on the earnings position.
      - Report states modelled expenditure of £2,206/month conflicts with its own stated current expenditure of ~£3,710/month equivalent — these are used interchangeably in different sections without reconciliation.
    Several material discrepancies exist between the cashflow assumptions stated in the report narrative and what appears in the supporting cashflow model files.
    
    1. EXPENDITURE INPUT: The report states the cashflow modelled expenditure as £2,206/month. The cashflow model files do not disclose this figure explicitly in the passages retrieved. The report's own 'current situation' section states current expenditure of approximately £2,493/month essential + £800/month discretionary + ~£5,000/year holidays (~£417/month), totalling approximately £3,710/month. The £2,206 figure is materially lower than the stated current expenditure and appears to conflate the target retirement income objective (~£2,200/month) with current expenditure. This is an internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction model, and no cashflow file passage confirms the £2,206 input. This is a data inconsistency that could affect the cashflow adequacy conclusion.
    
    2. SAVINGS GROWTH RATE: The report asserts a savings growth rate of 1.25%. None of the cashflow model files (P6, P8, P10) list this rate in their assumptions sections. The assumptions sections list general (2%), school fees (4%), property (4.7%), long-term care (4.5%), state pension (2.5%) and lending (4%). The 1.25% savings rate is unsupported by the model files.
    
    3. AVIVA PLATFORM INVESTMENTS GROWTH: The report states 4.5% for Aviva Platform investments. This does not appear as a named assumption in the cashflow files retrieved. It may be a return override rather than an inflation assumption, but it is not visible in the passages.
    
    4. GIA AMOUNT vs STATED INTENTION: The report states the client intends to invest 'the remaining £60,000' of inheritance but recommends £57,120 for the GIA. The £2,880 difference equals the net pension contribution, suggesting the funds were redirected, but this is not explicitly stated. This is a data inconsistency in the narrative.
    
    5. EARNINGS CONTRADICTION: The report states the client stopped working but also asserts earnings are sufficient to support the pension contribution. This internal contradiction is not resolved and affects the factual basis for the pension recommendation.
    
    6. The assumptions that ARE corroborated: general inflation 2% (P10), state pension 2.5% (P10), property 4.7% (P10), lending 4% (P10), surplus saved 0% (P10), market crash -30% in 2028 (P6), retirement age 60 for critical yield (P18), mid critical yield 2.94% (P18), high critical yield 5.88% (P18), ongoing adviser charge 1% (P18), Anthony age 55 (P10), retirement age 67 (P10).
    
    The guards are considered: the illustration growth rates guard does not apply here as the discrepancy is between the report's own narrative and its cashflow inputs, not between prescribed illustration rates and cashflow assumptions. The 1.25% savings rate absence in the cashflow files is a genuine gap — the cashflow files do not list it. The £2,206 vs £3,710 expenditure discrepancy is a material internal inconsistency that could affect whether the 'no shortfall' conclusion is robust. These are genuine findings.

CHK-002 — Objectives, Needs and Time-Horizon Alignment
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.3, G2.4, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7, G2.8). The report asserts an objective to simplify administration by consolidating onto one Aviva Platform (objective 4); no fact find note, file note or needs analysis records this as a client-stated objective. The report asserts an objective not to move the Chartered savings account at this time (objective 7); no fact find note or file note records this as a client-stated goal — it is presented as client-stated in the report. The report asserts an IHT monitoring objective (objective 8) and marks it as not client-stated; no supporting evidence in the file records this need having been identified or discussed with the client. 9 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts eight objectives: (1) ensure investments match Risk Level 4 Cautious Balanced profile; (2) invest inheritance tax-efficiently using a GIA and consider transferring Cash ISA to S&S ISA; (3) provide flexibility and growth to support a desired retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month; (4) simplify administration by consolidating onto one Aviva Platform with ongoing oversight; (5) take advantage of the pension annual allowance with a contribution of £3,600 gross (£2,880 net) in 2025/26; (6) review pension arrangements not assessed for six to seven years; (7) do not move the Chartered savings account (£40,000) at this time; (8) monitor IHT position given upcoming legislative changes from April 2027. Seven of the eight are marked as client-stated; the IHT monitoring objective (8) is not client-stated. No priority ranking or objective details are present in the extracted model and are noted as absent from the document.
    File:     The fact find retirement goals and objectives notes (P3) record: review his Aviva pension and look to improve upon it; wants to be fully retired by 60; minimum income in retirement is £2,200 net per month; looking at investment advice on £60k of his father's property money coming in this month and the £40k cash to avoid paying tax on interest; has already used his full ISA allowance this year, as has his wife; potentially interested in transferring his cash ISA to a S&S ISA but it would have to be really worth his while — not dead set on it. Adviser notes (P15) corroborate: about to receive circa £100k but will use £40k on kitchen/bathroom; has another £40k in high interest savings but only look at investing the £60k; possibly move ISAs — not likely though; only ATR 4; expected growth is 4.5%. P15 also notes the need to assess earnings for the year and whether a pension contribution is possible. P16 records: 'Retired, gets DB income, but works part-time. When will he stop? Defo 60?' The cashflow modelling scenarios (P6, P8, P9, P11, P14) evidence planning beyond the retirement age in a structured way. The risk profile report (P18, P20) records no additional notes on investment target or agreed risk level discussion. No file evidence specifically records the administration-simplification objective, the 'do not move Chartered savings' objective, or the IHT monitoring objective as client-stated goals.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts an objective to simplify administration by consolidating onto one Aviva Platform (objective 4); no fact find note, file note or needs analysis records this as a client-stated objective.
      - The report asserts an objective not to move the Chartered savings account at this time (objective 7); no fact find note or file note records this as a client-stated goal — it is presented as client-stated in the report.
      - The report asserts an IHT monitoring objective (objective 8) and marks it as not client-stated; no supporting evidence in the file records this need having been identified or discussed with the client.
      - The report states the target retirement income as approximately £2,200 net per month and the cashflow modeller uses approximately £2,206 per month as current expenditure — these are used interchangeably; the stated current expenditure in the report is approximately £3,293–£3,710 per month (£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + £5,000 annual holidays), which is materially inconsistent with the £2,200/£2,206 modelled figure and raises a concern about whether the income target is evidence-based or artificially low.
      - The fact find notes (P3) record the Cash ISA transfer as contingent ('would have to be really worth his while — not dead set on doing this') and P15 states 'not likely though', yet the report presents it as a positive client objective.
      - The report does not state a priority ranking for the eight objectives; the fact find declaration (P2) refers to objectives 'identified and prioritised in this document' but no ranking is recorded in either the report or the fact find.
      - The retirement target age of 60 is evidenced in P3 and P16 but the report does not explicitly state this as a planning horizon anchor in the objectives section (though it underlies the cashflow modelling).
      - The risk profile research report (P18, P20) records no notes on the investment target discussion or agreed risk level rationale, leaving the risk-alignment objective (objective 1) without evidenced discussion notes.
    Most core objectives are supported by evidence. The £2,200 net per month income target in retirement is recorded in P3 ('Min income in retirement is £2,200 net per month') and corroborated by P15, satisfying the minimum corroboration requirement for that objective. The pension review objective is evidenced in P3 ('Review his Aviva pension and look to improve upon it'). The tax-efficient investment of the inheritance is evidenced in P3 and P15. The retirement age of 60 is evidenced in P3 and P16. The pension contribution objective is evidenced in P15 (earnings and contribution assessment noted). The risk-alignment objective is partially supported by the agreed risk level of 4 in the research report (P10), though P18 and P20 record no discussion notes. However, several concerns arise. First, the administration-simplification objective (4) and the 'do not move Chartered savings' objective (7) appear in the report as client-stated but are not recorded in any fact find, file note or needs analysis in the file — their evidential basis is absent. Second, the IHT monitoring objective (8) is adviser-identified (correctly not client-stated) but no file evidence records this need being identified or discussed. Third, the income target of £2,200/month conflicts materially with stated current expenditure of approximately £3,293–£3,710/month; the cashflow modeller uses £2,206/month as the expenditure input, which is not reconciled against the higher current expenditure figure. This raises a genuine concern about whether the income target is grounded in a properly evidenced needs analysis or whether the cashflow model is understating future income needs. Fourth, the Cash ISA transfer objective is presented more positively in the report than the fact find and file notes justify — both P3 and P15 record the client as uncertain and unenthusiastic. Fifth, no priority order is recorded anywhere despite the fact find declaration referencing prioritised objectives. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability indicators are present in the file that materially affect this check. The guard that advisers paraphrase explains the wording differences between the report and file notes for the core objectives, but does not explain the absence of any file evidence for objectives 4, 7 and 8, nor the income target inconsistency.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) intended retirement age 60 for the client (Anthony Barnes, DOB 06/01/1970, currently 55); (2) time horizons classified as 'LongTerm' and 'ShortTerm' for different objectives; (3) life expectancy basis: ONS figure of approximately 84 for a 55-year-old male and 87 for a 59-year-old female; (4) the pension switch analysis uses age 60 as the retirement/maturity age. No explicit termYears, targetDate or targetAge fields were extracted from the objectives themselves.
    File:     Evidence from the case file: (1) Fact find [P23] records target age 60 and income goal of £2,200 net/month, with time horizon noted as 'Medium Term' — corroborating retirement age 60 but characterising the horizon as medium-term, not long-term. (2) Pension switch report [P2][P4][P6][P7] uses projected value at age 60 throughout, consistent with retirement age 60. (3) Cashflow models [P8][P10][P12][P14] record Anthony's 'Retire' event at age 67 in 2037 with STATE PENSION AGE 67 — a materially different retirement age from 60. [P17] also shows a timeline where 'retire in 2030' (age 60) appears as a separate event label for Yvette/Anthony (60/64 age band), alongside 'Retire (Anthony)' at 67. (4) Pension projections at age 67 [P15] and age 75 [P16] exist in the file, implying planning was also conducted to age 67 and beyond. (5) Life expectancy: the 84-year male figure is corroborated by [P17] (age 84 appears in timeline); the 87-year female figure is not matched — nearest is 85 in [P17], a 2.3% gap. (6) Adviser notes [P24] reference 'Medium Term' time horizon consistent with fact find. (7) Cashflow [P18] records Yvette's 'Retire' event at age 67 in 2033. (8) No passage corroborates the 'LongTerm' time horizon label for any objective.
    Discrepancies:
      - Retirement age in the report and pension switch analysis: 60; retirement age recorded in all cashflow models (Anthony): 67 — a 7-year difference.
      - The fact find [P23] records the time horizon as 'Medium Term'; the report asserts 'LongTerm' as one of the objective time horizons — these labels do not reconcile.
      - Life expectancy for a 59-year-old female stated in the report as 87; nearest figure in the evidence ([P17]) is 85 — a 2-year gap outside the 2-year tolerance.
      - Pension projections at age 67 [P15] and age 75 [P16] exist in the file, suggesting planning horizons extending well beyond 60, but the report does not explain the relationship between these and the stated retirement age of 60.
      - No extracted termYears, targetDate or targetAge from objectives — the report does not quantify the 'LongTerm' or 'ShortTerm' horizons in years, leaving them unverifiable against evidence.
      - The cashflow model [P17] shows a 'retire in 2030' label (age 60/64 band) but Anthony's formal 'Retire' event is set at age 67 — creating ambiguity about which retirement date the cashflow plan actually models.
    The central discrepancy is the conflict between the stated retirement age of 60 (in the report and pension switch analysis) and the cashflow model's 'Retire' event for Anthony set at age 67 ([P8], [P10], [P12], [P14]). The guard notes that a pension's investment horizon and the income goal date can differ, and that a switch report routinely uses its own retirement age. Taken together, the switch report's use of age 60 is consistent with the report's stated target, but the cashflow modelling tool — which is the primary evidence base for the planning assumptions — uses age 67. The label 'retire in 2030' visible in [P17] at the 60/64 age band does not resolve this: Anthony's formal 'Retire' event in every cashflow scenario is age 67/2037. The 7-year divergence between the report's planning horizon (age 60) and the cashflow model's retirement event (age 67) is material: it means the strategy and projections underpinning the suitability assessment may have been modelled to different end dates, which affects whether the recommendation can demonstrably deliver the stated objective by the stated date. The fact find [P23] does corroborate target age 60, so the client's stated goal is evidenced; but the planning tool does not reflect it consistently. The 'LongTerm' horizon label in the report is not corroborated by any external evidence — the fact find says 'Medium Term' — and no termYears are stated, so the horizon used to justify the strategy cannot be verified. The female life expectancy figure of 87 is unsupported (nearest evidence is 85), though this is a lower-severity issue. The adviser notes [P24] and fact find both use 'medium term', which conflicts with the 'LongTerm' label. No vulnerability indicators in the evidence appear to materially alter this assessment for CHK-002 purposes.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) Emergency fund of £10,000 held, confirmed sufficient (no shortfall). Client has 'significant liquid cash available should you require instant access to funds' during the switching period. (2) Planned withdrawal of circa £40,000 from inheritance for home renovations. (3) Planned investment of remaining £60,000 of inheritance (possibly including Cash ISA). (4) Ad-hoc access to Aviva Platform investments typically takes 10–15 working days. Total liquid assets stated as £190,000 comprising £100,000 inheritance, £40,000 Chartered savings, £20,000 client's Cash ISA, £20,000 Yvette's Cash ISA, and £10,000 emergency fund.
    File:     The fact find [P18] records: Emergency Fund amount put aside £10,000.00; Amount of Emergency Fund Required £10,000.00; Emergency Fund Shortfall £0.00. Total monthly household expenditure £2,206.67; total net monthly income £2,590.00; disposable income £383.33. The cashflow models [P4, P6, P8, P10] confirm Anthony's retirement event at age 67 in 2037 and use a current expenditure figure consistent with £2,206/month. The cashflow models show liquid assets and accessible pensions remaining positive across all scenarios. No document in the file independently corroborates the £40,000 renovation intention, the £60,000 investment intention, or the 10–15 working day ad-hoc access timeframe — these appear only in the suitability report (category I). The cashflow modeller inputs [P21] include the £57,120 GIA and £20,000 ISA transfer, consistent with part of the stated investment plan, but the £60,000 stated intention versus £57,120 actual GIA recommendation is not reconciled in any evidential document.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the client intends to invest 'the remaining £60,000' of inheritance, but the recommended GIA is £57,120 — a £2,880 difference that is not explicitly reconciled in any supporting document (though it plausibly equals the net pension contribution).
      - The £40,000 renovation plan and the £60,000 investment intention appear only in the suitability report; no independent fact-find entry, client questionnaire or meeting note in the file corroborates these planned uses of the inheritance.
      - The 10–15 working day ad-hoc access timeframe is stated only in the suitability report; no product literature or platform terms document in the file independently corroborates this figure.
      - The report states current expenditure of approximately £2,206/month in the cashflow section, consistent with the fact find, but the internal inconsistency noted in extraction between this figure and the separately stated essential expenditure of ~£2,493 plus £800 discretionary (totalling ~£3,293/month) is not addressed in the access/liquidity section — if actual monthly outgoings are ~£3,293, a £10,000 emergency fund represents roughly 3 months of essential expenditure rather than the implied higher cushion.
      - The report asserts 'significant liquid cash available' as the basis for comfort with the switching period access gap, but the £10,000 ring-fenced emergency fund is the only independently evidenced liquid reserve specifically labelled as such; the remaining £180,000 is not verified as freely accessible in any non-report document.
    The emergency fund figure of £10,000 is directly corroborated by the fact find [P18], which records both the amount put aside and the amount required as £10,000 with zero shortfall. This element is supported. The cashflow models confirm the broader liquidity picture remains positive across scenarios [P2, P11, P13, P14, P16], providing indirect support for the assertion that liquid assets are sufficient during the switching period. However, the specific planned access items — the £40,000 renovation spend and the £60,000 investment intention — are stated only in the suitability report (category I passages P19, P21, P24). The report cannot corroborate itself: these claims are unsupported by any independent document. The £57,120 GIA versus £60,000 stated intention discrepancy is an unreconciled internal inconsistency; the most plausible explanation (that £2,880 net was directed to the pension contribution) is noted in the extraction but not stated in the report or in any evidential document. The 10–15 working day access timeframe is similarly self-asserted. The expenditure inconsistency (£2,206/month cashflow input versus ~£3,293/month current expenditure stated elsewhere) is material to assessing whether the £10,000 emergency fund is truly sufficient: at £3,293/month outgoings it represents approximately 3 months' cover, whereas at £2,206/month it represents approximately 4.5 months — the report treats sufficiency as confirmed without resolving this discrepancy. The vulnerability overlay does not raise additional concerns here based on available evidence. Overall: the emergency fund quantum is evidenced; the planned access items and ad-hoc access terms are asserted without independent evidential support; the £57,120/£60,000 discrepancy is unreconciled. These represent a disclosure shortfall and missing evidence concern at low-to-moderate severity.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report identifies the following foreseeable life changes: (1) Yvette intends to reduce working hours to 3 days per week from next year; (2) Yvette plans to fully retire when C1 (Anthony) turns 60; (3) C1 to receive State Pension of £11,973 p.a. from age 67; (4) C2 (Yvette) to receive State Pension of £11,973 p.a. from age 67; (5) inheritance of £100,000 due this month from late father's estate, c.£40,000 for renovations, £60,000 to be invested; (6) from 6 April 2027, unused DC pensions will be included in the taxable estate for IHT; (7) a small period of income shortfall when Yvette retires until her State Pension begins.
    File:     Cashflow models show Anthony DOB 06/01/1970 (age 55) and Yvette DOB 06/10/1966 (age 59). Anthony's retirement event is modelled at age 67 in 2037 and State Pension age 67. Yvette's retirement event is modelled at age 67 in 2033 and State Pension age 67. Yvette's State Pension is stated as £11,973 (inflated at 2.5%) from age 67 in the cashflow. Adviser notes record the inheritance of ~£100k with £40k earmarked for kitchen/bathroom. The cashflow uses £26,480 total regular expenses and does not show a specific retirement event for Yvette at Anthony's age 60. The cashflow models Yvette retiring at age 67 (2033), not when Anthony turns 60 (which would be 2030 when Yvette is ~63/64). Anthony's State Pension age is shown as 67 in the cashflow. There is no cashflow or fact-find entry that separately models or confirms Yvette reducing to 3 days per week from next year nor Yvette retiring at Anthony's age 60 (2030). Adviser notes mention 'Retired, gets DB income, but works part-time. When will he stop? Defo 60?' — this refers to Anthony, not Yvette.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states Yvette plans to fully retire when Anthony turns 60 (i.e. 2030, when Yvette would be approximately 63–64), but the cashflow models consistently show Yvette's retirement event at age 67 in 2033 — three years later than the report's stated intention.
      - The report asserts Yvette intends to reduce hours to 3 days per week from next year; no cashflow scenario models an intermediate step-down in Yvette's income between now and retirement, and no fact-find entry independently records this intention.
      - The adviser notes reference 'When will he stop? Defo 60?' in relation to the part-time worker (Anthony), not Yvette, raising a question whether the report correctly attributes the age-60 retirement intention to Yvette rather than Anthony.
      - The report states C1 will receive State Pension of £11,973 p.a. from age 67; the cashflow confirms Anthony's State Pension age as 67 but does not quote a £11,973 figure for Anthony — only for Yvette (P2).
      - The report states C2 (Yvette) will receive State Pension of £11,973 p.a. from age 67; this is corroborated by the cashflow (P2).
      - No independent evidence (fact find, meeting notes) corroborates the £11,973 State Pension figure for Anthony specifically.
    The most significant discrepancy is between the report's assertion that Yvette plans to fully retire when Anthony turns 60 (2030) and the cashflow models, which consistently place Yvette's retirement event at age 67 in 2033 across every scenario (P6, P8, P15). This is a direct contradiction between the report and the supporting cashflow evidence — the cashflow is the primary quantitative document evidencing planning assumptions, and it uses a retirement date for Yvette that is materially later than what the report states. This matters because the report also notes a 'small period of shortfall' when Yvette retires until her State Pension begins; if Yvette retires at 60 (as stated), the shortfall period is approximately 7 years, whereas if she retires at 67 (as modelled), the shortfall is minimal or zero. The planning assumptions used in the cashflow are inconsistent with the stated life-change. The adviser note 'When will he stop? The reduction in Yvette's hours to 3 days per week from next year is stated in the report but is not modelled in any cashflow scenario as a step-down in income — this is an unsupported intermediate planning assumption. The IHT legislative change (6 April 2027) is a universal legislative development and per the guard is not a client-specific foreseeable change; no concern is raised on that item. The inheritance of £100,000 is corroborated by adviser notes. Yvette's State Pension of £11,973 from age 67 is corroborated by the cashflow. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability markers in the pack that would alter this assessment. The Yvette retirement-age mismatch between report and cashflow is a material concern because the income shortfall analysis and the capacity-for-loss assessment both depend on when Yvette retires.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states multiple objectives: (1) ensure investments match agreed Cautious Balanced risk level; (2) invest inheritance tax-efficiently via a GIA and consider transferring Cash ISA to Aviva Platform ISA; (3) review and switch existing Aviva pension to a suitable fund; (4) make a pension contribution of £3,600 gross (£2,880 net) in 2025/26 to use the annual allowance; (5) target retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month including the defined benefit pension; (6) fully retire by age 60 (per the research notes). The report also records £40,000 of the £100,000 inheritance earmarked for home renovations, £57,120 for the GIA, £2,880 net for the pension contribution, and £20,000 for the ISA transfer. The cashflow modeller uses approximately £2,206 per month as the expenditure input, while current stated expenditure is approximately £2,493 essential plus £800 discretionary plus ~£5,000 annually on holidays (totalling approximately £3,710/month equivalent). Anthony is aged 55 and described as recently retired, yet the cashflow models show a 'Retire' event for Anthony at age 67 in 2037. The report states the client wants to be fully retired by age 60, but the cashflow retirement event is set at age 67.
    File:     The fact find/policy research notes [P18] record client objective 3 as: 'Wants to be fully retired by age 60, so wants a plan for that.' The cashflow models [P14] and [P15] record Anthony's retirement event as age 67 in 2037, not age 60. The cashflow models [P11] show the Aviva GIA opening value as £52,326, not the £57,120 stated in the suitability report. The cashflow models show the Aviva Platform Personal Pension opening value as £82,692, consistent with the post-contribution figure. The expenditure modelled in the cashflow (implied from income/expenditure charts) is stated at approximately £2,206/month in the report, but the fact find records essential expenditure of £2,493/month plus £800 discretionary plus ~£5,000 annually on holidays. The research notes record 'Cash Reserve: £100k+' and objectives centred on tax-efficient investment of the inheritance and reviewing the existing pension, consistent with the report's objectives 1–4. No evidence in the file explicitly records a target retirement income of £2,200 net per month as a client-stated figure independent of the report itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - Retirement age conflict: the suitability report records the client's objective as being 'fully retired by age 60' (per [P18] objective 3), but the cashflow models ([P14], [P15]) set Anthony's 'Retire' event at age 67 in 2037 — a 7-year discrepancy. The report does not resolve or acknowledge this.
      - Cashflow modeller expenditure input (~£2,206/month) is materially lower than the stated current expenditure of ~£3,710/month equivalent (£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + ~£417/month holiday equivalent) from [P3]. The lower figure is used to demonstrate no shortfall, but may understate the income requirement.
      - GIA investment amount discrepancy: the suitability report recommends a GIA investment of £57,120 but the cashflow model [P11] records the Aviva GIA opening value as £52,326 — a difference of £4,794. The report also states the client's intention was to invest 'remaining £60,000' of inheritance, which neither figure matches exactly.
      - The target retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month is stated in the report as an objective, but the cashflow modeller input uses £2,206/month as current expenditure, conflating expenditure with income target. Current actual expenditure is substantially higher at ~£3,710/month.
      - No priority ranking is given for the objectives, yet multiple goals compete for the same £100,000 inheritance capital (renovations £40,000, GIA £57,120, pension contribution £2,880, ISA transfer from existing savings) — the report does not explicitly resolve the allocation or acknowledge the competition.
      - The report states the client recently retired and has stopped working altogether, yet simultaneously asserts earnings are sufficient to support the £3,600 gross pension contribution, which is internally inconsistent with the retirement status.
    The most material conflict for this check is the retirement age discrepancy. The research notes [P18] clearly record the client's objective as being 'fully retired by age 60', which would imply a planning horizon to age 60 (approximately 5 years from advice date at age 55). However, the cashflow models in both the current situation and stress-test scenarios ([P14], [P15]) set Anthony's retirement event at age 67 — the state pension age — not age 60. The suitability report does not address this discrepancy, explain why the planning horizon has shifted to age 67, or demonstrate that the recommended investments and pension strategy are consistent with a retirement at age 60. This is a structural conflict: the client states a goal (retire at 60) that the planning model does not reflect (models to 67). The client is already described as having retired from his factory role and stopped part-time work, so in one reading he has already retired — but the cashflow treats age 67 as the retirement event. The report does not reconcile these. The expenditure modelling discrepancy is also material: the cashflow uses ~£2,206/month to show no shortfall, but actual stated expenditure is approximately £3,710/month. Using the lower figure flatters the cashflow outcome and may mask a genuine income shortfall against the client's actual lifestyle costs. The GIA amount discrepancy (£57,120 in the report vs £52,326 in the cashflow) is unexplained and means the cashflow model is not modelling the actual recommended investment. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that bear on this check. The internal inconsistency regarding the earnings basis for the pension contribution is noted but falls primarily within CHK-006. The retirement age conflict and expenditure modelling shortfall are the primary concerns here.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies five recommendations linked to six objectives: OBJ1 (ensure investments match Cautious Balanced risk level), OBJ2 (invest inheritance tax-efficiently using GIA), OBJ3 (target retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month), OBJ4 (consider transferring Cash ISA), OBJ5 (make a pension contribution for this tax year), OBJ6 (IHT/estate planning). The pension (NP1) is linked to OBJ1, OBJ4 and OBJ6; the ISA to OBJ1 and OBJ6; the pension contribution to OBJ5 and OBJ3; and two further recommendations to OBJ2, OBJ3 and OBJ4. The client is aged 55 with a stated retirement age of 60 (a 5-year investment term for the pension). The cashflow modeller inputs monthly expenditure as approximately £2,206 per month. The report states a target income of approximately £2,200 net per month including the DB pension. An emergency fund of £10,000 is noted and the client confirms sufficient liquid capital is retained. Yvette (aged 59) is noted as intending to reduce hours next year. The client recently retired and stopped working altogether. The pension access mechanism is Flexi Access Drawdown from age 55 (rising to 57 from April 2028), and the selected retirement age is 60. The report notes that from 6 April 2027 unused DC pensions will be included in the taxable estate for IHT purposes. The client intends to spend circa £40,000 of inheritance on renovations. The GIA investment is £57,120 against a stated intention to invest 'remaining £60,000'.
    File:     The cashflow files confirm Anthony is aged 55 and Yvette 59 (ages 55/59 in the asset statement). The cashflow models use £82,692 as the pension fund value (post-contribution), 4.5% expected return, 0.63% ongoing charges plus 1% financial planning fee. Total regular expenses in the cashflow are modelled at £26,480 per annum (comprising entertainment £9,600, household £8,760, holiday £5,000, miscellaneous £480, transport £2,640). The suitability report separately states current regular expenditure of approximately £2,493 per month (essential) plus £800 discretionary plus approximately £5,000 annually on holidays, totalling approximately £3,710 per month equivalent. The cashflow modeller input is stated in the report as approximately £2,206 per month — this maps to approximately £26,472 per annum, which aligns with the cashflow file total expenses of £26,480. Yvette's retirement event is set at age 67 (2033) in the cashflow. The pension switch report confirms the projected retirement age of 60. The minimum pension age is shown as 55. No separate needs analysis document is present in the retrieved passages. The cashflow scenarios modelled include current situation, higher inflation, market crash, Tony predeceases in 2040, and Yvette predeceases in 2040 — confirming foreseeable life changes (mortality scenarios) were considered. The £82,692 pension value in the cashflow files corresponds to the post-contribution value (£79,092 + £3,600 gross), consistent with the report's own figures.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states a target retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month (objectives section), while the cashflow modeller input uses approximately £2,206 per month as current expenditure — these are used interchangeably but conflate income target with current expenditure, which is not the same concept.
      - The report states current regular expenditure of approximately £2,493 per month (essential) plus £800 discretionary plus approximately £5,000 annually on holidays (totalling approximately £3,710 per month equivalent), but the cashflow modeller input is stated as approximately £2,206 per month. The cashflow files confirm £26,480 per annum (approximately £2,207 per month), which is materially lower than the stated current expenditure of approximately £3,710 per month. The reason for this discrepancy (whether expenditure is expected to reduce in retirement or a different period is being modelled) is not explained in the report.
      - The client states an intention to invest 'the remaining £60,000' of inheritance, but the GIA investment recommended is £57,120, a shortfall of £2,880 that is not explicitly reconciled in the report (though it is plausible the £2,880 net pension contribution accounts for it).
      - Yvette's retirement is modelled at age 67 in the cashflow file, but the report notes she intends to reduce hours to 3 days per week from next year — the cashflow models employment income of £28,000 pro-rata for 3 days per week, which partially reflects this, but the transition to reduced hours and then full retirement is not explicitly discussed as a foreseeable life change in the objective or planning sections.
      - The report states the client 'stopped working altogether' and has no ongoing employment income, yet also asserts 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level', without quantifying those earnings against the £3,600 gross contribution threshold. The maximum non-earner contribution coincides with the recommended amount, suggesting this may be relying on the non-earner limit rather than actual earnings.
    The objectives are identified in the report and the recommendations are each linked to named objective IDs, which satisfies the threshold for specific linkage rather than pure boilerplate. The pension is linked to risk alignment (OBJ1), ISA transfer (OBJ4) and IHT planning (OBJ6); the GIA and ISA to tax-efficient investment of inheritance (OBJ2) and income target (OBJ3); and the pension contribution to annual allowance utilisation (OBJ5) and income target (OBJ3). These are specific enough to pass the boilerplate guard. The access mechanism is Flexi Access Drawdown from a selected retirement age of 60. The client is currently 55. The minimum pension age rises to 57 in April 2028, meaning there is no access conflict for the intended retirement age of 60. The pension access guard is therefore satisfied. The most material concern is the expenditure/income target inconsistency. The report states current monthly expenditure of approximately £3,710 equivalent (£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + £5,000/12 holiday), yet the cashflow modeller is fed only £2,206 per month, and the target retirement income of £2,200 is derived from or conflated with this lower figure. The cashflow files confirm the modelled expenses of £26,480 per annum (£2,207 per month), which aligns with the report's cashflow input but not with the report's own stated current expenditure. This means the cashflow modelling — the key tool used to assess whether the recommendations can deliver the stated income objective (OBJ3) — appears to understate current and projected expenditure by approximately £1,500 per month. The report does not explain whether expenditure is expected to fall materially in retirement, whether the lower figure represents a post-retirement budget, or whether the higher figure includes items not expected to continue. This is a genuine inconsistency between the expenditure figure used to justify the strategy and the stated current expenditure, which weakens the evidential basis for OBJ3. The £60,000 vs £57,120 GIA discrepancy is internally noted in the extraction but the plausible explanation (that the £2,880 net pension contribution accounts for the difference) is not stated in the report, leaving the reconciliation of source of funds incomplete. Yvette's planned reduction in working hours is partially addressed in the cashflow (employment income modelled at 3-day rate), and mortality scenarios are modelled, evidencing that foreseeable changes were considered. The earnings sufficiency point for the pension contribution creates a further weakness in the justification for OBJ5, but this is primarily a CHK-006 concern and is noted here only insofar as it bears on whether OBJ5 is achievable. The primary finding for this check is the material inconsistency between the expenditure figures used in the cashflow model and the stated current expenditure, which undermines the evidential support for the income planning objective (OBJ3) without explanation.

  [G2.7] Goals, assumptions or planning requirements stated in the report that are not supported by the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states eight objectives: (1) ensure investments match Cautious Balanced risk level 4; (2) invest inheritance tax-efficiently via GIA and consider Cash ISA to S&S ISA transfer; (3) provide flexibility and growth for target retirement income of approximately £2,200 net per month (including DB pension); (4) simplify administration on one Aviva Platform; (5) take pension annual allowance of £3,600 gross (£2,880 net) in 2025/26; (6) review pension arrangements not assessed for 6–7 years; (7) do not move Chartered savings (£40,000); (8) monitor IHT position given pension inclusion changes from April 2027. Target retirement age is 60. Planning assumptions include: general expenditure inflation 2%, savings growth 1.25%, state pension escalation 2.5%, property growth 4.7%, lending rate 4%, Aviva Platform investments growth 4.5%, surplus income saved 0%, low/mid/high growth rates for critical yield (0%, 2.94%, 5.88%).     File:     Fact find [P3] records: 'Wants to be fully retired by 60. Min income in retirement is £2,200 net per month. No evidence in the file of earnings being quantified against the pension contribution requirement.     Discrepancies:
