The report does not reconcile these figures.
      - The report uses a critical yield retirement age of 60 and the pension illustration projections in the file [P16][P17] use ages 67 and 75 — not age 60. No illustration projecting to age 60 is present in the file.
      - The report states the client has 'stopped working altogether' and separately states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' — an internal tension flagged in the extraction. No quantification of residual earnings appears in the file to support the sufficiency assertion.
      - The report states the client intends to invest 'the remaining £60,000' of inheritance in the GIA, but the recommended GIA investment is £57,120 — a £2,880 shortfall. The report does not explicitly reconcile this. [P7] adviser notes confirm '£60k' as the intended investment amount.
      - The IHT monitoring objective (OBJ8) is recorded as adviser-inferred (isClientStated: false) with no client-authored statement in the file to corroborate it as a client goal.
      - The report's cashflow modeller uses £2,206/month as current expenditure matching the fact find total [P5], yet separately the fact find [P10] records discretionary and holiday expenditure on top, suggesting the £2,206 figure may understate the full expenditure base used in the report's planning.
    Most of the key objectives are well evidenced. The target retirement age of 60 is confirmed in [P3] and [P9]. The £2,200/month income target is confirmed in [P3] and [P9]. The ATR 4 / 4.5% growth assumption is corroborated by [P7]. The £2,206/month cashflow figure matches [P5] exactly. However, several concerns arise. First, the pension projection illustrations in the file [P16][P17] project to ages 67 and 75 — not the stated planning horizon of age 60. The critical yield section of the report uses age 60, but no provider illustration to that age is present in the file to corroborate the projection basis, which is a gap between the stated planning horizon and the available evidence. Second, the expenditure figure used in cashflow modelling (£2,206/month) is drawn from the fact find total, but [P10] shows £800/month entertainment and £5,000/year holidays sitting outside this total (or included within it in a way that is not clear). The report models the retirement income target at £2,200/month, which is approximately equal to the modelled expenditure, but the full expenditure recorded in the file is materially higher. This conflation was flagged in the internal inconsistencies. The report does not explain how the client will meet holidays and entertainment costs from a £2,200/month income when current total expenditure is approximately £3,600+/month. Fourth, the £57,120 vs £60,000 GIA discrepancy is an unexplained difference, though the arithmetic of £60,000 minus £2,880 net pension contribution = £57,120 is plausible but not stated in the report. The IHT objective being adviser-inferred is disclosed in the extraction; file evidence does not show a client-authored IHT concern. On the vulnerability overlay: the ATRQ was not completed at the meeting [P1], and no vulnerability assessment outcome is present in the extraction. The client is recently retired at approximately 53 (born circa 1972 given the fact find date of 2025 and a target retirement age of 60, suggesting he is approaching but has not yet reached 60). No vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this check's conclusions. The primary concerns are: (a) the retirement income target of £2,200/month appears to understate full expenditure needs given [P10]; (b) no projection to the stated age-60 planning horizon exists in the file; (c) earnings sufficiency for the pension contribution is unquantified. These are material gaps between stated planning assumptions and supporting evidence.

  [G2.8] Target retirement income stated in the report is consistent with the evidence and with the figures the plan is modelled on
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states the client's target retirement income as approximately £2,200 net per month (Objectives section). The cashflow model uses £2,206 per month as the current expenditure input, effectively treating expenditure as the income target. Retirement age is 60. Cashflow assumptions include 2% general expenditure inflation, 4.5% Aviva Platform investments growth, 1.25% savings growth, 2.5% state pension escalation, 4.7% property growth, 4% lending rate, and 0% surplus income saved. The critical yield projections use retirement age 60 and mid growth rate 2.94%, high growth rate 5.88%.
    File:     The fact find [P1] and [P5] record the client's minimum income in retirement as £2,200 net per month with target age 60, corroborating the report's objective and retirement age. The fact find [P4] records total monthly household expenditure of £2,206.67, which is the figure the cashflow model uses as expenditure. The fact find [P4] also records total net monthly income of £2,590.00. The expenditure breakdown in [P15] and [P16] includes: council tax £130, electricity £160, holidays £5,000 annually, entertainment £800 monthly — summing to a figure materially higher than £2,206 when all items are included. The pension switch report [P9] and [P13] confirm retirement age 60 and growth rates of 0%, 2.94%, 5.88%. Notes [P2] and [P3] record the client as working part-time and intending to be fully retired by 60.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report's cashflow model uses £2,206/month as the expenditure input, but the fact find detailed expenditure breakdown (essential + £800/month entertainment + £5,000/year holidays) totals approximately £3,710/month equivalent, which is materially higher than £2,206/month. The £2,206 figure in the cashflow model matches the fact find's 'Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure' of £2,206.67 [P4], but this appears inconsistent with the itemised expenditure in [P15] and [P16].
      - The report conflates the target retirement income (£2,200 net/month) with current expenditure modelled in the cashflow (£2,206/month). The two figures are used interchangeably but serve different analytical purposes: one is the income needed in retirement, the other is current expenditure as a planning proxy. Neither the report nor the cashflow explicitly reconciles these or confirms the £2,200 retirement income target is what the cashflow plan models as the required income at age 60.
      - The cashflow modelled expenditure of £2,206/month does not reconcile with the itemised fact find expenditure (essential items plus £800/month entertainment plus £5,000/year holidays), which yields approximately £3,293–£3,710/month. This discrepancy is present in the evidence but the report does not address it.
      - The report states the cashflow expenditure figure as £2,206 (rounded), whereas the fact find records £2,206.67 — a negligible rounding difference, not a material discrepancy.
    The core objective — £2,200 net/month retirement income at age 60 — is directly corroborated by the fact find [P1] and [P5], which record exactly this figure and target age. The retirement age of 60 is confirmed across multiple sources including the pension switch projections [P9],[P13],[P14]. The cashflow growth rate assumptions (4.5% Aviva Platform, 2%, 2.5%, etc.) are internal to the report and the cashflow documents; the 4.5% figure is corroborated by [P2] which notes the client already gets this on Moneybox ISAs.
    
    However, a material concern arises from the income/expenditure conflation. The report and cashflow treat £2,206/month (current expenditure from the fact find summary [P4]) as equivalent to the £2,200/month retirement income target. The fact find's summary expenditure figure of £2,206.67 is arithmetically consistent with the cashflow model input, but it conflicts with the itemised expenditure in [P15] and [P16] which includes £800/month entertainment and £5,000/year holidays on top of essential items — totalling materially more than £2,206/month. The cashflow model therefore appears to use a lower expenditure figure than what the itemised fact find records, without explanation.
    
    Furthermore, using current expenditure as a proxy for retirement income target creates a planning risk: the report does not explicitly confirm whether the £2,200 income target is intended to cover the same expenditure pattern as today, or a reduced pattern in retirement. The internal inconsistency noted in the extraction (£2,200 objective vs £2,206 cashflow input vs £3,710 detailed expenditure) is a data quality concern that is not resolved in the report.
    
    The false-positive guard regarding DB pension income is relevant: the client receives a DB pension (Aon Final Salary, £1,700 net/month per [P15]). The £2,200 target may represent total required income including the DB pension, with only the gap needing to be funded from the DC pension and investments. The report does not explicitly clarify whether the £2,200 target is gross of or net of the existing DB income. If the DB pension alone provides £1,700/month net, the shortfall to be planned for is only £500/month — but the cashflow modelling at £2,206/month current expenditure does not make this decomposition clear. This is an unsupported assumption in the planning.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability drivers are identified in the evidence that materially affect this specific income/time-horizon check.
    
    Overall: the retirement income target of £2,200 net/month at age 60 is evidenced and consistent. The concern is that (a) the cashflow model input of £2,206 conflicts with the higher itemised expenditure figure, (b) the report conflates expenditure with income target without reconciliation, and (c) the interaction between the DB pension income (£1,700/month net) and the £2,200 target is not made explicit in the planning.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.6, G3.7, G3.9, G3.10, G3.11). The canonical model records the CFL rating as 'Unspecified', yet [P11] states 'Medium-High' — the canonical extraction failed to capture the label used in the report itself; this is an internal extraction inconsistency but the report does assert 'Medium-High'. The risk profile report's Capacity for Loss questionnaire section contains no additional notes ([P1]: 'No additional notes were recorded') — the file holds no CFL questionnaire responses or narrative rationale beyond what the suitability report itself states. No CFL rating figure or label appears in any supporting document (risk profile report, fact find, cashflow outputs) — the 'Medium-High' label exists only in the suitability report. 3 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report assigns a CFL of 'Medium-High' (the canonical model records the rating as 'Unspecified' but the passage [P11] confirms the label used is 'Medium-High'). The assessment basis is: (1) the client has a good level of guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions, meaning any income shortfall will only arise for a small period while waiting for Yvette's State Pension; (2) the client has significant funds providing resilience to investment volatility; and (3) cashflow analysis was used to assess whether sufficient funds will be available for retirement. The report states this is separately assessed from ATR and is supported by client-specific financial reasoning.
    File:     The risk profile report [P13] records the agreed risk level as 4 (Cautious Balanced) and notes no additional information in the Capacity for Loss section ([P1]: 'No additional notes were recorded'). The capacity for loss questionnaire section in the risk profile document is blank. The cashflow documents [P3]–[P10], [P14]–[P15] show that multiple scenarios were modelled (current situation, higher inflation, market crash, Tony predeceases, Yvette predeceases) and confirm cashflow analysis was performed. The current situation cashflow [P14] shows liquid assets and accessible pensions remaining positive throughout the projection, with shortfalls appearing from 2030–2032 (ages 60–62) before Yvette's state pension commences in 2033, corroborating the report's statement about a small shortfall period. No quantified CFL rating, no numerical loss tolerance figure, and no narrative notes are recorded in the risk profile document's CFL section.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model records the CFL rating as 'Unspecified', yet [P11] states 'Medium-High' — the canonical extraction failed to capture the label used in the report itself; this is an internal extraction inconsistency but the report does assert 'Medium-High'.
      - The risk profile report's Capacity for Loss questionnaire section contains no additional notes ([P1]: 'No additional notes were recorded') — the file holds no CFL questionnaire responses or narrative rationale beyond what the suitability report itself states.
      - No CFL rating figure or label appears in any supporting document (risk profile report, fact find, cashflow outputs) — the 'Medium-High' label exists only in the suitability report.
      - The cashflow documents confirm income shortfalls in 2030–2032, consistent with the report's 'small period' assertion, but the cashflow outputs do not explicitly translate this into a CFL rating or confirm that the CFL was assessed as Medium-High on this basis.
      - No quantified loss tolerance (e.g. maximum percentage or monetary loss acceptable) is evidenced anywhere in the file.
    The report's CFL assessment contains genuine client-specific financial reasoning (guaranteed income, significant assets, cashflow analysis) and is not simply a restatement of the ATR — so the 'moderate CFL as restatement of ATR' guard does not apply here. The cashflow files corroborate the existence of cashflow modelling and the income shortfall pattern described in the report (shortfalls in 2030–2032 before Yvette's state pension, consistent with [P14]). However, the critical gap is that the 'Medium-High' CFL label appears only in the suitability report itself. The risk profile document's CFL section is blank ([P1]), and no supporting document independently assigns, confirms or derives the 'Medium-High' rating. The report cannot corroborate itself. The cashflow analysis supports the descriptive reasoning but does not corroborate the specific rating label 'Medium-High'. The absence of any CFL rating or notes in the risk profile document means the rating is asserted with no independent evidential support on file. Under the decision logic, a CFL rating asserted with no assessment on file is a Potential Concern. Severity is Moderate because the assessment does contain client-specific financial reasoning (not a pure ATR restatement) and cashflow analysis is evidenced, but the rating label itself is unsupported. No vulnerability indicators in the evidence materially affect this assessment.

  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states that tolerance for loss was discussed with the client following agreement of attitude to risk, and that the client confirmed the potential impact on losses is within an acceptable tolerance based on their capacity to suffer losses. No quantified maximum loss percentage or monetary amount is stated. Capacity for loss is described narratively as 'Medium-High', supported by reference to guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions and significant liquid funds. No explicit need-level risk figure is extracted from the report.
    File:     The risk profile report [P12] and [P22] confirm a natural and agreed risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) on a 1–10 scale, derived from a psychometric ATR questionnaire completed 09/08/2025. The capacity for loss questionnaire responses are recorded in [P17] and [P23], but [P23] records 'No additional notes were recorded' following the CFL discussion. The research reports [P6], [P7], [P14] confirm the agreed risk level of 4 for the pension recommendation, with fund risk mapping also at 4. Cashflow modelling [P13], [P15], [P16], [P21] shows stress-tested projections including a 30% market crash scenario and high-inflation scenario, with no shortfall projected to age 100. The file contains no quantified tolerance-for-loss figure (e.g. maximum acceptable percentage or monetary loss). The need-level risk (/objectives[]/needLevelRisk) is absent from both the report and the extracted model.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts tolerance for loss was confirmed by the client and is within acceptable tolerance, but the capacity for loss questionnaire file record [P23] contains no additional notes, leaving the confirmation unsupported beyond the report's own assertion.
      - No quantified tolerance-for-loss figure (percentage or monetary amount) appears in either the report or the supporting file documents, despite cashflow modelling including a 30% crash stress test — the report does not link the stress-test outcome to a stated tolerance level.
      - Need-level risk is absent from both the report and the evidence; it cannot be confirmed that the strategy risk was matched against any required return or need-level risk figure.
      - The report describes capacity for loss as 'Medium-High' on a narrative basis; the CFL questionnaire responses in [P17] are on file but no scoring or banding outcome from the questionnaire is recorded in [P23], so the 'Medium-High' label cannot be traced back to the questionnaire output.
    The report's tolerance-for-loss treatment is narrative only. It asserts client confirmation of acceptable tolerance but provides no quantification. The capacity-for-loss questionnaire is on file [P17] with responses recorded, but the notes section [P23] records nothing further, meaning the 'Medium-High' CFL label in the report is self-asserted and cannot be corroborated by the questionnaire output. The guard acknowledges that many firms do not quantify tolerance separately and that absence is a documentation gap rather than a contradiction. However, the report goes further than merely omitting a figure — it positively asserts client confirmation of acceptable tolerance and assigns a 'Medium-High' CFL label, both of which require file support that is absent. The cashflow modelling does provide stress testing (30% crash, high inflation) and shows no shortfall to age 100 [P21], which offers indirect support for the capacity-for-loss conclusion, but the report does not explicitly link the stress-test outputs to a stated tolerance threshold. Need-level risk is entirely absent from both the report and the evidence, so no comparison of strategy risk against required return can be made. The agreed risk level of 4 is well evidenced [P12], [P22], [P14], [P6], [P7], and fund risk mappings at 4 are confirmed [P7], so the ATR-to-strategy alignment is sound. The concern here is specifically the unsupported 'Medium-High' CFL label and the absence of any quantified tolerance for loss, particularly given that a 30% crash scenario is modelled but not anchored to a stated client tolerance. No vulnerability factors appear in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment. The finding is a documentation gap rather than a contradiction, landing at Moderate severity.

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report records an investment term of 5 years (/riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears = 5). The objectives carry two time horizons: LongTerm and ShortTerm. The agreed risk level across all three new recommendations (pension, ISA, GIA) is 4 (Cautious Balanced).
    File:     The risk profile document [P1] records the natural risk level as 4 (Cautious Balanced) following a questionnaire completed 09/08/2025, and describes the process of agreeing a risk level by reference to investment amount, target and desired time horizon. The Defaqto research reports for the pension ([P10], [P13]), ISA ([P2]) and GIA ([P9]) all confirm agreed risk level 4. The pension review report [P13] records '5' as the investment term in the context of the recommendation (corroborated by the figure-match against [P11]). The cashflow models [P4], [P7], [P8] extend projections to age 102–107, implying a planning horizon of several decades. The Aviva Key Features [P14] describes pension investments as 'medium to long term (typically more than five years)'. No research report or risk profile document explicitly states a 5-year investment term for any of the three goals, nor does any document explain how a 5-year term was used to set or support a risk level of 4. The risk profile document [P3] records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both 'Investment target' and 'Agreed risk level' sections, meaning there is no documented rationale linking the 5-year term to the agreed risk profile.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report records an investment term of 5 years but also records both LongTerm and ShortTerm objective horizons — these are not reconciled. It is not explained which goal carries which horizon or how each term feeds into the agreed risk level.
      - The cashflow modelling extends to the client's age 102–107, which is inconsistent with a 5-year investment term being the operative horizon for all goals; no explanation is given for the difference.
      - The risk profile notes sections record 'No additional notes were recorded' for investment target and agreed risk level, meaning no documented link between the 5-year term and the risk level of 4 exists on file.
      - The report asserts a single investment term of 5 years yet recommends products across three wrappers (pension, ISA, GIA) with materially different liquidity profiles and planning horizons; no per-goal term breakdown is evidenced.
      - The pension recommendation involves a retirement-income objective with cashflow modelling to age 100+, yet no explanation is given that money remaining invested through retirement carries a longer horizon than 5 years, as the guard requires.
    The report states a 5-year investment term and simultaneously records both LongTerm and ShortTerm objective horizons without reconciling them or attributing each to a specific goal. The agreed risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) is consistently evidenced across all three research reports [P1], [P2], [P9], [P10], [P13]. The figure '5' for the investment term is corroborated by [P11]. However, the critical link — that the 5-year term was used to arrive at, or is consistent with, the agreed risk level of 4 — is absent. The risk profile document records 'No additional notes' for both the investment target and agreed risk level discussions [P3], so there is no documented rationale on file. The cashflow models run to age 102–107, which is far beyond 5 years; the guard permits a longer horizon where money remains invested through retirement, but requires the report to say so — it does not. The ISA and GIA are described with a ShortTerm horizon in the objectives, yet the pension carries a LongTerm horizon; neither is mapped explicitly to the 5-year figure. The absence of any per-goal term breakdown, combined with the lack of documented rationale linking the stated term to the risk level, means the time horizon is asserted but not evidenced as supporting the risk profile. This is a missing-evidence concern: the claim that the 5-year term supports risk level 4 is unsubstantiated.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The client's agreed risk profile is 4 (Cautious Balanced) on a 1-10 scale. The recommended investment strategy targets risk rating 4. Capacity for loss is described as 'Medium-High', supported by guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions and significant liquid assets. The existing pension is noted as having a risk level of 6, which does not match the agreed profile. The recommended funds (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II for the pension, CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc for ISA and GIA) are stated to be aligned to risk profile 4. The suitability report states the capacity for loss is Medium-High based on guaranteed income and resilience to volatility, and that cashflow analysis was used to assess sufficiency for retirement.
    File:     The risk profile report (D) confirms the client's natural ATR questionnaire result on 09/08/2025 gives a risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced). All three Defaqto research reports (G) confirm the agreed risk level is 4. The pension switch research report confirms the new portfolio risk mapping is 4, matching the agreed level. The GIA research report confirms the CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc is mapped at risk level 4. The CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund KIID (H) shows a Risk and Reward Indicator of 4 with medium volatility, consistent with a risk-4 profile. The pension review report (G) explicitly states: 'The client's agreed risk level is 4. The 10 year future volatility for the existing holdings based on the current asset allocation is 12.34. This volatility maps to a risk level of 6 which is outside the client's agreed risk level.' This confirms the existing holdings are outside the agreed profile, which the suitability report acknowledges. The capacity for loss section in the suitability report (I) describes it as 'Medium-High' but the rationale largely restates income adequacy (guaranteed income, small shortfall period) without a distinct quantified loss tolerance separate from ATR. The risk profile report (D) records no additional notes for capacity for loss discussion. No quantified tolerance for loss (e.g. maximum acceptable percentage drawdown) is documented in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The existing pension risk level is 6 on the same 1-10 scale used for the client's agreed ATR of 4 — a difference of 2 risk levels. The report acknowledges this mismatch but the recommended action (switch to risk-4 fund) addresses it going forward.
      - Capacity for loss is rated 'Medium-High' in the suitability report but no separate quantified loss tolerance is evidenced in the risk profile report (D), which records 'No additional notes were recorded' for the capacity for loss discussion. The CFL rationale in the suitability report largely mirrors the ATR rationale (cautious, guaranteed income), raising a concern that CFL has been conflated with ATR rather than assessed distinctly.
      - The risk profile report (D) records no additional notes for capacity for loss, meaning the 'Medium-High' CFL rating in the suitability report is asserted without a documented evidential basis in the supporting file.
      - No quantified tolerance for loss scenario (e.g. maximum acceptable percentage or monetary loss) is documented anywhere in the file.
    The core strategy alignment question is answered: the recommended funds are all mapped at risk level 4, matching the agreed ATR of 4 on the same 1-10 scale. The pension switch research report explicitly confirms the new portfolio risk mapping is 4, matching the agreed risk level. The CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund KIID independently confirms a risk-reward indicator of 4. The existing pension's risk level of 6 is noted in both the suitability report and the review report as being outside the agreed profile — this is the driver for the switch recommendation, not a flaw in the recommended strategy itself. The recommended strategy does not exceed the agreed risk profile.
    
    However, there is a material concern with the capacity for loss assessment. The suitability report describes CFL as 'Medium-High' and justifies this by reference to guaranteed income and low shortfall period — factors that are also used to justify the ATR discussion. The risk profile report, which is the primary supporting document for the ATR/CFL assessment, records 'No additional notes were recorded' for the capacity for loss section. This means the 'Medium-High' CFL rating is asserted in the suitability report without any documented basis in the supporting evidence. This is consistent with the severity guidance: 'Moderate: capacity for loss asserted as a restatement of attitude to risk.' The CFL appears to be derived from income adequacy considerations (which is a legitimate input) but there is no evidence of a distinct CFL assessment process, no quantified loss tolerance, and the risk profile tool records nothing for CFL. The report's assertion of 'Medium-High' CFL is therefore unsupported by independent evidence in the file.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability indicators are identified in the file that would materially alter this assessment.
    
    In summary: the recommended strategy risk rating matches the agreed ATR (4=4), and the existing mismatch is acknowledged and addressed. The concern is confined to the CFL assessment being unsupported and potentially conflated with ATR, without quantified loss tolerance or documented CFL discussion.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   ATR assessed and agreed at 4 on a 1-10 scale ('Cautious Balanced'), assessed via risk profiling questionnaire on 9 August 2025. Capacity for loss assessed separately as Medium-High, based on guaranteed income from State and Private Pensions, significant liquid assets, and cashflow analysis. Investment term 5 years. Recommended funds: Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II (pension, 100%, risk rating 4, OCF 0.41%) and CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc (ISA and GIA, 100%, risk rating 4, OCF 0.29%), both on a 1-10 scale, both rated 4 to match the agreed ATR. Strategy described as CIP, blend of active and passive, benchmark ABI Mixed Investment 20-60% Shares. Rationale states funds reflect the risk profile and are managed to remain within risk tolerance.
    File:     [P17] risk profile report dated 11 August 2025 confirms Mr Tony Barnes' natural risk level is 4 Cautious Balanced on the Defaqto 1-10 scale. [P4] personal pension plan research report confirms agreed risk level 4 and states 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' [P6] GIA research report confirms agreed risk level 4 Cautious Balanced following interactive discussion considering ATR, CFL and required return. [P13] and [P14] personal pension plan research reports confirm agreed risk level 4 with Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II at 100%. [P1] CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc KIID rates the fund 4 on the SRRI risk-and-reward indicator and notes 'this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years.' [P5] Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II factsheet gives risk rating on Aviva's own 1-7 volatility scale; the factsheet does not assign a rating of 4 on the Defaqto 1-10 scale independently, but [P4] confirms the portfolio mapping is 4 on the Defaqto scale. [P18] suitability report itself states existing pension funds carry combined risk level 6, higher than agreed profile 4, justifying the switch. No separate documentary evidence of the CFL assessment methodology or cashflow model outputs is in the retrieved passages beyond the report's own assertions.
    Discrepancies:
      - The CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc KIID [P1] states 'this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years', yet the report states the investment term is 5 years — this creates a potential minimum holding period alignment concern for the ISA and GIA investments.
      - The Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II factsheet [P5] uses Aviva's own 1-7 volatility scale, which is a different scale from the Defaqto 1-10 profiler scale used to set the client's ATR of 4. The report presents both as '4' without distinguishing the scales, though the false-positive guard notes SRRI/risk-reward indicators run on their own scale.
      - Capacity for loss is assessed as 'Medium-High' but the report does not quantify tolerance for loss (e.g. maximum acceptable percentage fall) or model a large-loss scenario to underpin the Medium-High rating; the assessment is primarily a narrative restatement of the client's guaranteed income and asset position.
      - No cashflow model outputs or supporting document evidencing the CFL analysis are present in the retrieved passages — the cashflow analysis is asserted but not corroborated by any file evidence.
      - The report's CFL basis references guaranteed income covering a 'small period of time' shortfall, but no quantified figure is given for the income shortfall amount, its duration, or the stress-tested investment loss scenario that would breach capacity for loss.
    ATR: The risk profiling questionnaire result of 4 on the Defaqto 1-10 scale is well-evidenced. [P17] confirms Mr Barnes' natural risk level as 4 Cautious Balanced dated 11 August 2025. Multiple Defaqto research reports ([P4], [P6], [P13], [P14]) confirm the agreed risk level as 4. Both recommended funds carry a Defaqto risk mapping of 4, matching the agreed ATR. No override is present; the assessed and agreed codes are the same. This element is supported.
    
    Fund risk ratings on the Defaqto scale: [P4] explicitly states 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 4 which matches the client's agreed risk level' for the Aviva fund. The SRRI indicator on the Aviva factsheet [P5] and the CT KIID [P1] both use their own separate scales (Aviva 1-7 volatility; SRRI 1-7). The false-positive guard confirms these are not directly comparable to the Defaqto 1-10 scale. Both recommended funds are on the Defaqto platform and the Defaqto research confirms the portfolio mapping of 4. No incompatible scale comparison problem arises on the primary Defaqto mapping. The SRRI/volatility ratings are corroborative context, not a value match.
    
    Investment term vs minimum holding period: The CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc KIID [P1] states the fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years.' The report states the investment term is 5 years. This is a boundary-case mismatch — the KIID caution applies to investors planning to withdraw within 5 years, and the client's stated term is exactly 5 years. The report does not acknowledge or address this tension. The false-positive guard states a minimum holding period longer than the client's term is a genuine mismatch; a shorter one is not. Here the minimum holding period equals the client's term, which sits at the boundary. The KIID language ('within 5 years') suggests withdrawals at the end of 5 years may be acceptable; however, a client whose ISA and GIA investment term is stated as exactly 5 years and who may need to access funds at or around that point is at the margin of the KIID's own caution. The report does not address this at all, which is a gap in the justification for the CT fund selection.
    
    Capacity for loss: The report separately assesses CFL as Medium-High and provides narrative justification (guaranteed income, significant assets, cashflow analysis). The isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr flag is true and the basis is not merely a restatement of ATR. However, the CFL is not quantified — no maximum loss tolerance percentage is stated, no stress scenario is presented, and no cashflow model output appears in the file evidence. The severity guidance notes 'Moderate: capacity for loss asserted as a restatement of ATR' — that specific concern does not apply here as CFL is separately argued. However, the absence of any corroborating file evidence for the cashflow analysis (asserted in the report but no outputs retrieved) means the claim that cashflow analysis was used is unsupported by the file, which is a missing evidence concern. The CFL rating of Medium-High being higher than the ATR of 4 (Cautious Balanced) could be seen as permissive — it supports rather than constrains the strategy — but the lack of quantified stress-testing or evidenced cashflow outputs means the CFL assessment is not fully corroborated.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability flags have been identified in the evidence that would materially affect this check.
    
    Overall: The ATR and fund risk mapping are well-evidenced and aligned. The primary concern is: (1) the CT fund KIID's 5-year minimum holding period caution is not addressed by the report given the 5-year investment term; and (2) the cashflow analysis underpinning the CFL is asserted but not evidenced in the file. These are material gaps in justification and evidence, warranting a Potential Concern.

  [G3.11] Funds inside a retained arrangement align with the agreed risk rating
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The existing pension (EA1) is subject to a RetainAndSwitchFunds advice action. Switching to the recommended fund will ensure your investments match your risk tolerance.' The action is RetainAndSwitchFunds — the plan wrapper is retained but the funds inside it are being switched to the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II, which the research report maps to risk level 4.

The existing Vanguard funds are not rated in any retrieved passage on the same scale; the existing risk level of 6 appears only in the suitability report itself. The fund-switch is the advised action, so the existing holdings (at risk level 6) will be replaced by the new fund (at risk level 4). There is no independent documentary evidence in the file confirming the existing Vanguard funds carry a risk level of 6 on the adviser's 1-10 scale.
    Discrepancies:
      - The existing fund risk level of 6 is stated only in the suitability report ([I] category); no retrieved passage from a D, G or H category document independently confirms that Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% carry a combined risk level of 6 on the adviser's 1-10 scale.
      - The existing fund risk level (6) exceeds the client's agreed profile (4) — the report acknowledges this mismatch explicitly as the reason for the fund switch.
      - The extraction noted an internal inconsistency: the report uses '6' as a blended overall risk level in the Existing Arrangements table but elsewhere refers to 'a risk profile of 5 and the other to a risk profile of 7' for the individual Vanguard funds — these two characterisations are not reconciled in the report.
    The advice action is RetainAndSwitchFunds: the pension wrapper is kept but the funds are replaced. The requirement asks whether the funds inside the retained arrangement align with the agreed risk rating. In this case, the report itself confirms they do not — the existing funds sit at risk level 6 against an agreed profile of 4 — and the switch is expressly motivated by that misalignment. The new fund (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II) is independently confirmed at risk level 4 by [P10], matching the agreed profile. So the post-switch position will be aligned. However, there are two residual concerns. First, the risk level of 6 assigned to the existing Vanguard holdings appears only in the suitability report itself (an [I] passage); no independent D, G or H document in the file confirms those funds carry risk level 6 on the adviser's 1-10 scale. The guards confirm that where the report asserts alignment (or here, misalignment and a remedy), that assertion is testable and the absence of corroborating evidence for the risk level attributed to the existing funds is a gap. The assigned level of 6 may well be correct, but it is unsupported by any retrieved evidence. Second, the internal inconsistency between the blended '6' and the individual fund ratings of '5' and '7' is unreconciled in the report — the client is invested 64.95% in the level-7 fund and 35.02% in the level-5 fund, and neither characterisation (6 overall, or 5 and 7 individually) is supported by an independent source on the adviser's scale. The new recommended fund is confirmed at risk level 4 by [P10] and therefore the forward position is supported. The concern is confined to the unsubstantiated characterisation of the existing fund risk levels and the internal inconsistency in how they are described, not to the post-switch outcome.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). The report records the client's knowledge level as 'Limited' (a single label), whereas the questionnaire responses are granular and show 'No understanding / knowledge' against stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and unit trusts/GIAs — which are below even 'Limited' — and only 'A fair degree' for cash/savings products. The single label 'Limited' may overstate knowledge for investment products specifically. The risk profile narrative describes the client as having 'limited or moderate experience', whereas the report uses only 'Limited' — the 'or moderate' qualifier from the risk profile tool is not reflected in the report's single-label characterisation. The questionnaire records 'Very little understanding / knowledge' for pension planning and investing generally, which is a lower grade than 'Limited' as used by the report. 1 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report records the client's knowledge level as 'Limited'.
    File:     The risk profile questionnaire (category D) records the client as having 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, 'No understanding / knowledge' of EIS/VCTs, 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and income in retirement, 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of investing generally, 'No understanding / knowledge' of Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts, and 'A fair degree of understanding / knowledge' of cash/savings/deposit accounts. The risk profile narrative (category D) states 'If you have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand then you may be suited to a Cautious Balanced approach to investing.' The suitability report itself (category I, P22) states 'You have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The report records the client's knowledge level as 'Limited' (a single label), whereas the questionnaire responses are granular and show 'No understanding / knowledge' against stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and unit trusts/GIAs — which are below even 'Limited' — and only 'A fair degree' for cash/savings products. The single label 'Limited' may overstate knowledge for investment products specifically.
      - The risk profile narrative describes the client as having 'limited or moderate experience', whereas the report uses only 'Limited' — the 'or moderate' qualifier from the risk profile tool is not reflected in the report's single-label characterisation.
      - The questionnaire records 'Very little understanding / knowledge' for pension planning and investing generally, which is a lower grade than 'Limited' as used by the report.
    The report labels the client's knowledge as 'Limited'. The risk profile questionnaire (category D, [P1] and [P12]) provides the most granular evidence available. Against direct knowledge questions, the client scored 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and unit trusts/GIAs — the asset classes directly relevant to the recommended ISA and GIA products — and 'Very little understanding / knowledge' for pensions and investing generally. Only for cash/savings products did the client score positively ('A fair degree'). The risk profile narrative in [P2] and [P4] uses the phrase 'limited or moderate experience', and P22 (category I, suitability report) repeats this, but neither is evidence independent of the report. The questionnaire responses are the objective evidence. On that evidence, 'Limited' as a single label is arguably generous for investment products: 'No understanding' and 'Very little understanding' sit below what 'Limited' typically connotes, and the recommended products (stocks and shares ISA, GIA, and pension investing in multi-asset funds) are precisely the areas where the client recorded the lowest knowledge scores. The 'Cautious Balanced' risk profile description acknowledges limited or moderate experience and difficulty with financial matters, which is broadly consistent with a 'Limited' characterisation when taken holistically. However, the nuance that the client has no understanding of unit trusts and GIAs — the wrapper types recommended — is not captured. No vulnerability factors appear in the file that bear directly on this requirement. The first guard notes a real mismatch only where the report describes the client as 'comfortable' while the file records 'limited'; here the report says 'Limited', which is directionally aligned with the questionnaire findings but potentially overstates knowledge for the specific product types recommended. The second and third guards remind that self-assessed comfort differs from assessed knowledge. The questionnaire answers are assessed responses. The mismatch between 'No understanding' on the relevant product types and the report's single 'Limited' label is a genuine, if moderate, discrepancy — the report does not overstate knowledge dramatically, but it does smooth over the fact that for the exact product categories recommended, the client recorded the lowest possible knowledge score. This is a Potential Concern of moderate severity rather than high, because 'Limited' is in the right direction and the products recommended (risk-rated multi-asset funds at level 4 on a cautious profile) are not highly complex, but the report's characterisation is insufficiently precise given the questionnaire evidence.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states the client has invested in current accounts, savings, Cash ISAs, and stocks and shares within the last 5 years (by himself), and in pension planning through a financial adviser. It also states the client has no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes — creating an internal tension where 'stocks and shares' experience is both claimed as held and disclaimed as understood. The report records the client as having knowledge consistent with a Cautious Balanced risk profile and frames the recommended products (Aviva Personal Pension with Multi-Asset Plus Fund II, Aviva Platform ISA and GIA with CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund) as consistent with that experience level.
    File:     The risk profile questionnaire [P1] records: Current Accounts/Savings/Cash ISA — 'Yes, by myself'; Stocks and shares invested within last 5 years — 'Yes, by myself'; Pension planning products within last 5 years — 'Yes, through a financial adviser'; Unit Trusts/GIA — 'No'; Enterprise Investment Schemes — 'No'. For understanding, the questionnaire records: stocks and shares — 'No understanding / knowledge'; EIS/VCTs — 'No understanding / knowledge'; pension planning and income in retirement — 'Very little understanding / knowledge'. The risk profile narrative [P7, P9] describes the client as a Cautious Balanced investor who 'may be suited to this approach if you have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand'. The fact find [P8] records the client's retirement and investment objectives. No additional evidence of comprehension of the recommended products' unusual features (multi-asset fund with risk-level 4 wrapper, GIA tax considerations) is present in the pack beyond the risk profile narrative.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report asserts the client has held 'stocks and shares' (productExperience hasHeld: true) while simultaneously stating the client has 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes' — the report both claims experience of stocks and shares and disclaims any understanding of them in the same section. The risk profile [P1] corroborates the 'no understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, but the 'invested by myself' response also corroborates prior ownership. These two facts coexist in the file but the report conflates them ambiguously.
      - The suitability report asserts the client has invested in pension planning 'through a financial adviser'. The risk profile [P1] corroborates this ('Yes, through a financial adviser'). However, the risk profile also records the client's understanding of pension planning as 'Very little understanding / knowledge' [P1]. The suitability report does not reflect this qualification — it does not record or acknowledge that the client's self-assessed understanding of pensions is very limited, despite recommending a pension switch as the primary transaction.
      - The fact find [P3] records a Personal Pension at Aviva Platform with policy start date 09/03/2026, which post-dates the advice date (06/08/2025), raising a data quality question about the existing arrangement entry — though this does not directly bear on the knowledge/experience assertion.
      - The risk profile narrative [P7, P9] explicitly states the client 'may be suited to a Cautious Balanced approach if you have limited or moderate experience of investments and do not find financial matters particularly easy to understand', consistent with limited knowledge — but the suitability report does not translate this caveat into its knowledge and experience narrative, which presents the client more positively than the underlying questionnaire data warrants.
    The core finding is a material omission rather than an outright contradiction. The risk profile questionnaire [P1] records the client's self-assessed understanding of pension planning as 'Very little understanding / knowledge'. This is the directly relevant data point given the primary recommendation is a pension switch. The suitability report acknowledges the client has 'invested in pension planning through a financial adviser' (corroborated by [P1]) but does not record or discuss that the client's understanding of how pension planning works is very limited. The report's knowledge and experience section therefore overstates — or at least omits the qualification on — the client's comprehension of the recommended product type.
    
    The stocks-and-shares tension is less critical: prior ownership by the client is corroborated ('Yes, by myself' in [P1]) and the disclaimer of understanding is also corroborated ('No understanding / knowledge' in [P1]). The report is internally inconsistent on this point but the file supports both underlying facts. What the report fails to do is reconcile them — the 'stocks and shares' experience entry conveys a higher level of engagement than the questionnaire answer warrants.
    
    The guard 'Plans held do not prove understanding — do not treat plan ownership as evidence of knowledge' reinforces the concern: the fact that the client held a pension through an adviser does not evidence comprehension of how the recommended switch product works. With 'very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning on the questionnaire, and no record of explanations given, questions asked, or comprehension verified in the file, the recommendation of a pension switch (the primary and most complex transaction) to a client of limited pension understanding is inadequately evidenced. No vulnerability flags are recorded in the file that would materially change this analysis beyond what is already captured by the limited-knowledge finding.
    
    The product complexity (multi-asset pension, GIA with CT Universal MAP Cautious, ISA transfer) is broadly proportionate to a Cautious Balanced profile, and the recommended funds are not exotic. However, the absence of any record of the adviser explaining the pension mechanics, the switch implications, or the GIA tax consequences to a client who self-reports very little pension understanding and no understanding of stocks and shares, is a moderate concern. This sits at the 'moderate' severity level per the guidance: no record of client questions or explanations where the product has unusual features.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report classifies the Aviva Platform Personal Pension (SIPP) as Complex, citing five complexity drivers: SIPP structure, flexi-access drawdown availability, tax treatment dependent on contribution levels and individual circumstances, annual allowance and MPAA considerations, and IHT legislative changes from April 2027. The Aviva Platform ISA and GIA are both classified as NonComplex with no complexity drivers listed. The client (Mr Anthony Barnes) is described as having existing pension holdings (the Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd pension being switched), prior investment experience evidenced by existing Vanguard LifeStrategy funds within the pension at risk levels 5 and 7, and an agreed risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) for the new recommendations. The report recommends the SIPP into the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II and both the ISA and GIA into the CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc.
    File:     The case file contains: (1) Defaqto research reports for the Personal Pension, GIA and ISA, all recording an agreed risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced), with the pension research report dated 03-12-2025 and a pension switching research report dated 16-12-2025. (2) Key Features documents for the Aviva Pension, ISA and GIA, confirming these are standard platform-based products. The pension KFD confirms flexi-access drawdown is available. The GIA KFD describes a standard investment portfolio. The ISA KFD describes a standard stocks and shares ISA. (3) The Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II factsheet confirms risk warnings including General (A), Foreign Exchange Risk (B), Emerging Markets (C), Fixed Interest (E) and Derivatives (F), and a Aviva risk/return rating on a 1–7 scale. (4) The pension switching research report confirms the recommended product is 'Pension Portfolio - Choice' from Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited on the Aviva Platform. (5) No fact-find or client knowledge/experience questionnaire is present in the retrieved passages. No document in the file explicitly records the client's stated level of investment knowledge, graded understanding, or any record of client questions or explanations given about the SIPP's complex features (flexi-access drawdown, MPAA, IHT changes).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client has sufficient knowledge and experience to be recommended a Complex product (SIPP with flexi-access drawdown, MPAA and IHT considerations), but no fact-find, knowledge questionnaire or equivalent graded record of the client's investment knowledge appears in the retrieved evidence to corroborate this.
      - No evidence in the file records that the client was given specific explanations of, or demonstrated understanding of, the SIPP's complexity drivers (flexi-access drawdown mechanics, MPAA, IHT legislative changes from April 2027), which the report itself flags as complexity drivers for a product it classifies as Complex.
      - The report classifies the ISA and GIA as NonComplex with no complexity drivers — this is consistent with the KFDs and research reports for those products, so no discrepancy on those two wrappers.
    The SIPP is classified as Complex by the report, which is appropriate given the five complexity drivers stated (flexi-access drawdown, MPAA, annual allowance, IHT changes, tax treatment). The KFDs and product literature on file confirm these features exist in the recommended product. The ISA and GIA classifications as NonComplex are consistent with the KFDs and research reports for those products — no issue arises there. However, for a product the report itself classifies as Complex, the decision logic requires that comprehension be corroborated in category C (supporting documents / fact-find). No fact-find, knowledge questionnaire or equivalent record appears in the retrieved passages. The report does not contain a graded record of the client's investment knowledge level, nor any record of client questions or explanations given about the specific complexity drivers. The existing Vanguard LifeStrategy holdings within the pension do evidence some prior investment experience, but these are [I]-category assertions from the report itself and cannot self-corroborate. The Defaqto research reports confirm an agreed risk level of 4 but do not address knowledge or comprehension of complex product features. The guard states that a mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds is not complex per se — however, the report itself has classified this as Complex and listed specific drivers including flexi-access drawdown and MPAA; the guard therefore does not dismiss the absence of comprehension evidence. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the retrieved passages as material to this check. The absence of a graded knowledge record and absence of any comprehension evidence for the Complex product amounts to a moderate concern: there is no record of client questions or explanations where the product has unusual features, consistent with the moderate severity band.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The client (Mr Tony Barnes) has a stated knowledge level of 'Limited'. Three products are recommended: one classified as 'Complex' (the pension) and two as 'NonComplex' (ISA and GIA). The suitability report states the client has 'very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing', a 'fair degree of understanding of cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs', but 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes'. The client has invested in cash-type products by himself, stocks and shares by himself, and pension planning through a financial adviser. The report describes the risk profile as 'Cautious Balanced' (level 4) and states the client does not find financial matters particularly easy to understand. The report asserts the recommended products are suitable for the client's knowledge and experience level.
    File:     The risk profile questionnaire [P16] confirms: no understanding/knowledge of stocks and shares; no understanding/knowledge of EIS/VCTs; very little understanding/knowledge of pension planning and income in retirement. The client has invested in current accounts, savings/deposit accounts and Cash ISAs by himself in the last 5 years; has invested in stocks and shares by himself in the last 5 years; has NOT invested in unit trusts or GIAs in the last 5 years; has invested in pension planning through a financial adviser. The risk profile report [P4][P7] describes the Cautious Balanced (level 4) profile as suited to someone with 'limited or moderate experience of investments' who does 'not find financial matters particularly easy to understand'. No category C record (record of client questions, explanations of complex product features, or confirmation of comprehension) is present in the pack for the pension, which is classified as Complex. The fact find [P21] records the ATRQ was not completed at the meeting — 'Client did not have time to complete an ATRQ today, so have sent him an email version to complete himself on Defaqto.' The ISA and GIA are invested in a multi-asset cautious fund (CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc) rated at risk level 4, consistent with the client's profile. No evidence in the file that the adviser explained the complex features of the pension product or that the client demonstrated comprehension of it.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report classifies the pension as 'Complex' and the client's knowledge level as 'Limited', yet provides no evidence of a comprehension check, explanation of complex features, or category C record to support suitability of the complex product for this client.
      - The risk profile questionnaire records 'very little understanding/knowledge' of pension planning and income in retirement [P16], yet the report does not address how a complex pension product is appropriate given this very limited specific knowledge of pensions.
      - The report states the client has invested in stocks and shares 'by himself' [P5], but the questionnaire [P16] simultaneously records 'No understanding/knowledge' of how stocks and shares work — these two items are in tension: prior experience of an asset class alongside stated no understanding of it.
      - No category C corroboration (record of client questions or adviser explanations regarding the complex pension product's features) is present in the pack, despite the pension being classified as Complex.
      - The ATRQ was not completed at the meeting and was sent to the client to complete himself [P21], which means the knowledge and experience record relied upon may not have been gathered under controlled, observed conditions — reducing its evidentiary weight.
    The central issue is the recommendation of a product classified as 'Complex' (the Aviva Personal Pension) to a client whose stated knowledge level is 'Limited' and who recorded 'very little understanding/knowledge' of pension planning specifically. Under the decision logic, this sits between the highest severity band (complex product to client with no/limited knowledge) and the moderate band (no record of client questions or explanations where the product has unusual features). Both apply here: the client has limited knowledge and there is no category C record. The report reproduces the risk profile description that itself conditions suitability on 'limited or moderate experience' [P4][P7], but the risk profile description is not evidence of comprehension of a complex product — the guard makes clear that reproducing the profile description does not establish knowledge. The assertion in [P5][P6] that the client's experience and knowledge have been assessed and that the recommendation is appropriate is an [I] passage and cannot corroborate itself. The tension between the client having invested in stocks and shares 'by himself' [P5] and recording 'no understanding/knowledge' of stocks and shares [P16] is a secondary concern — it suggests the knowledge record itself may be unreliable — but it does not resolve the primary gap. The GIA has not been invested in previously (questionnaire records 'No' to unit trusts and GIAs [P16]), which is relevant to the GIA being NonComplex but still novel to this client; however, the GIA and ISA use a cautious multi-asset fund at the client's agreed risk level [P18][P20], and these are rated NonComplex, so that dimension is lower concern. The pension, however, is Complex, and the file contains no record that the adviser explained how the product works, its features, or that the client demonstrated comprehension — the required category C corroboration is absent. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability assessment outcome is present in the pack (noted as absent in the extraction metadata), but the profile of limited financial understanding, the delegated ATRQ completion, and the complexity of pension planning for a recently retired client with little pension knowledge are characteristics that could heighten concern. This does not change the category of finding but reinforces it. On balance, a Potential Concern is warranted: a complex product has been recommended to a client with limited knowledge of the relevant product type, and there is no corroborating evidence of comprehension or explanation in the file.

  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client describes himself as having very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing; has a fair degree of understanding of cash accounts, savings accounts, and Cash ISAs but no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes; checks the valuation of his investment and retirement portfolio on an annual basis; and that an investor experience questionnaire was completed on 9 August 2025. The risk profile report is referenced and records that the client has 'limited or moderate experience of investments and does not find financial matters particularly easy to understand' with a risk profile of 4 on a scale of 1–10.
    File:     The fact find (P20) confirms a telephone meeting on 06/08/2025 covering retirement planning. The risk profile report (P8/P15/P21) confirms a risk profile of 4, noting the client is 'quite cautious' and has 'limited or moderate experience of investments' and 'does not find financial matters particularly easy to understand', but records no additional notes under capacity for loss, investment target or agreed risk level discussions. Adviser notes (P9, P10) record that the client 'has another £40k in high interest savings', is 'only ATR 4', and that the adviser flagged needing to assess earnings and pension contributions — but contain no record of the client asking questions about the recommended products or demonstrating comprehension of their features or risks. No category C comprehension evidence (client questions, explanations given, client feedback) appears in any retrieved passage. The recommended products include an Aviva Personal Pension (pension switch), an Aviva Platform ISA (stocks and shares ISA), and an Aviva Platform GIA — all invested in multi-asset funds (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II and CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc), which are complex relative to the client's stated understanding of cash accounts only. The fact find (P11) notes the client did not complete an ATRQ at the meeting and was sent an email version to complete independently. Search Q4.5.2, which targeted comprehension evidence, returned nothing.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the client has 'very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' and no understanding of unit trusts or stocks and shares, yet recommends multi-asset investment funds held within a stocks-and-shares ISA, a GIA and a pension — products and wrappers materially more complex than the cash products the client understands. No evidence of comprehension checks, client questions or explanations recorded in the file bridges this gap.
      - The investor experience questionnaire is referenced as completed on 9 August 2025, but no questionnaire document or its results appear in the evidence pack — its contents cannot be verified.
      - The fact find records that the client did not complete the ATRQ at the meeting and was sent an email version; no confirmation of completion or results appears in the file.
      - No category C comprehension passages were retrieved from any search, meaning there is no file-side evidence that the client demonstrated understanding of the recommended complex products.
    The report candidly acknowledges that the client has very little investment knowledge and no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares, or enterprise investment schemes. The risk profile report independently corroborates limited or moderate investment experience and difficulty understanding financial matters. These are not contradicted. However, the recommended solution places the client into multi-asset funds across three wrappers (pension, stocks and shares ISA, GIA), all of which are materially more complex than the cash products the client understands. This triggers the requirement for positive evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of these products. No such evidence exists in the pack: the investor experience questionnaire is referenced but absent, the ATRQ was not completed at the meeting, and search Q4.5.2 (targeting comprehension evidence) returned nothing. The absence of recorded questions is not conclusive proof of non-understanding (per the guard), but where a client is explicitly recorded as having no understanding of the relevant product types and the products recommended are complex, the guard requires the gap to be weighted heavily. The report asserts the client can proceed with these products but provides no file-side evidence of comprehension checks or explanation of features and risks beyond the standard product descriptions in the suitability report itself. Two [I]-category passages (P8, describing the risk profile outcome) partially corroborate the knowledge level but cannot corroborate the claim that the client understood the recommended products — the report cannot corroborate itself. No vulnerability overlay is triggered on the evidence available: no vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the file beyond the knowledge/experience limitations already assessed here, and CHK-010 covers vulnerability in its own right. The outcome is a Potential Concern: a complex product has been recommended to a client recorded as having no relevant product knowledge, and the file lacks the comprehension evidence required to support the recommendation.

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report states the client has invested in current accounts, savings accounts, Cash ISAs, and stocks and shares within the last 5 years, and has invested in pension planning through a financial adviser. The report also records that the client has no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares (in the sense of direct equities/EIS), or enterprise investment schemes. The existing pension arrangement is a SIPP (Aviva platform) holding Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity (35.02%), Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (64.95%), and Cash (0.03%).
    File:     The fact find (P2, P12) records an existing personal pension with Aviva Platform (policy AV2075926-001), confirming pension ownership. The transaction history (P9, P10, P11) shows repeated transactions in Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% Equity and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity dating back to at least January 2018, confirming long-standing holding of these funds within the pension. The client report (P3, P5) confirms the Aviva account AV2075926 with an opening value of £59,017.42 and closing value of £79,092.32 over the period 24/11/2022 to 23/11/2025. The policy research document (P13) confirms fund value of £79,092.32 for AV2075926. The fact find (P4) notes the client wants to 'Review his Aviva pension and look to improve upon it' and is interested in investment advice on cash proceeds, consistent with prior experience of savings and cash products. No direct corroboration of current accounts, savings accounts, or Cash ISA holdings appears in the retrieved passages as standalone account records, though the fact find narrative (P4) references £40,000 cash and intentions around Cash ISA. The fact find (P2) records no money purchase schemes under the dedicated section but separately records the Aviva personal pension — this appears to be a data-entry inconsistency in the fact find rather than a denial of the pension's existence. The pension has been set up and managed through an adviser (transactions from 2018 onwards on the Aviva platform), consistent with the report's statement that pension planning was conducted through a financial adviser.
    Discrepancies:
      - The fact find (P2) records 'Do you have any existing money purchase schemes? No' but separately records an existing personal pension with Aviva Platform — internally inconsistent within the fact find, though the pension's existence is confirmed elsewhere in the file.
      - The report asserts the client has held stocks and shares investments 'made by yourself' but the transaction history shows the pension (containing equity funds) was set up and managed through a financial adviser, not independently by the client — this slightly overstates the nature of self-directed equity experience.
      - No standalone documentary evidence of current accounts, savings accounts, or Cash ISA holdings appears in the retrieved passages to directly corroborate those assertions in the report, beyond the narrative reference in the fact find objectives section.
      - The report records 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares' in one breath but also asserts the client has held stocks and shares investments — this internal tension in the report (between experience and understanding) is noted, though the existing holdings are multi-asset funds rather than direct equities or unit trusts per se.
    The core assertion — that the client has pension experience — is well corroborated. The transaction history (P9, P10, P11) evidences holdings of Vanguard LifeStrategy funds within the Aviva pension from at least January 2018, and the client report (P3) and policy research (P13) confirm the current fund value and composition. The report's statement that pension planning was conducted through a financial adviser is consistent with the file: the pension was established on the Aviva platform and transactions appear to have been managed via the platform over several years. The guard that an arrangement set up by a previous adviser says little about the client's own experience is relevant here — the pension holdings corroborate ownership and some familiarity, but not independent investment decision-making. The report's assertion of 'stocks and shares investments made by yourself' is not clearly supported; the file shows equity exposure only through an adviser-managed pension using blended multi-asset funds, not self-directed stock picking. This slightly overstates the character of the experience, but does not rise to a material misstatement given the broader context. The internal tension in the report between 'no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares' and 'has invested in stocks and shares' is an internal inconsistency within the report itself. The fact find's 'No money purchase schemes' entry contradicts the simultaneously recorded Aviva personal pension — this appears to be a data-entry error rather than a genuine denial, since the pension is recorded in full detail immediately below. The absence of standalone documentary evidence for current accounts, savings accounts and Cash ISAs is a minor gap; the fact find narrative references cash savings and ISA holdings, and these are low-complexity products that do not alter the overall experience picture materially. The recommended products (Aviva Personal Pension with a multi-asset fund, Stocks and Shares ISA, and GIA, all using blended cautious/multi-asset funds) are of moderate complexity and broadly proportionate to the demonstrated level of experience via the existing pension holding. No vulnerability indicators in the file appear to affect this assessment. Overall the pension experience assertion is well-supported; the minor overstatement about self-directed equity experience and the absence of separate corroboration for savings/ISA holdings are low-level concerns.
