  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report classifies the Aviva Personal Pension as 'Complex', citing complexity drivers including SIPP structure, flexi-access drawdown availability, tax treatment depending on contribution levels and individual circumstances, annual allowance and MPAA considerations, and IHT legislative changes from April 2027. The rationale for the pension recommendation includes: lower costs (platform charge reduced from 0.32% to 0.21%), professional oversight/fund monitoring, adviser charge facilitation, platform integration (pension, ISA and GIA on same Aviva Platform), risk alignment (current funds at risk level 6 do not match agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4), risk-aligned fund recommendation (Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II designed for risk level 4), performance consistency (lower volatility than current Vanguard LifeStrategy 60%/80% mix), and CIP oversight with quarterly investment committee review.
    File:     The case file contains a pension switching research report [P9] which shows a new solutions comparison ranking and stakeholder pension comparison, evidencing that product and platform selection was researched. The risk profile report [P14] records no additional notes on agreed risk level or investment target discussions. The fact find [P20] records the meeting as telephone on 06/08/2025. Adviser notes [P10] reference the inheritance, savings, ISA considerations, ATR 4, and pension contribution assessment questions. Notes [P11] record the client as retired with a DB income working part-time. The ISA and GIA key features documents [P4][P5][P1] and illustrations [P6][P2] are present. No category C passage (meeting note or file note) records the adviser explaining the complex pension features — SIPP structure, flexi-access drawdown, annual allowance/MPAA, or IHT changes from April 2027 — to the client or records the client's comprehension of those features.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the pension is complex and identifies five complexity drivers (SIPP, flexi-access drawdown, tax/contribution considerations, annual allowance/MPAA, IHT changes from April 2027), but no category C passage corroborates that these complex features were explained to the client or that the client demonstrated comprehension of them.
      - The risk profile report [P14] records no additional notes on the agreed risk level discussion, meaning the risk alignment rationale (current funds at risk level 6 vs agreed profile of 4) is stated in the report but not corroborated by a recorded discussion note.
      - Adviser notes [P10][P11] address contribution eligibility and pension logistics but do not record any explanation of SIPP complexity, drawdown options, MPAA, or IHT legislative changes to the client.
    The pension is correctly identified as complex, carrying five stated complexity drivers. The report provides a substantive rationale covering costs, risk alignment, fund suitability, performance and platform integration. However, the adequacy requirement for a complex product goes beyond stating a rationale in the report: where a product has unusual or complex features, there should be corroborating evidence — ideally category C meeting or file notes — that those features were explained to the client and that the client has the knowledge and experience to understand them. The file contains no such record. The risk profile report [P14] records no notes on the agreed risk level or investment target discussions. The adviser notes [P10][P11] are transactional and do not address the pension's complex features. No passage records an explanation of flexi-access drawdown, annual allowance/MPAA implications, or the IHT legislative changes from April 2027 being communicated to the client. The rationale in the report itself is internal to the report (category I) and cannot self-corroborate. The absence of any category C record of explanation or comprehension for a product classified as complex constitutes a missing component and weak justification gap. The vulnerability overlay does not add materially here as no vulnerability markers are recorded in the file that would elevate this gap further, but the absence of meeting notes is itself notable given the telephone meeting format. The guard that a rationale written for the adviser's file rather than the client still counts as evidence but does not satisfy the report's own duty to explain is relevant: even treating the research reports as file rationale, none addresses communication of complex features to this client.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). The suitability report states the GIA investment is £57,120, but the cashflow model records the Aviva GIA at £52,326 — a difference of £4,794, which corresponds to the initial adviser charge shown on the GIA illustration (£4,794.37). The report states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but simultaneously acknowledges 'We are unsure of your earnings this tax year, as you retired and then only worked part-time' — the earnings figure is not quantified anywhere in the file, making this assertion unverifiable. The cashflow modeller expenditure input is stated in the report as approximately £2,206 per month, but the cashflow file records total regular expenditure of £29,915 p/a (approximately £2,493 per month essential items only, or £3,710/month including holidays and discretionary) — the £2,206 figure does not reconcile with the cashflow model's own expenditure statement. 2 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that a net contribution of £2,880 (£3,600 gross, single) into the new Aviva pension is affordable. It states: (1) 'This is an amount you can comfortably afford from your inheritance funds without affecting your current lifestyle'; (2) 'This is covered by your joint expenditure and leaves you with surplus income each month, which you currently save or spend as required'; (3) 'However, your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level.' The report also records liquid assets of £190,000 (including £100,000 inheritance, £40,000 Chartered savings, £20,000 client's Cash ISA, £20,000 Yvette's Cash ISA, £10,000 emergency fund), an emergency fund of £10,000, and a cashflow modelling expenditure input of approximately £2,206 per month. The client has recently retired and stopped working.
    File:     The cashflow files show: Anthony's income is a £22,000 p/a DB pension; Yvette's income is £16,800 p/a salary (until 2030). Total regular income in the first year of the plan is £38,800 p/a. Total regular expenditure in the first year is £29,915 p/a (comprising Tax & NI £3,435, Entertainment & Leisure £9,600, Holidays £5,000, Household £8,760, Transport £2,640, Miscellaneous £480). The cashflow model shows a liquid assets opening position of £225,018 in 2025, rising to approximately £239,271 by 2030 before Yvette's retirement income drops away. The cashflow scenarios model an emergency fund of £10,000 (liquidated 8th) and the Aviva GIA at £52,326 (not £57,120 as stated in the suitability report). The cashflow model records 0% of any in-year surplus saved within the plan. The pension illustration shows total new payments of £82,600 at end of year 1 (i.e. transfer value £79,092 plus £3,600 gross contribution). No explicit income figure for Anthony post-retirement other than the £22,000 DB pension is evidenced in the file. The report acknowledges uncertainty about Anthony's earnings in the tax year.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states the GIA investment is £57,120, but the cashflow model records the Aviva GIA at £52,326 — a difference of £4,794, which corresponds to the initial adviser charge shown on the GIA illustration (£4,794.37).
      - The report states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but simultaneously acknowledges 'We are unsure of your earnings this tax year, as you retired and then only worked part-time' — the earnings figure is not quantified anywhere in the file, making this assertion unverifiable.
      - The cashflow modeller expenditure input is stated in the report as approximately £2,206 per month, but the cashflow file records total regular expenditure of £29,915 p/a (approximately £2,493 per month essential items only, or £3,710/month including holidays and discretionary) — the £2,206 figure does not reconcile with the cashflow model's own expenditure statement.
      - The report asserts surplus income each month and joint income covering expenditure, but the cashflow model shows 0% of any in-year surplus is saved within the plan, and from 2030 onwards (when Yvette retires) the model records annual deficits of approximately £7,000 per year met by drawing on liquid assets.
      - The emergency fund is stated as £10,000 and is evidenced in the cashflow files, but is the last asset liquidated (8th in order) — no quantification is provided of whether £10,000 is sufficient relative to the client's expenditure level of approximately £29,915–£44,520 p/a.
      - The £2,880 net pension contribution is described as affordable 'from your inheritance funds', but the earnings sufficiency claim is separately asserted for the same contribution — the basis of affordability is internally ambiguous (inheritance capital vs. earnings).
      - The cashflow model records the Aviva GIA at £52,326 whereas the suitability report and GIA illustration use £57,120/£57,100 — the cashflow appears to have been modelled on a post-adviser-charge basis, but this is not stated or reconciled in the suitability report.
    The initial commitment of £2,880 net is funded from inheritance capital, not from income, so the guard protecting ongoing income commitments from being tested against monthly surplus does not apply here. The one-off pension transfer guard applies to the £79,092 switch but not to the additional £2,880 net contribution, which is a new capital deployment. The affordability assertion rests on three legs: (1) inheritance funds are available — evidenced (£100,000 inheritance recorded in cashflow); (2) surplus income covers joint expenditure — the cashflow files confirm total income of £38,800 against total expenditure of £29,915 in year 1, confirming a surplus of approximately £8,885 p/a in the near term, though this is modelled as 0% saved; and (3) current earnings are sufficient to support the contribution — this is unquantified and contradicted by the report's own acknowledgement of uncertainty about earnings. The earnings sufficiency claim is a potential concern in its own right: the client has stopped working, only worked part-time briefly, and the report does not quantify residual earnings against the £3,600 gross threshold — it merely asserts sufficiency while admitting ignorance. The cashflow expenditure discrepancy is material: the report quotes £2,206/month as the cashflow modeller input, but the cashflow's own first-year expenditure statement totals £29,915 p/a (£2,493/month before discretionary and holidays), and approximately £3,710/month including all items. The £2,206 figure appears to conflate the income target with expenditure, as flagged in the extraction ambiguities. This means the cashflow model may be using an understated expenditure figure relative to the client's actual lifestyle costs. The GIA value discrepancy (£57,120 in the report vs. £52,326 in the cashflow) is explained by the initial adviser charge of £4,794.37 being deducted at source — the cashflow models the post-charge invested amount. This is not a fabrication but is unexplained in the suitability report and creates an inconsistency a reader would notice. The emergency fund of £10,000 is asserted and evidenced but never quantified against expenditure (approximately 4 months' basic expenditure at £2,493/month, or 2.7 months at £3,710/month) — this is a low-severity disclosure shortfall. Overall, the most material issues are: (a) the unquantified and self-contradicted earnings sufficiency assertion, and (b) the cashflow expenditure input being materially inconsistent with the stated current expenditure figures. These constitute a Potential Concern at Moderate severity.

  [G5.2] Financial situation plus cashflow supports the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Total assets £669,092, total liabilities £0, net worth £669,092. Liquid assets £190,000 (comprising £100,000 inheritance, £40,000 Chartered savings, £20,000 client Cash ISA, £20,000 Yvette's Moneybox Cash ISA, £10,000 emergency fund). Emergency fund £10,000 stated as sufficient. Cashflow modelled at £2,206/month expenditure. Monthly income £2,590, disposable income £383.33. Baseline cashflow shows no shortfall throughout lifetime. Three stress tests (high inflation 6%, market crash 30% in 2028, early death 2040 scenarios) all show no shortfall. Life expectancy: male 55 = 84, female 59 = 87; funds projected to last well beyond. Cashflow assumptions: general expenditure 2%, savings 1.25%, state pensions 2.5%, property 4.7%, Aviva platform investments 4.5%, surplus income 0% saved. Recommended contributions: £2,880 net (£3,600 gross) pension contribution described as supported by current earnings, though report acknowledges uncertainty ('We are unsure of your earnings this tax year'). GIA investment £57,120 vs stated intention to invest remaining £60,000 of inheritance — £2,880 difference not explicitly reconciled. Stated current expenditure in 'Your current situation' section: £2,493/month essential plus £800/month discretionary plus ~£5,000/year holidays (~£3,710/month equivalent), which differs materially from the £2,206/month used in the cashflow model.
    File:     Fact find [P1]: calculated total monthly household expenditure £2,206.67; total net monthly income £2,590.00; total monthly disposable income £383.33; emergency fund amount put aside £10,000, amount required £10,000, shortfall £0. Cashflow model files: first year of plan income — Anthony DB pension £22,000, Yvette employment £16,800, total £38,800 [P20, P12, P14]. First year total regular expenditure £29,915 per annum [P20, P9, P12, P14], comprising Tax & NI £3,435, Entertainment & Leisure £9,600, Holidays £5,000, Household £8,760, Transport £2,640, Miscellaneous £480.     Discrepancies:
      - Cashflow modelled expenditure £2,206/month vs stated current expenditure ~£3,710/month (£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + £417/month holidays equivalent) in the suitability report's 'current situation' section — the cashflow model uses a figure that is approximately 40% lower than the full current expenditure described in the same report.
      - Cashflow modelled expenditure £2,206/month matches the fact find figure of £2,206.67 [P1] but the fact find's own expenditure breakdown in the cashflow files shows first-year total regular expenditure of £29,915/year (£2,493/month) [P20], producing an internal inconsistency as to which figure the model actually uses.
      - GIA investment amount £57,120 vs client's stated intention to invest 'remaining £60,000' of inheritance — £2,880 shortfall not explicitly reconciled in the report (though it plausibly equals the net pension contribution).
      - Report states current earnings 'sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but also states client 'stopped working altogether'; earnings for the tax year are unquantified and the report acknowledges uncertainty ('We are unsure of your earnings this tax year') — the sufficiency claim is not supported by a quantified figure.
      - Total assets stated as £669,092 in the suitability report but this figure is not corroborated in any supporting document; the nearest cashflow file figure (liquid assets + pension opening 2025 = £225,018) is far lower, with the balance presumably attributable to property not valued in the retrieved passages.
      - Cashflow first-year income in model files is £38,800/year [P20, P12] (Anthony DB £22,000 + Yvette employment £16,800), which annualises the £2,590/month net income figure (£31,080/year) differently — the £38,800 gross/combined figure differs from £2,590 net monthly, suggesting the model uses gross or combined figures while the fact find records net figures; this difference is not reconciled in the report.
      - Emergency fund of £10,000 is stated and fact find confirms it as fully funded with no shortfall [P1], but the report does not address whether this remains intact after the recommended capital deployments (ISA transfer £20,000, GIA £57,120, pension contribution £2,880 net, renovations £40,000 totalling ~£120,000 from the £190,000 liquid assets), leaving ~£70,000 residual — the £10,000 emergency fund appears to be a separate identified pot, but no post-recommendation liquidity reconciliation is shown.
      - Life expectancy for 55-year-old male stated as approximately 84; nearest passage [P13] shows age 85 in chart labels — minor difference but noted. Similarly female 59 stated as 87; nearest in [P17] is 88.
    The primary and most material concern is the discrepancy between the cashflow modelled expenditure of £2,206/month and the stated current expenditure of approximately £3,710/month (£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + ~£417/month for £5,000 holidays) described in the suitability report's own current situation section. The fact find [P1] records £2,206.67 as the 'Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure', which is the figure the cashflow model uses. However, the cashflow files [P20, P9, P12, P14] show a first-year total regular expenditure of £29,915/year (equivalent to ~£2,493/month before Tax & NI, or ~£2,493/month of actual spending items), which is materially higher than £2,206/month. The £2,206/month figure appears to be net of tax (Tax & NI of £3,435/year = ~£286/month is added separately in the cashflow files bringing total to ~£29,915/year = ~£2,493/month of outgoings). This is an internal inconsistency already flagged in the extraction: the cashflow assumption section uses £2,206/month as the expenditure input, but the detailed expenditure statement in the model itself totals £29,915/year gross including tax. The report conflates the net-of-tax expenditure figure (£2,206/month) with total expenditure, underdisclosing the true total. Furthermore, the suitability report's current situation section describes £2,493 essential + £800 discretionary per month (~£3,293/month, ~£39,516/year before holidays), materially exceeding even the £29,915/year model figure. This means the cashflow model appears to exclude discretionary expenditure of ~£800/month that the report itself acknowledges the clients currently incur. The surplus income assumption of 0% saved means all surplus is spent — but if actual expenditure is higher than modelled, the projected surpluses are overstated and the no-shortfall conclusion is at risk of being based on an understated expenditure assumption. The earnings sufficiency claim for the pension contribution is also a genuine concern: the report simultaneously states the client stopped working altogether and that earnings are sufficient, without quantifying those earnings. The £3,600 gross/£2,880 net contribution sits at the maximum non-earner limit, suggesting the report may be relying on the non-earner allowance rather than actual earnings — but this is not stated explicitly. The GIA £57,120 vs £60,000 intention difference is minor and plausibly explained by the net pension contribution of £2,880 being deducted, but this is not stated in the report. The emergency fund of £10,000 matches the fact find exactly [P1] and appears to be a separately identified pot; post-recommendation liquid assets (~£70,000 residual from £190,000 after £120,000 deployments) appear adequate for emergencies, though this reconciliation is not explicitly performed in the report. The total assets figure of £669,092 cannot be corroborated from the supporting documents as retrieved — the cashflow files show opening liquid assets + pension of £225,018, with the balance (~£444,000) presumably attributable to property — but no property valuation appears in the retrieved passages. The life expectancy minor discrepancies (84 vs nearest 85; 87 vs nearest 88 in chart labels) are likely chart label artefacts and not material given the guard about chart parseability. The most significant finding is the expenditure inconsistency: the cashflow model uses £2,206/month which is materially lower than the £2,493 + £800 + holidays figure described in the same report, and the no-shortfall conclusion rests on this understated expenditure input. This is a Data inconsistency and Weak justification concern of Moderate-to-High severity given that the cashflow model's conclusions drive the affordability assessment. The earnings sufficiency claim without quantification is a further Weak justification / Missing evidence concern. Vulnerability: no specific vulnerability factors have been identified in the retrieved passages as materially affecting this affordability assessment.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states: (1) £10,000 emergency fund held; the client has confirmed sufficient liquid capital, immediately or on short notice, to meet unexpected emergencies; (2) total liquid assets of £190,000 comprising £100,000 inheritance (due this month), £40,000 Chartered savings, £20,000 client Cash ISA, £20,000 Yvette Cash ISA (Moneybox), and £10,000 emergency fund; (3) circa £40,000 of inheritance earmarked for home renovations; (4) remaining £60,000 of inheritance intended for investment, possibly including Cash ISA; (5) the GIA investment is £57,120 (not £60,000); (6) significant liquid cash available for instant access during pension/investment switching; (7) investments accessible in 10–15 working days; (8) current expenditure modelled at approximately £2,206 per month in the cashflow modeller; (9) stated current expenditure of £2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + ~£5,000 annual holidays (≈ £3,710/month equivalent); (10) no required amount for the emergency fund is stated anywhere in the report; (11) the report asserts earnings are 'sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' of £2,880 net despite the client having stopped working altogether.
    File:     The fact find [P11] records: amount put aside for emergency fund £10,000; amount of emergency fund required £10,000; emergency fund shortfall £0. Total monthly household expenditure £2,206.67; total net monthly income £2,590.00; total monthly disposable income £383.33. The cashflow models [P3], [P6] show the emergency fund of £10,000 listed as the last asset to be liquidated (8th), with all other liquid and investment assets drawn down before it. The Aviva GIA is shown in the cashflow as £52,326 (not £57,120 as recommended). Income: Yvette salary £16,800 p.a. until 2030; State Pension £11,973 from age 67. The cashflow stress tests [P4], [P5], [P7] show liquid assets and accessible pensions remaining positive throughout the projection period across multiple scenarios. No document quantifies what emergency fund amount is required beyond the fact find's self-referential £10,000 = £10,000 required. The fact find expenditure figure of £2,206.67/month is consistent with the cashflow modeller input but inconsistent with the suitability report's own current situation section (£2,493 + £800 + £5,000/year holidays ≈ £3,710/month).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the intended GIA investment is from 'remaining £60,000' of inheritance, but the actual recommended GIA amount is £57,120 — a £2,880 shortfall not explicitly reconciled in the report.
      - The cashflow model [P3] shows the Aviva GIA as £52,326, whereas the recommended GIA investment is £57,120 — a difference of £4,794 with no explanation.
      - The cashflow modeller expenditure input of £2,206/month (consistent with fact find £2,206.67) conflicts with the suitability report's own current situation section stating £2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + ~£5,000 annual holidays (≈ £3,710/month equivalent).
      - The emergency fund sufficiency is asserted by the client's self-confirmation with no independent quantification of the required amount; the fact find records required amount as £10,000 which is simply the held amount — the requirement figure is not independently derived.
      - The report states the client has 'stopped working altogether' but also asserts 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' — these are in tension and the earnings figure underpinning the pension contribution basis is unquantified in the evidence.
      - The £60,000 figure for intended investment from inheritance is not matched in the evidence (nearest is £63,398 in a cashflow projection table, 5.7% away).
      - The 10–15 working days access timeframe is stated in the report but 15 is not matched in the evidence passages.
    The core emergency fund position is that the report asserts £10,000 is sufficient, the client has confirmed this, and the fact find records required = held = £10,000 with zero shortfall [P11]. However, this is a self-referential adequacy assessment: the required amount is simply set equal to the held amount rather than being independently derived from expenditure. Given the suitability report's own current situation section records expenditure of approximately £3,710/month equivalent (£2,493 + £800 + £5,000/year holidays), a £10,000 emergency fund represents less than three months of stated expenditure. This is never addressed in the report. The guard 'An asserted sufficiency with no quantified requirement cannot be verified — record it as unquantified' applies: the report's claim of sufficiency cannot be confirmed or denied, but the self-referential nature of the fact find figure means there is no independent evidence supporting the claimed sufficiency. The cashflow models [P3], [P6] correctly show the emergency fund as the last asset liquidated, confirming the adviser's sequencing approach protects it, and the stress-test cashflows [P4], [P5], [P7] show the plan remains solvent across multiple scenarios — these support the broader liquidity picture. The significant liquid cash holdings (£40,000 Chartered savings, two Cash ISAs of £20,000 each) do provide a meaningful liquidity buffer over and above the emergency fund, and the report's assertion of 'significant liquid cash available' [I] is supported by the overall asset picture in the cashflow models. The GIA discrepancy between the cashflow model (£52,326 in [P3]) and the recommended investment (£57,120) is a data inconsistency not explained in the file — the cashflow may have been prepared on an earlier or different figure. The expenditure inconsistency between £2,206/month (fact find and cashflow modeller) and ~£3,710/month (suitability report current situation section) is a material data inconsistency flagged in the extraction's internal inconsistencies — it means the cashflow modeller is using a figure materially lower than stated current expenditure, potentially understating the income required and overstating the surplus. The disposable income of £383.33/month from the fact find [P11] is based on the £2,206.67 figure; if the £3,710/month figure from the suitability report's own current situation section were used, there would be a significant deficit rather than a surplus, which would alter the affordability conclusion materially. The pension contribution earnings ambiguity is a process risk: the report simultaneously states the client has stopped working and that earnings are sufficient to support the contribution, without quantifying the residual earnings. On balance: the emergency fund claim is unquantified in its requirement; the expenditure figure underpinning the cashflow and affordability assessment materially understates the suitability report's own current situation expenditure; and the GIA cashflow input does not match the recommended amount. These together constitute material data inconsistencies affecting the affordability assessment.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts the £2,880 net initial pension contribution is affordable because it is funded from inheritance funds 'without affecting your current lifestyle'. It further asserts that 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' and that expenditure is covered by joint income leaving surplus income each month. No monthly surplus figure is stated in the report. The GIA investment of £57,120 and ISA transfer of £20,000 are presented as one-off capital deployments from the inheritance and existing savings. The emergency fund is stated at £10,000. The report uses a cashflow modeller with expenditure input of approximately £2,206 per month.
    File:     The Fact Find records Anthony Barnes's total net monthly income as £2,590.00 (comprising £890.00 net employed income and £1,700.00 net private pension/Aon Final Salary), with total monthly expenditure of £2,206.67 and monthly disposable income of £383.33. The emergency fund is recorded as £10,000 with a required amount of £10,000 and zero shortfall. The cashflow modeller in the Fact Find shows revised monthly income of £2,590.00 and revised expenditure of £2,206.67, giving a revised disposable income of £383.33. However, the report separately states the client's current essential expenditure is £2,493 per month plus £800 discretionary plus approximately £5,000 annual holidays (approximately £3,710/month equivalent). The Fact Find income figure of £14,000 gross annual appears to relate to employment income, and the client is recorded as 'recently retired' and having 'stopped working altogether'. The cashflow model in the supporting files (Yvette Predeceases, Market Crash, Tony Predeceases scenarios) uses total regular expenses of £26,480 per annum (approximately £2,207/month), which aligns with the Fact Find figure of £2,206.67 but not with the higher expenditure figures stated in the suitability report's current situation section.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report's cashflow modeller input uses approximately £2,206/month expenditure, which matches the Fact Find figure of £2,206.67, but the suitability report's own current situation section states essential expenditure of £2,493/month plus £800 discretionary plus ~£5,000 annual holidays — approximately £3,710/month equivalent — which is materially higher and is not reconciled with the cashflow input.
      - The report asserts 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' but also states the client has 'stopped working altogether'; the Fact Find records employed income of £890/month net and gross earnings of £14,000 per annum, but the employment end date is recorded as 06/08/2025 and the client's retirement status creates ambiguity about whether any earnings remain at date of advice to support the £3,600 gross pension contribution.
      - The report states the £2,880 net contribution is affordable 'from your inheritance funds' yet simultaneously asserts it is supported by 'current earnings' — these two bases are inconsistent with each other and the report does not reconcile them.
      - The monthly disposable income evidenced in the Fact Find is £383.33. While the one-off capital commitments (GIA £57,120, ISA £20,000, pension £2,880 net) are sourced from capital rather than income, the report does not quantify whether the £383.33 monthly surplus is sufficient for any ongoing adviser charges or product charges drawn from cash accounts, nor is any ongoing monthly commitment explicitly quantified against the surplus.
      - The report states the client intends to invest 'the remaining £60,000' of inheritance into the GIA, but the actual GIA investment is £57,120 — a £2,880 shortfall equal to the net pension contribution — which is not explicitly reconciled in the report.
      - The cashflow modeller expenditure input of approximately £2,206/month effectively equates the target retirement income of £2,200/month with current expenditure, conflating two distinct figures without explanation.
    The central affordability concern is the inconsistency between the cashflow modeller expenditure input (£2,206/month, consistent with the Fact Find) and the current expenditure figures stated in the suitability report's own current situation section (£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + ~£417/month holidays = ~£3,710/month). The cashflow modelling appears to use a figure that matches the target retirement income (£2,200/month) rather than actual current expenditure, which means the cashflow model may be underestimating current spending and therefore overstating the available surplus. This is an internal inconsistency within the report itself, corroborated by the Fact Find's separate statement of higher expenditure categories. The Fact Find's cashflow section produces a disposable income of £383.33/month based on the £2,206.67 expenditure figure, which does not capture the full expenditure picture. The emergency fund of £10,000 is quantified in the Fact Find and shown as meeting the required amount with zero shortfall — this element is adequately evidenced. The capital commitments (GIA, ISA, pension) are funded from the £100,000 inheritance and existing savings, not from monthly income, so the £383.33 surplus is not tested against ongoing contribution requirements in the strict sense. However, the report's assertion that earnings support the pension contribution is undermined by the client's retired status, and the report acknowledges uncertainty about earnings for the tax year ('We are unsure of your earnings this tax year') while simultaneously asserting sufficiency — this is a weak justification. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability factors identified in the evidence appear to materially alter the affordability assessment for this client. The primary finding is that the cashflow modeller input materially understates current expenditure as described elsewhere in the report, which means the affordability basis for the recommendation is not fully internally consistent, and the earnings sufficiency assertion for the pension contribution is weakly evidenced given the client's retired status.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts total liquid assets of £190,000 comprising: £100,000 inheritance (due this month), £40,000 Chartered savings, £20,000 client's Cash ISA, £20,000 Yvette's Cash ISA (Moneybox), and £10,000 emergency fund. The emergency fund is identified as a separate £10,000 cash holding. The report asserts that the recommendation is funded from liquid cash holdings, inheritance funds, and Aviva Platform investments. No shortfall on the emergency fund is present in the extracted model (path absent). The report states a cashflow modeller has been used and that ongoing commitments are affordable, with the £10,000 emergency fund retained outside the recommended investment structure. The cashflow modeller input uses £2,206/month expenditure. The recommendation involves a one-off pension contribution of £2,880 net, an ISA transfer of £20,000, and a GIA investment of £57,120 — all funded from the inheritance and existing savings rather than from the emergency fund.
    File:     The cashflow file documents show the Cash Emergency Fund at £10,000 and record it as LIQUIDATED 8TH in the liquidation order — meaning it is the last liquid asset to be drawn on, behind the Surplus Account, Chartered Savings Account, Aviva ISA, Wife's MoneyBox Cash ISA, and Aviva GIA. The cashflow scenarios (Current Situation, Market Crash, Higher Inflation, Tony Predeceases in 2040, Yvette Predeceases in 2040) all show the emergency fund at £10,000 in the same LIQUIDATED 8TH position. The cashflow tables show Liquid Assets & Accessible Pensions remaining positive and no year with a red shortfall bar across all scenarios presented. Yvette's salary of £16,800 p/a is modelled to 2030, and State Pension of £11,973 p/a is modelled from age 67. The cashflow modeller input records £26,480 total regular expenses (Higher Inflation scenario) and Yvette's salary of £22,000 (inflated 6% p/a) in that scenario. The Aviva GIA in the cashflow is shown as £52,326 (not £57,120 as recommended). The emergency fund is shown as LIQUIDATED 8TH in all scenarios, meaning it is never the first resource drawn and is preserved unless all other liquid assets are exhausted, which the tables show does not occur in the modelled lifetime.
    Discrepancies:
      - The cashflow model records the Aviva GIA at £52,326 whereas the suitability report recommends investing £57,120 into the GIA — a difference of £4,794 that is unexplained in the file.
      - The emergency fund is included in the cashflow liquidation order (LIQUIDATED 8TH), meaning it is technically available to be drawn upon if all other liquid assets are exhausted; the report asserts it is retained as a separate emergency fund but does not explicitly state it is ring-fenced outside the cashflow model.
      - The cashflow expenditure input of £2,206/month is materially lower than the stated current expenditure of £2,493/month essential + £800/month discretionary (approximately £3,293/month), and the Higher Inflation scenario records £26,480 total regular expenses (approximately £2,207/month), consistent with the lower figure rather than the fact-find figure — this discrepancy in cashflow inputs is not reconciled in the report.
      - The report states total liquid assets of £190,000 but the cashflow model opening balances do not explicitly show £190,000 as a starting liquid asset figure; the £100,000 inheritance is not separately itemised in the cashflow scenario files.
      - The /financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall path is absent from the extraction — the report does not quantify whether £10,000 is sufficient relative to monthly expenditure, nor does it state how many months' expenditure this represents.
    The emergency fund of £10,000 is identified in both the report and all cashflow scenario files. Critically, in the cashflow model it is designated LIQUIDATED 8TH — the last resource to be drawn upon — which is consistent with the guard that notes a cashflow scheduling the emergency fund to be spent has not retained it. However, across all modelled scenarios (Current Situation, Market Crash, Higher Inflation, Tony Predeceases, Yvette Predeceases), the cashflow tables show liquid assets and accessible pensions remaining materially positive throughout the plan lifetime, with no year showing a red shortfall. This means the emergency fund is never in practice reached in any modelled scenario, which provides reasonable support for the assertion that it is preserved. The GIA discrepancy (£52,326 in cashflow vs £57,120 recommended) is unexplained and represents a data inconsistency between the cashflow model inputs and the recommended investment amount, though the direction of the difference (cashflow shows a lower value) does not worsen the resilience picture. The more significant concern is the cashflow expenditure input of ~£2,206/month used in the model, which is substantially lower than the £3,293/month current expenditure stated in the fact-find (essential plus discretionary, excluding holidays). This means the cashflow model, while showing surpluses, may be based on an understated expenditure base — this is a material cashflow input discrepancy. The report does not reconcile these figures. The report's assertion that the £10,000 emergency fund is sufficient is never quantified against monthly expenditure (approximately 3 months at the lower modelled figure, approximately 3 months at the fact-find essential-only figure), and no explicit commentary on adequacy of the emergency fund relative to expenditure is present. The extraction confirms /financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall is absent because the report does not address it. This is a weak justification and a data inconsistency in the cashflow inputs. The vulnerability overlay does not add a specific concern here beyond what is already assessed — no vulnerability driver has been identified that would specifically heighten the emergency fund risk.

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The client's liquidity and access need is 'On demand'. The recommended wrappers are SIPP, GIA and ISA. The selected retirement age for the SIPP is 60. Planned withdrawals from the SIPP are not recorded in the extracted model. The client (Anthony) is currently age 55 (born 06/01/1970) per the cashflow file. The GIA and ISA are open-ended wrappers with no statutory access restriction. The SIPP is a pension wrapper subject to minimum pension age rules.
    File:     The cashflow files confirm Anthony is 55 years old (age 55/59 in 2025 per P19; DOB 06/01/1970 per P14). The cashflow current situation (P19) shows liquid assets and accessible pensions of £225,018 in 2025, with the plan running surplus until Yvette's income stops around age 60 (2030), at which point an annual deficit of ~£7,000 emerges and is drawn from liquid assets. The cashflow's own definition (P12/P13) distinguishes 'Pension - accessible' (already past minimum pension age) from 'Pension - non accessible' (not yet reached minimum pension age). The GIA key features (P20) confirm withdrawals can be made at any time. The ISA key features (P6) confirm the ISA is a flexible wrapper with no access restriction. The Aviva pension key features (P1, P3) confirm the pension is a drawdown-capable product but does not specify when access becomes available in relation to the client's current age. No passage explicitly states the current statutory minimum pension age (NMPA) of 55 rising to 57 in 2028. The cashflow does not separately label pension assets as non-accessible for Anthony at age 55, which would be consistent with the pension being accessible at 55 today but potentially non-accessible after April 2028 when NMPA rises to 57.
    Discrepancies:
      - The access need is stated as 'On demand', but the SIPP wrapper has a statutory minimum pension age restriction. Anthony is currently 55. The NMPA is legislatively scheduled to rise to 57 in April 2028, at which point Anthony will be 58 — meaning a transitional period exists where pension access may be restricted depending on scheme rules and any transitional protections. The report does not address this.
      - Planned withdrawals from the SIPP are absent from the extracted model (confirmed by extraction metadata as NotPresentInDocument), so the report's treatment of pension access timing cannot be verified.
      - The cashflow modeller shows a deficit beginning at age 60 (2030) that is met from liquid assets (P19). It is not clear from the passages whether the pension is treated as accessible or non-accessible during the 2025–2030 deficit period, and the report does not narrate this explicitly.
      - The cashflow expenditure input (£2,206/month modelled) is materially lower than stated current expenditure (£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + ~£417/month holidays = ~£3,710/month equivalent), which is an inconsistency in cashflow inputs noted in the extraction's internal inconsistencies.
    The GIA and ISA wrappers are fully liquid and consistent with an on-demand access need — no concern arises there. The SIPP raises two issues. First, Anthony is currently 55 and can access the pension today, so the minimum pension age guard (check age before flagging) is satisfied for the present moment and there is no current lockout. However, the NMPA rises to 57 in April 2028, and Anthony will be 58 at that point — so after a brief transitional window he will again be above the new NMPA. The practical impact on liquidity is therefore limited, but the report does not discuss this at all, leaving the on-demand assertion unsupported for the transitional period 2028 onwards without confirmation of scheme transitional protection. Second, and more materially, the planned withdrawals field is absent — the report does not narrate when or how the pension is intended to be accessed, what income will be drawn and from which wrapper, or how the GIA/ISA liquid assets interact with the pension during the deficit years shown in the cashflow (from age 60). The cashflow does show liquid assets remain positive throughout the plan with no red bars visible from the passages available, which provides some comfort that capital is not inaccessible when needed. However, the cashflow expenditure input of £2,206/month is substantially lower than the stated current expenditure of ~£3,710/month, meaning the cashflow's apparent sufficiency rests on an understatement of expenditure — this is a material data quality and consistency concern that undermines the affordability conclusion. The on-demand access assertion for the SIPP specifically is not supported by any evidence addressing access timing or planned withdrawal strategy. Taken together: the GIA and ISA access is unproblematic; the SIPP transitional NMPA issue is real but limited in practical impact given Anthony will be 58 when NMPA rises; but the absence of planned withdrawal narration and the cashflow expenditure inconsistency mean the liquidity and affordability case is inadequately evidenced.

The Yvette salary discrepancy (£28,000 vs £16,800) is a genuine data inconsistency between the suitability report and the supporting cashflow evidence and is not explained by any guard. This affects the affordability case materially.

  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report identifies the following foreseeable financial needs and future expenditure: (1) Yvette to reduce to 3 days/week from next year, reducing employment income pro-rata; (2) Yvette to fully retire when C1 turns 60; (3) C1 to receive State Pension of £11,973 p/a from age 67; (4) C2 to receive State Pension of £11,973 p/a from age 67; (5) £100,000 inheritance due this month — circa £40,000 earmarked for renovations, £60,000 to be invested; (6) IHT rule change from 6 April 2027 affecting DC pensions; (7) a small shortfall period when Yvette retires until her State Pension begins; (8) significant liquid cash available for emergency access during switching. The cashflow modeller expenditure input is stated as approximately £2,206 per month. The report also notes current expenditure of approximately £2,493 essential plus £800 discretionary plus ~£5,000 annual holidays (approximately £3,710/month equivalent). The emergency fund is stated as £10,000.
    File:     The Fact Find records: total monthly household expenditure £2,206.67; total net monthly income £2,590.00; total monthly disposable income £383.33; emergency fund amount put aside £10,000; amount required £10,000; shortfall £0. The Fact Find income section shows Anthony: £890 net/month employment plus £1,700 net/month private pension (Aon Final Salary) = £2,590/month; Yvette: employment £16,800/year per cashflow. The cashflow current situation document records first-year income: Anthony DB Pension £22,000, Yvette Employment £16,800, Total £38,800; first-year regular expenditure: Tax & NI £3,435, Entertainment & Leisure £9,600, Holidays £5,000, Household £8,760, Transport £2,640, Miscellaneous £480, Total £29,915 (~£2,493/month). Min income in retirement is £2,200 net per month.' The cashflow timelines confirm Anthony retires at 67 and State Pension age 67. The higher inflation scenario cashflow and other scenario documents corroborate the structural plan.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report's cashflow modeller input uses approximately £2,206/month expenditure, which matches the Fact Find's £2,206.67 figure, but the same report also states current expenditure of approximately £2,493 essential plus £800 discretionary plus ~£5,000 annual holidays (~£3,710/month equivalent) in its current situation narrative — these two figures within the report are materially inconsistent with each other, and the cashflow modeller appears to use only £2,206 rather than the full ~£3,710.
      - The Fact Find first-year cashflow shows total regular expenditure of £29,915 (~£2,493/month excluding tax), which is substantially higher than the £2,206 modelled expenditure input cited in the report — indicating the cashflow model may understate current expenditure.
      - The suitability report asserts the client intends to invest 'the remaining £60,000' of inheritance, but the recommended GIA is £57,120 — a £2,880 shortfall that is not explicitly reconciled in the report (though plausibly explained by the net pension contribution).
      - The Fact Find records Anthony's income as £890 net employment plus £1,700 net pension = £2,590/month, but the cashflow current situation document shows Anthony's DB Pension as £22,000 p/a (£1,833/month), not £1,700/month — a £133/month difference in pension income.
      - The suitability report notes Yvette intends to reduce to 3 days/week from next year, but the Fact Find income changes section states 'No' expected changes in income in the foreseeable future — the fact find does not reflect this foreseeable income reduction.
      - The cashflow model opening liquid assets for 2025 are £142,326, which does not immediately reconcile with the stated total liquid assets of £190,000 (£100k inheritance + £40k Chartered savings + £20k client ISA + £20k Yvette ISA + £10k emergency fund) — the difference (~£47,674) is unexplained in the pack, though the £40k earmarked for renovations and £57,120 GIA investment could partially account for it.
      - Both State Pension figures of £11,973 p/a for C1 and C2 are stated in the report but no supporting State Pension forecast document appears in the evidence pack to corroborate either figure.
    The central affordability concern for this requirement is the expenditure inconsistency. The suitability report uses £2,206/month as the cashflow modeller expenditure input — consistent with the Fact Find's £2,206.67 figure — but the same report's current situation narrative discloses actual expenditure of approximately £2,493 essential plus £800 discretionary plus £5,000 holidays annually, totalling approximately £3,710/month. The cashflow model first-year expenditure of £29,915 (£2,493/month excluding tax) from the current situation cashflow document also sits above the £2,206 input. This is a material discrepancy: the cashflow model appears to have been run on an expenditure figure that is lower than the client's stated outgoings — a moderate data inconsistency concern per the severity guidance. The £10,000 emergency fund is confirmed by the Fact Find (amount required £10,000, shortfall £0), so the emergency fund is quantified and evidenced as sufficient. The foreseeable income changes (Yvette reducing hours, then fully retiring when Anthony turns 60, the State Pension shortfall window) are referenced in the cashflow model with retirement and State Pension age events at 67 for Anthony, consistent with the report. The capacity for loss section acknowledges a shortfall period when Yvette retires until her State Pension begins, which the cashflow model structure supports. However, the Fact Find records 'No' to expected income changes, which contradicts the report's assertion about Yvette's planned hours reduction — a data inconsistency that should have been captured in the Fact Find. The £57,120 vs £60,000 inheritance investment discrepancy is noted but is plausibly explained by the £2,880 net pension contribution (£60,000 − £2,880 = £57,120), though the report does not state this explicitly. The Anthony income discrepancy (£1,700 net/month in Fact Find vs £1,833/month or £22,000 p/a in cashflow) is a data quality issue. The State Pension figures (£11,973 p/a each) are asserted by the report with no corroborating State Pension forecast documents in the pack — these are unsupported assertions. No vulnerability factors in the evidence appear to materially alter this affordability assessment. The dominant concern is the cashflow model using £2,206/month expenditure when the client's actual expenditure is materially higher at approximately £3,710/month, raising a question about whether the cashflow projections accurately reflect the clients' foreseeable financial position.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 9 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.7, G6.8, G6.9). The report states the client intends to invest 'the remaining £60,000' of inheritance in the GIA, but the recommended GIA investment is £57,120 — a £2,880 difference not explicitly reconciled in the report (the difference equals the net pension contribution, suggesting a deliberate allocation, but this is not stated). The cashflow modeller input uses £2,206/month expenditure, which is inconsistent with the stated current expenditure of approximately £3,710/month equivalent (£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + ~£417/month holiday allowance) recorded in the current situation section of the report. The existing pension risk level is recorded as '6' in the existing arrangements table, but the report separately refers to the individual Vanguard funds as risk level 5 (LifeStrategy 60%) and risk level 7 (LifeStrategy 80%) in the alternative solutions section — both characterisations are used for the same holdings. 7 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report provides rationale bullets for five recommendation groupings, each linked to objective IDs (OBJ1–OBJ6). The rationale covers: (1) pension platform switch to Aviva — cost reduction from 0.32% to 0.21% platform charge, administrative convenience, adviser charge deduction; (2) pension fund switch — current risk level 6 misaligned with agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile 4, recommended fund lower volatility, CIP oversight; (3) pension lump sum contribution — client recently retired, earnings uncertainty acknowledged, maximum non-earner contribution of £2,880 net / £3,600 gross, tax relief benefit, affordable from inheritance; (4) GIA investment — capital growth, CGT planning via annual allowance, Bed & ISA facility for future tax efficiency; (5) ISA investment — tax-efficient wrapper, Moneybox rate circa 4.5% variable versus recommended fund 5-year average 4.72%. The report flags two rationale items as not client-specific (isClientSpecific: false): 'ongoing professional oversight/investment monitoring' and 'CIP quarterly investment committee'. Objectives are linked across recommendations. The report does not state disadvantages or risks for the recommendations. Retirement age for the existing arrangement is not present. No income shortfall is quantified. The GIA amount (£57,120) differs from the stated client intention to invest 'the remaining £60,000' of inheritance without explicit reconciliation. The expenditure figure used in cashflow modelling (£2,206/month) is inconsistent with stated current expenditure (~£3,710/month equivalent). The existing pension fund risk level is described as '6' overall in the existing arrangements table but the report also refers to the individual Vanguard funds as risk 5 and risk 7.
    File:     [P14] (suitability report, category I) confirms the fund performance comparison narrative and the risk mismatch rationale verbatim. [P4] (pension switch research report) confirms the existing plan valuation of £79,092.32, the 0.32% platform charge, and the retirement age of 60 used in illustrations. [P2] (personal pension research report) and [P7] (pension review report) evidence the research process was conducted. [P3] and [P6] (ISA research reports) confirm the Aviva ISA Portfolio was selected from a whole-of-market search with filters including Bed and ISA availability, and describe the risk profile 4 Cautious Balanced definition. [P5] (GIA research report) evidences GIA research was conducted. [P8] (policy research) shows the 'Overall File Strategy' table with fields for 'Alternative Strategies', 'Justification for the initial and ongoing fee', 'Income/Expenditure Goal and Retirement', and 'Investment Choice Goals' — all of which are blank with no notes recorded. [P10] (risk profile report) shows 'No additional notes were recorded' for both investment target and agreed risk level discussion sections. [P12] and [P13] (client agreement) confirm adviser fee structure but add no rationale for recommendations. [P9] (firm description) confirms independent advice basis. The research reports confirm a research process was undertaken and products were shortlisted, but the supplementary justification fields in [P8] are entirely blank.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the client intends to invest 'the remaining £60,000' of inheritance in the GIA, but the recommended GIA investment is £57,120 — a £2,880 difference not explicitly reconciled in the report (the difference equals the net pension contribution, suggesting a deliberate allocation, but this is not stated).
      - The cashflow modeller input uses £2,206/month expenditure, which is inconsistent with the stated current expenditure of approximately £3,710/month equivalent (£2,493 essential + £800 discretionary + ~£417/month holiday allowance) recorded in the current situation section of the report.
      - The existing pension risk level is recorded as '6' in the existing arrangements table, but the report separately refers to the individual Vanguard funds as risk level 5 (LifeStrategy 60%) and risk level 7 (LifeStrategy 80%) in the alternative solutions section — both characterisations are used for the same holdings.
      - The report asserts earnings are 'sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' while simultaneously acknowledging uncertainty ('We are unsure of your earnings this tax year') and stating the client has stopped working altogether — this is an internal inconsistency in the rationale for the pension contribution.
      - The policy research file [P8] contains blank fields for 'Alternative Strategies', 'Justification for the initial and ongoing fee', 'Income/Expenditure Goal and Retirement', and 'Investment Choice Goals', providing no documented alternatives considered or reasons for rejection.
      - Two rationale items are flagged isClientSpecific: false in the canonical model ('ongoing professional oversight/investment monitoring' and 'CIP quarterly investment committee') — these are generic across clients.
      - No income shortfall is quantified in the report (extraction confirms NotPresentInDocument), meaning the link between the recommended strategy and the stated retirement income target of ~£2,200/month net is asserted but not demonstrated numerically.
      - Disadvantages and risks section is absent from the recommendations (extraction confirms NotPresentInDocument).
      - The retirement age for the existing arrangement is not present in the report (extraction confirms NotPresentInDocument), though [P4] records retirement age 60 in the switch illustration.
    The report contains a reasonable volume of client-specific rationale tied to named objectives and actual client figures (fund values, charge percentages, risk levels, inheritance amounts, contribution figures). The research file evidences that product, platform and investment research was conducted across all four recommended wrappers, and the pension switch research [P4] corroborates the cost reduction rationale. The ISA research [P3]/[P6] corroborates the Bed and ISA rationale and the whole-of-market selection. The fund performance comparison in [P14] corroborates the risk-realignment and volatility rationale for the pension fund switch.
    
    However, several material weaknesses arise:
    
    1. Alternatives: The policy research file [P8] has entirely blank fields for 'Alternative Strategies' across all schemes. While the report references the existing Vanguard funds as an alternative considered and rejected (with performance data), no documented alternatives are recorded for the GIA, ISA wrapper choice, or pension provider selection. The ISA research shows only 1 product from 168 matched filters — the filter set was so narrow it produced a single result, which means no genuine comparison was made and documented. This is a missing component for alternatives.
    
    2. GIA amount discrepancy: The £57,120 GIA investment versus the stated £60,000 intention is not reconciled in the report. The £2,880 difference matching the net pension contribution strongly implies deliberate structuring, but the report does not say this. This is a weak justification — a client reading the report cannot follow the arithmetic.
    
    3. Cashflow modelling inconsistency: The £2,206/month modelled expenditure versus ~£3,710/month stated current expenditure is a material inconsistency that undermines the retirement income sufficiency narrative (OBJ3). The report links recommendations to a retirement income target of ~£2,200/month but the stated actual expenditure is substantially higher. This means the cashflow rationale for whether the recommended strategy meets the client's income objective is built on a figure that does not match the client's stated expenditure — a data inconsistency that weakens the justification.
    
    4. Pension contribution rationale internal inconsistency: The report simultaneously states the client has stopped working and that earnings are sufficient. This is internally inconsistent. The recommended contribution (£2,880 net / £3,600 gross) is the maximum non-earner contribution — suggesting the report is relying on the non-earner allowance rather than actual earnings — but this is not clearly stated, leaving the contribution basis ambiguous.
    
    5. Risk level inconsistency: Using both '6' and '5 and 7' to describe the same existing holdings in different sections is minor but creates potential confusion about the degree of misalignment with risk profile 4.
    
    6. No quantified income shortfall: The report does not quantify the gap between projected retirement income from all sources and the £2,200/month target, so the reader cannot assess whether the recommended strategy is sufficient to meet OBJ3.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability drivers are recorded in the evidence as materially affecting the suitability assessment for this check.
    
    The false-positive guards: the two generic rationale items (isClientSpecific: false) are already flagged; the report's own extraction acknowledges them. The charge comparison rationale ('lower costs') is directionally accurate — the platform charge does reduce from 0.32% to 0.21%, though total ongoing charges increase from 0.54% to 0.62% including the new fund — this is disclosed in the switch charges table but the cost reduction rationale could be misleading as framed (it refers only to the platform charge, not the total). This is noted as a potential concern but is primarily addressed under CHK-009.
    
    On balance: the report has rationale, but it contains material weaknesses — an undocumented alternatives process, an unreconciled GIA figure, a cashflow modelling inconsistency material to the retirement income objective, and an internally contradictory contribution basis. These are sufficient for a Potential Concern at Moderate severity: rationale is present but contains gaps and inconsistencies that a reviewer cannot resolve from the document alone.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report justifies Aviva as provider on: FTSE 100 membership, S&P A (Stable) rating, £407bn AUM, over 6,900 funds, ISA flexibility (withdrawals and replacements in same tax year), transparent/competitive charges, and — the one client-specific point — that keeping pension, ISA and GIA on the same platform provides administrative convenience and a single point of contact (OBJ4). Investment strategy is justified on: risk alignment to client profile, diversification across asset classes, active/passive blend keeping costs low, client preference for simplicity, competitive charges, peer-relative performance, CIP governance via quarterly investment committee, and multi-asset fund design aligning to client risk profile and financial goals (OBJ1, OBJ3). Five alternatives were considered for the pension (Options 1–5) with Option 2 (internal fund switch) selected. Provider selection rationale items are predominantly generic/platform-marketing statements; only the platform-integration statement is flagged isClientSpecific=true.
    File:     Research documents confirm: (1) Pension switching research [P4, P16] shows Aviva Pension Portfolio – Choice was selected following a panel process (TFAS panel), risk level 4 matched, risk-rated multi-asset investment at level 4. (2) GIA research [P9, P15] and ISA research [P19, P20] both show CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc at risk level 4, matching agreed client risk level. (3) Pension fund research [P2] contains performance comparison: existing strategy (combined risk 6) vs recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II (risk 4) showing recommended fund provides more consistent performance aligned to risk level 4, lower volatility than current Vanguard mix, with competitive performance. (4) Research reports [P3, P17] show product filtered from Defaqto panel — only 1 product matched filters for the pension review. (5) No comparative provider analysis is present in the pack; the pension review report [P3] states 'No additional justification text has been entered' for platform selection and 'No Product DNA analysis was carried out'. (6) The ISA and GIA research reports [P6, P9] confirm risk level 4 mapping. (7) Firm uses TFAS panel for platform selection [P3]. (8) CIP governance claim (quarterly investment committee) in the report is not corroborated by any non-[I] document in the pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts provider selection rationale including 'Transparent, competitive charges with potential tiered pricing' and 'Over 6,900 funds' as justifications, but these are absolute marketing claims with no comparative evidence in the pack — the pension review research report [P3] explicitly states 'No additional justification text has been entered' for platform selection.
      - The report asserts 'The funds have performed well in comparison with their peers' as an investment rationale, but the performance evidence in [P2] compares the recommended fund against the existing strategy and a benchmark, not a peer group — no peer comparison table is present in the pack.
      - The report asserts CIP governance via 'a quarterly investment committee' and 'a leading external research team', but no document in the pack corroborates this claim — it is self-asserted in an [I] passage only.
      - The existing arrangement risk level is recorded as 6 in the existing arrangements table [P12], but the alternative solutions section of the report uses '5 and 7' (individual Vanguard fund levels) to characterise the same holdings — the report uses inconsistent risk descriptors for the existing arrangement across sections.
      - Provider rationale entries are predominantly generic (isClientSpecific=false for 5 of 6 items); the only client-specific provider rationale is the platform-integration convenience point, which is not supported by a comparative provider analysis showing alternatives were assessed.
      - No ISA-specific provider or wrapper justification is separately stated in the canonical model; the ISA wrapper rationale (annual allowance use, flexibility) is bundled into general platform claims without a standalone ISA wrapper justification linked to the client's specific ISA objective.
      - The report states 'No disadvantages and risks' path is absent (extraction flag: NotPresentInDocument for /recommendations/0/disadvantagesAndRisks), meaning no disadvantages or risks of the recommended products are disclosed in the report.
    The report provides a structured rationale for provider, wrapper and investment choices, and five alternatives for the pension are considered with Option 2 selected. Research documents confirm risk-level alignment (risk 4 for all new investments), product panel selection, and fund performance comparison against the existing strategy and benchmark. These aspects are supported by the evidence.
    
    However, several weaknesses remain after applying the guards:
    
    1. Provider selection: The report's provider rationale is overwhelmingly generic. The guard permits panel/CIP membership as legitimate justification, and [P3] confirms the TFAS panel was used. This partially covers the absence of a whole-of-market provider search. However, the pension review research report explicitly states 'No additional justification text has been entered' for platform selection — the report's specific claims about competitive charges and fund breadth are not comparative and are not supported by any comparative document. The guard cautions that marketing strengths stated in isolation are not a justification for selecting over alternatives, and here no comparative provider analysis is present.
    
    2. Investment rationale: The claim that funds 'performed well in comparison with their peers' is not corroborated — [P2] shows comparison against the existing strategy and a risk-level-4 benchmark, not a peer group. This is a weak justification as stated.
    
    3. CIP governance: The quarterly investment committee claim appears only in [I] passages. No supporting governance document is in the pack. The report cannot corroborate itself.
    
    4. Inconsistent risk descriptors: The existing arrangement is described as risk level 6 in the existing arrangements table but as 'risk levels 5 and 7' in the alternatives section. This is an internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction model.
    
    5. Missing disadvantages/risks disclosure: The extraction flags /recommendations/0/disadvantagesAndRisks as NotPresentInDocument. A suitability report should address risks and disadvantages of the recommended solution; their absence is a disclosure shortfall.
    
    6. ISA wrapper: No standalone client-specific ISA wrapper justification is present beyond generic platform claims.
    
    The vulnerability overlay does not appear to materially affect this check — no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would alter the justification requirements for product/wrapper/investment selection.
    
    Overall: the investment selection is reasonably evidenced by research documents, but provider selection rationale is substantially generic and unsupported by comparative analysis beyond panel membership, the peer-performance claim is not evidenced, CIP governance is self-asserted only, and disadvantages/risks are absent. These collectively constitute moderate weaknesses in justification and a disclosure shortfall.

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model asserts that 15 out of 17 rationale entries are client-specific (isClientSpecific = true) and 2 are not (false at indices 2 and 7). The report therefore claims the overwhelming majority of its recommendation rationale is personalised to Anthony Barnes's individual circumstances, objectives and needs.
    File:     The fact find (P3, P4, P6, P13) confirms client-specific objectives: review existing Aviva pension, invest ~£100k inheritance tax-efficiently, retire fully by age 60, minimum income in retirement of £2,200 net per month. The policy research (P13, P14) shows a pension switching research report was produced. The fact find records specific asset values, expenditure figures and preferences. However, the extraction flags two rationale entries as explicitly not client-specific (indices 2 and 7), and several internal inconsistencies undermine the personalisation claim: (a) the cashflow modeller uses £2,206/month expenditure which conflicts with the stated £3,293–£3,710/month current expenditure; (b) the report simultaneously states the client has stopped working and that earnings are sufficient for the pension contribution, without quantifying residual earnings; (c) the GIA investment of £57,120 is not explicitly reconciled against the stated intention to invest £60,000; (d) the existing plan risk level is described as both 6 (overall) and as individual funds at 5 and 7 in different sections. The ATR in the research note (P13) shows Natural ATR from Questions as 4, whereas the ATRQ was not completed at the fact-find stage and was sent separately.
    Discrepancies:
      - Two rationale entries (indices 2 and 7) are flagged as not client-specific by the extraction, contradicting the overall claim of personalised advice throughout.
      - Cashflow modeller expenditure input of ~£2,206/month (P5) is inconsistent with the stated current essential expenditure of ~£2,493/month plus £800 discretionary (£3,293/month), undermining the personalisation of the cashflow-based retirement planning rationale.
      - The report states the client has stopped working altogether but also asserts earnings are sufficient to support the £2,880 net pension contribution — no quantified earnings figure is provided to personalise or substantiate this claim.
      - GIA recommendation of £57,120 is not reconciled against the client's stated intention to invest the remaining £60,000 of inheritance; the £2,880 difference is not explained in the rationale.
      - The existing arrangement's risk level is described as 6 in one section and as individual funds of 5 and 7 in the alternatives section — inconsistent characterisation of the same holding undermines the personalised comparison rationale.
      - The ATRQ was not completed at the meeting and was sent for the client to complete independently (P2, P13), raising a question about whether the risk-profiling element of personalisation was complete at the time of recommendation.
    The report claims 15 of 17 rationale entries are client-specific, and the underlying file does contain genuine client-specific data (retirement age target of 60, income target of £2,200/month, specific asset values, named products). This confirms a foundation of personalisation exists. However, several material weaknesses reduce the quality of that personalisation. First, two entries are self-identified as not client-specific. Second, the cashflow modelling — which is central to the retirement income rationale — uses an expenditure figure (£2,206/month from P5) that contradicts the detailed expenditure breakdown recorded elsewhere (essential £2,493 + discretionary £800 = £3,293/month), meaning the retirement adequacy analysis is built on a figure that does not match the client's own stated spending. This is not boilerplate, but it is a data inconsistency that materially undermines the personalisation of the retirement planning rationale. Third, the employment/earnings ambiguity means the pension contribution rationale asserts sufficiency without evidence, which is a weak justification specific to this client's transitional employment status. The GIA figure discrepancy (£57,120 vs £60,000) is minor and plausibly explained by the pension contribution offset, but the report does not say so — leaving a gap in the client-specific reconciliation. The ATRQ incompleteness at meeting is noted in P2 and P13; P13 shows a Natural ATR of 4 was recorded from questions, suggesting the risk profile was ultimately captured, but the process gap is a concern. The regulatory standard wording guard applies to generic tax caveats and past-performance warnings and does not rescue the substantive inconsistencies identified. Overall: personalisation is present but materially undermined by the cashflow inconsistency and the earnings ambiguity, both of which affect the suitability conclusions directly. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the available evidence that would separately affect this assessment beyond what is already captured in the main analysis.
