  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report considers the following alternatives: (1) Retain current Vanguard funds within pension — rejected because funds are risk levels 5 and 7 respectively, misaligned with the agreed risk profile, and portfolio drift could cause risk to be higher or lower at any time. (2) Investment Pathways — rejected because the client requires a more tailored solution than the four generic options, which do not reflect individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile. (3) VCTs and EIS — rejected as high-risk, illiquid and more suitable for sophisticated investors; inappropriate given the client's objectives. (4) Investment Bonds — rejected because objectives do not involve complex trust planning, IHT mitigation or income smoothing. (5) Cash Deposits and National Savings — rejected because long-term cash cannot keep pace with inflation and the client's objectives include medium-to-long-term growth. The research/optionsConsidered model also records: OPT-1 (leave pension untouched, rejected — risk level 6 does not match agreed Cautious Balanced profile of 4); OPT-2 (in-situ fund switch, recommended); OPT-3 (consolidate into existing); OPT-4 (employer scheme); OPT-5 (set up new plan) — OPT-3 through OPT-5 are listed without individual rejection reasons. OPT-6 retains current Vanguard funds and OPT-7 retains Moneybox Cash ISA (rejected on performance grounds). The existing pension risk is described in the options section as risk level 6 vs agreed profile of 4, but the Alternative Solutions section of the report characterises the same Vanguard funds as risk levels 5 and 7 (blended 6 overall).
    File:     The Defaqto Pension Switching research report (G) confirms agreed risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) and an investment of £79,092 into Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II at 0.41% AMC. A data comparison table in the Personal Pension Plan research report [P11] shows four Risk-4 funds compared head-to-head: Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II (5yr 36.21%, 10yr 70.66%, volatility 6.46/6.76, AMC 0.41%), CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc (5yr 31.28%, volatility 6.9, AMC 0.29%), HSBC Global Strategy Conservative Portfolio AC (5yr 25.90%, volatility 6.38, AMC 0.10%), Vanguard LifeStrategy 40% Equity Fund A Acc (5yr 21.49%, 10yr 66.95%, volatility 7.25/6.55). The pension switch comparison [P2, P3, P4] shows the new solution projected values at age 60 are £100 lower than the existing pension at mid and high growth rates (£86,700 vs £86,800; £97,300 vs £97,400), with the new solution's reduction in yield (0.7%) being marginally higher than the existing (0.6% at mid growth). The switch report [P4] records an immediate fund value impact of £0 (no switching cost). No justification text is recorded in the product selection section [P9]: 'No additional justification text has been entered'. Platform selection [P14] likewise records 'No additional justification text has been entered'. OPT-3, OPT-4 and OPT-5 from the options considered model are listed without rejection reasons. The search Q6.4.6 returned nothing.
    Discrepancies:
      - OPT-3 (consolidate into existing pension), OPT-4 (employer scheme) and OPT-5 (set up new pension) are listed in the report's options section but carry no rejection reasons in either the suitability report or the supporting research file.
      - The pension switch comparison shows the new solution projects £100 less than the existing pension at both mid and high growth rates (£86,700 vs £86,800 mid; £97,300 vs £97,400 high) and carries a marginally higher reduction in yield (0.7% vs 0.6% at mid growth), yet the report does not explicitly acknowledge that the recommended solution produces a lower projected maturity value than the existing arrangement.
      - The existing pension risk level is described as 6 overall in the options section but as two separate funds at risk levels 5 and 7 in the Alternative Solutions section — the report uses both characterisations without reconciling them.
      - The research product selection and platform selection sections both record 'No additional justification text has been entered', meaning no written rationale for provider/product choice was captured in the research tool beyond the filter/ranking outputs.
      - The report's rejection of the Vanguard funds in the Alternative Solutions section states risk mismatch against the client's 'risk profile' without explicitly naming the agreed level 4 (Cautious Balanced), whereas the options section does name level 4 explicitly — creating an inconsistency in specificity.
      - The fund comparison in [P11] shows HSBC Global Strategy Conservative Portfolio AC at 0.10% AMC and CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc at 0.29% AMC, both at risk level 4, yet both are cheaper than the recommended Aviva fund at 0.41%; the report does not address why the lower-cost alternatives were rejected in favour of the higher-cost recommended fund beyond the performance ranking.
    The report presents a substantive set of alternatives with client-specific rejection reasons for the principal alternatives (Vanguard retention, Investment Pathways, VCTs/EIS, Investment Bonds, Cash). These reasons link to the client's agreed risk profile and stated objectives and are broadly corroborated by the research evidence. However, three material concerns arise.
    
    First, OPT-3 (consolidate into existing), OPT-4 (employer scheme) and OPT-5 (set up new plan) appear in the options considered section without any rejection reasons. The guard notes that options listed without individual rejection reasons are a partial answer. None of these three options is addressed in the Alternative Solutions section of the suitability report either. This is a missing component in the alternatives presentation.
    
    Second, and more significantly, the pension switch comparison [P2, P3, P4] shows that the recommended new solution produces a projected maturity value £100 lower than the existing pension at both mid and high growth rates, and carries a marginally higher reduction in yield (0.7% vs 0.6% at mid). The decision logic states: 'A ranked comparison placing the recommendation below the existing arrangement is the finding, whether or not the report mentions the ranking.' The report does not acknowledge this ranking or explain why accepting a marginally worse projected outcome is nonetheless suitable — the rationale rests on risk alignment rather than on explicitly conceding and justifying the performance trade-off. The charge increase is likewise not foregrounded in the alternatives analysis (new plan 0.62% vs existing 0.54%, +£63/year). The report's assertion that the new solution is superior does not engage with the projection table showing the contrary on a like-for-like basis.
    
    Third, the fund comparison table [P11] places the recommended Aviva fund as the highest performer over 5 and 10 years, which supports the recommendation on performance grounds, but two cheaper risk-4 alternatives (CT Universal MAP Cautious at 0.29%, HSBC at 0.10%) are not addressed in the narrative rationale beyond the ranking. The report does not explain why cost was not the primary consideration or why performance justified the higher charge — this is weak justification rather than missing evidence, as the ranking data is present.
    
    The internal inconsistency between the risk level described as '6' (OPT-1) and 'levels 5 and 7' (Alternative Solutions) is noted but does not materially change the rejection rationale, since both characterisations lead to the same conclusion of risk misalignment with the agreed level-4 profile.
    
    The vulnerability overlay does not affect this assessment: no vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the file that would require a different alternative or a modified presentation of alternatives.
    
    Overall: three missing rejection reasons for listed options, a projected maturity value table that places the new solution below the existing arrangement without explicit acknowledgment or client-specific justification in the report, and a higher-cost recommendation without reasoned rejection of cheaper alternatives — these together constitute material concerns warranting a Potential Concern finding at Moderate severity.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The single existing arrangement is Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd - Pension Portfolio (SIPP), policy AV2075926. The recommended action is RetainAndSwitchFunds. The rationale given comprises ten client-specific points: (1) Lower Costs — platform charge reduced from 0.32% to 0.21%; (2) Risk Alignment — current fund risk level 6 is too high for agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4, switching to recommended fund corrects this; (3) Professional Oversight — access to ongoing fund monitoring and portfolio review; (4) Adviser Charging Facilitation — platform allows adviser charges deducted directly; (5) Tax-Efficient Contribution — £3,600 gross contribution utilises annual allowance and benefits from 20% tax relief; (6) Platform Integration — pension, ISA and GIA on same Aviva Platform for administrative convenience; (7) Platform Suitability — Aviva Platform described as excellent with comprehensive investment choices; (8) Critical Yield — recommended plan needs to grow at 2.94% per year (mid-growth rate) up to age 60 to match existing plan value; and (9)–(10) further rationale points from the truncated canonical extract. All rationale items are marked isClientSpecific: true and linked to objective IDs (OBJ1, OBJ4, OBJ5).
    File:     Policy research (P1–P5) confirms the existing plan is Aviva policy AV2075926, fund value £79,092.32, transfer value £79,092.32, no exit penalties, no guaranteed annuity rates, no GMP, no with-profits, Origo transfer available, adviser charging can be applied. The pension switch report (P6) confirms the existing plan platform fee is 0.32% and the projected maturity values at mid-growth rate of 2.94% show £86,800 for the existing plan. It also confirms the new solution is Aviva Platform Pension Portfolio Choice with no platform labelled. The investment research report (P19, P20) confirms that the recommended fund for the pension is the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II at risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced), weighted AMC 0.41%, 100% allocation. The personal pension plan review report (P17) records the existing holdings at 10-year volatility risk level 6 (Balanced Growth). The transaction history (P11–P15) confirms the existing funds are Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80%, consistent with the report's description of risk level 6 holdings. No evidence in the file contradicts the recommended action of RetainAndSwitchFunds.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the existing fund risk level is 6 in the rationale ('current funds have a risk level of 6'), but the internal inconsistency noted in the extraction identifies that the same funds are described elsewhere in the report as having individual risk levels of 5 (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60%) and 7 (Vanguard LifeStrategy 80%), with 6 being a blended figure — P17 records the existing holdings at risk level 6 (Balanced Growth), which is consistent with the blended figure used in the rationale, so there is no contradiction with the external evidence, but the report uses two different characterisations of the same risk internally.
      - The report asserts 'Lower Costs' as a rationale citing a platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%, but the derived figures and switch charges table confirm the total ongoing charge rises from 0.54% to 0.62% (an increase of 0.08% / £63 per annum) when fund charges are included. The report does not disclose the net cost increase in this rationale point, presenting a partial picture that focuses only on the platform charge reduction while omitting the fund charge increase.
      - The report's critical yield rationale states the recommended plan needs to grow at 2.94% per year to match the existing plan — P6 confirms the mid-growth rate used is 2.94% and the maturity value at that rate for the existing plan is £86,800, which is consistent — but the critical yield comparison is described as being before the effect of any advice charges, which the report does not make clear in the rationale text.
      - No alternative providers or platforms are identified in the external evidence as having been formally compared and rejected; the policy research file (P24) contains an 'Alternative Strategies' section in the file strategy template but its content is blank/empty.
    The existing arrangement (Aviva SIPP AV2075926) has a clearly stated recommended outcome (RetainAndSwitchFunds) and a multi-point rationale. The majority of the rationale points are corroborated by the case file: the platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21% is confirmed by P6 and P21; the risk misalignment (existing risk 6 vs agreed profile 4) is corroborated by P17 and P20; the investment selection at risk level 4 is corroborated by P20; the pension switch comparison at 2.94% mid-growth rate is corroborated by P6. The arrangement's material features (no exit penalties, no GAR, no GMP, Origo-transferable) are confirmed by P1–P5 and P8–P9, supporting the absence of any retention reason that would override the switch recommendation. However, two concerns arise. First, the 'Lower Costs' rationale is materially misleading as presented: the report highlights only the platform charge reduction (0.32% to 0.21%) without disclosing that the total ongoing charge increases by 0.08% (from 0.54% to 0.62%), adding approximately £63 per year. This is not a false-positive; the derived figures confirm the net cost position is adverse, and stating 'Lower Costs' without qualification misrepresents the true cost impact. This is a weak justification at minimum and arguably a disclosure shortfall. Second, the alternative strategies section of the policy research file (P24) is blank — no alternatives are documented as having been considered and rejected in the external evidence, though the report's own text references alternatives. The risk characterisation discrepancy (6 blended vs 5 and 7 individual) is explained by P17 confirming 6 as the recorded blended level, so the rationale's use of 6 is not contradicted by external evidence. The vulnerability overlay does not materially affect this specific requirement based on the evidence available. The 'Lower Costs' assertion contradicted by the net charge increase is the primary concern.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Five recommendations are made, all with adviceStatus 'Recommended': (1) Add TFAS as servicing adviser and reduce platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%; (2) Switch pension investments from Vanguard LifeStrategy 60%/80% to Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II; (3) Make a lump sum pension contribution of £2,880 net / £3,600 gross in 2025/26; (4) Open Aviva Platform GIA and invest £57,120 from inheritance in CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc; (5) Transfer £20,000 Cash ISA to Aviva Platform Stocks & Shares ISA in CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc. Each carries a stated intended outcome. Implementation timescale is 10–15 working days. The responsibleParty for implementation is recorded as 'NotStated' for all five recommendations, and the field willAdviserImplement is absent from the extracted model (confirmed as NotPresentInDocument by the extraction report).
    File:     The Client Agreement to Proceed [P14] confirms that 'we arrange and oversee on your behalf' investments and that fees are set out in the suitability report, implying adviser-led implementation, but it does not name who will implement each specific recommendation. No passage in the file explicitly states, recommendation by recommendation, whether the adviser will arrange and implement the change or whether the client is expected to act. The Aviva product key features documents [P4, P7] and illustration [P5, P6] are product-level documents that do not address implementation responsibility at the recommendation level.
    Discrepancies:
      - For all five recommendations, the responsibleParty is recorded as 'NotStated' — no recommendation states explicitly who will implement it (adviser, client, or provider).
      - The field willAdviserImplement is absent from the report entirely (confirmed NotPresentInDocument), meaning the report does not state whether the adviser will arrange or implement any of the five changes.
      - The Client Agreement [P14] states the firm arranges and oversees investments generally but this is generic language and does not resolve the per-recommendation implementation responsibility.
    All five recommendations are clearly labelled 'Recommended' and each carries a stated intended outcome — those elements of the requirement are met. The intended outcomes are client-specific and linked to named objectives (cost reduction, risk alignment, tax efficiency, growth, ISA wrapper benefit). The implementation timescale of 10–15 working days is stated. However, the requirement under G6.6 also asks that the report states who will implement the change. For all five recommendations, responsibleParty is 'NotStated' and willAdviserImplement is absent from the document (the extraction report confirms NotPresentInDocument, so this is report silence, not extraction failure). The Client Agreement [P14] contains a general statement that the firm 'arrange[s] and oversee[s]' investments, but this is generic contract language rather than a per-recommendation implementation statement in the suitability report itself. The guard notes that a step handled by the provider rather than the adviser is still a stated step and the finding is silence, not who happens to act — this reinforces that silence is the issue here. The Bed & ISA next step recommends annual action at review but again does not state who will execute it. The omission of a clear per-recommendation statement of who will implement each change is a missing component in the suitability report. Given that all five recommendations are affected and the omission is consistent and confirmed as report silence, this is a moderate concern — rationale and intended outcomes are present and personalised, but the implementation-responsibility element is systematically absent.

  [G6.7] Recommendations that present multiple possible outcomes without stating which is recommended and why
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report presents five options for the pension: Option 1 (leave untouched, not recommended — reason: risk level 6 does not match agreed profile of 4), Option 2 (leave scheme, switch funds — recommended — reason: Aviva Platform is excellent with comprehensive investment choices; switching funds reduces risk to match agreed profile while retaining platform benefits), Option 3 (switch into another existing arrangement — not recommended, no reason given), Option 4 (switch into employer arrangement — not recommended, no reason given), Option 5 (set up new pension — not recommended, no reason given). The report records presentsUnresolvedOptions as false. All five recommendations (adviser agency terms, fund switch, pension contribution, GIA, ISA transfer) carry adviceStatus 'Recommended' with individual rationales. No recommendation presents an unresolved fork.
    File:     The case file (adviser notes [P1], [P9]) confirms the client's objectives: reviewing the existing Aviva pension for suitability, investing inheritance tax-efficiently, and retiring by 60. Risk profile research [P10] and [P7] confirm agreed risk level 4 Cautious Balanced. GIA research [P6] confirms CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund C Acc at risk level 4 selected for the GIA. Pension switching research [P10] confirms the risk-rated fund selection for the pension at risk level 4. No file note resolves Options 3, 4 or 5 beyond the report's own text — but no file evidence contradicts the report's designation of Option 2 as the recommended choice. The notes [P1] and [P9] do not record client deliberation between these pension options.
    Discrepancies:
      - Options 3, 4 and 5 are listed as not recommended but carry no reason given in the canonical model — the report names no justification for rejecting these three alternatives beyond designating Option 2 as recommended.
      - The reason given for rejecting Option 1 (risk mismatch) is client-specific, but the reasons for rejecting Options 3, 4 and 5 are absent.
    The check method is PresenceOnly: the question is whether any recommendation presents multiple possible outcomes without stating which is recommended and why. Option 2 is clearly identified as the recommended option and carries a reason. Option 1 is rejected with a stated, client-specific reason (risk level mismatch). Options 3, 4 and 5 are listed but carry no description and no reason for rejection. However, the guard states that setting out options and naming one as recommended is good practice, not a finding — the finding is an option left unresolved. Here, none of the five options is left open or unresolved: Option 2 is unambiguously recommended, and Options 1, 3, 4 and 5 are all marked as not recommended. The practical effect is that the client is not left facing an unresolved choice. That said, Options 3, 4 and 5 have no rejection rationale whatsoever — not even a brief explanation. This is a weak justification: the alternatives are listed without reasons, leaving the client unable to understand why those paths were not taken. Under the severity guidance this is a Low concern (alternative listed with brief rather than reasoned rejection — here not even a brief reason). The vulnerability overlay does not materially affect this finding: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would elevate the concern about absent rejection rationale. The report does not present any unresolved multi-outcome recommendation; it does present three alternatives with no rejection reasoning.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report states a timescale of '10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes' for fund access. Next steps include: (1) an Expression of Wish form has been posted to the client for signature and return; (2) the Bed & ISA facility action is recommended to be completed at each annual review. Five recommendations are recorded with actions: Other, Switch, IncreaseContribution, NewPlan, Transfer. The responsible party for all five recommendations is recorded as 'NotStated'.
    File:     The retrieved passages are from provider key features documents (Aviva Pension KFD, GIA KFD) and research/cashflow documents. The Aviva Pension KFD [P4] notes that delays in payments, transfers and switching out of funds may occur. The GIA KFD [P5] notes the client should retain an adviser to make certain changes. No passage from the case file independently addresses the who/when/by-whom question for the specific recommendations made. The timescale of 10-15 working days is a quote from the suitability report itself and is consistent with a provider service standard as described in the KFDs (which reference delays but do not contradict the stated timescale).
    Discrepancies:
      - The responsible party for all five recommendations is recorded as 'NotStated' — the report does not name who will implement any of the five recommendations (Switch, NewPlan, Transfer, IncreaseContribution, Other).
      - The Bed & ISA next step says 'I recommend that this action is completed each year review' without stating who will arrange or implement it — the client, the adviser, or Aviva.
      - The Expression of Wish next step states the form has been posted to the client for return, but does not confirm who will lodge it with Aviva on receipt.
      - No timescale is given for implementation of the Switch, NewPlan, Transfer or IncreaseContribution recommendations — the 10-15 working days figure relates to fund access after setup, not to the implementation process itself.
    The guard states that naming the adviser firm rather than an individual satisfies 'by whom', and that a timescale quoted as a provider service standard is a stated timescale. The 10-15 working days figure satisfies the timescale guard for the out-of-market/access element. However, the responsible party is 'NotStated' across all five recommendations. For routine transactions this might not be material, but this case involves a pension switch (EA1 to new Aviva plan), a new ISA, a new GIA, a pension contribution increase and a transfer — several of these carry suitability-bearing implementation steps. In particular: (a) the pension switch requires the adviser to submit the transfer authority and manage the process; (b) the Bed & ISA is an annual recurring action where it is material to know whether the adviser will action it or the client must instruct; (c) the pension contribution relies on the client having sufficient earnings, and no party is identified to confirm or verify this before submission. The omission of responsible party across all recommendations, and the absence of an implementation timescale for the recommendations themselves (as distinct from post-setup fund access), is a process gap. It is not high-severity because the report does provide some next-steps narrative and the timescale for fund access is present, but the systematic 'NotStated' on responsible party across five recommendations, including a switch and transfer, constitutes a process risk and a missing component of the required disclosure.

  [G6.9] Ancillary recommendations — beneficiary nomination, Power of Attorney, will review — are stated with an owner and are consistent with what the file records
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report makes two ancillary next-step recommendations: (1) An Expression of Wish form has been posted to the client for signature and return regarding beneficiary nomination on the Aviva plan — the adviser is acting as intermediary (posted the form, provided pre-paid envelope). (2) A Bed & ISA facility action is recommended to be completed at each annual review. Both clients are recorded as having a will (hasWill: true for both) and having granted Power of Attorney (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: true for both). The responsible party for all recommendations is recorded as 'NotStated' across all five recommendation actions. The report does not contain a will review recommendation or a POA recommendation — it merely records their existence. No assessment of whether the will is up to date is present in the extracted model (path /parties/clients[]/estatePlanning/isWillUpToDate absent).
    File:     The fact find [P8] records the advice area as 'Retirement Planning: Yes' and 'Estate Planning: [blank]' — estate planning was not an advice area selected at the meeting. The fact find [P6] and other passages do not record will status, POA status, or any estate planning details. No passage in the file corroborates that the client holds a will, has granted POA, or that these were discussed. The Expression of Wish form posting is an assertion in the suitability report itself (category I) and is not corroborated by any supporting document in the file. No file passage records whether a beneficiary is or is not currently nominated on the Aviva plan.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts both clients have wills (hasWill: true) and have granted POA (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: true), but no file passage corroborates either assertion — the fact find estate planning section is blank and estate planning was not recorded as an advice area.
      - The responsible party for the Expression of Wish recommendation is recorded as 'NotStated' in the canonical model, yet the report text implies the adviser has already taken action (posted the form) — the owner of the outstanding action (client to sign and return) is implicit rather than explicitly stated.
      - The report is silent on whether the will is up to date (/parties/clients[]/estatePlanning/isWillUpToDate absent) despite recording that wills exist.
      - No file evidence corroborates the Expression of Wish action having been taken or that no beneficiary is currently nominated on the Aviva plan.
    The check method is PresenceOnly. The report makes one active ancillary recommendation: the Expression of Wish form. This is client-specific and the report explains the adviser has posted the form with a pre-paid envelope, making the outstanding action the client's (sign and return). The owner is implicit rather than explicitly named, but the action and its mechanism are clear enough that this does not rise to a material gap on its own. The Bed & ISA recommendation is linked to annual review and is implementation-oriented rather than estate-planning ancillary. Regarding will and POA: the report records both as in place but makes no recommendation about them, which is consistent with the fact that estate planning was not selected as an advice area in the fact find [P8]. The guards note that an assertion the client already has a will or POA, where the file does not record it, is a gap. Here, the fact find estate planning section is blank, and no passage corroborates the will or POA assertions. However, the adviser may have obtained this information verbally during the telephone meeting; the absence from the fact find is a data quality concern but is not a direct contradiction. The more material gap is that the report asserts POA is granted (true for both clients) and wills exist, with no file corroboration whatsoever — these are unsupported assertions. The will-up-to-date question is absent entirely. Given estate planning was not an advice area and no recommendation is made about will review or POA, the ancillary recommendation requirement is substantially met for the Expression of Wish. The unsupported will/POA status assertions represent a data quality gap but do not undermine the active recommendation. No vulnerability factors in the file materially affect this ancillary recommendation assessment.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). The report's GIA illustration input is stated as £57,100.00 in [P17] ('The payments into your plan £57,100.00') but the report states the GIA investment is £57,120 — a £20 discrepancy between the illustration and the report's stated investment amount. The derived figures show the new pension annual charge of £512.69 at 0.62% implies a fund base of approximately £82,692 (post-contribution), whereas the existing plan £427 at 0.54% uses £79,092 as base. The report's switch charges table uses a like-for-like basis of £79,092 to derive +£63 difference, but the annual recurring charges table uses the post-contribution base for the new plan monetary figures. This inconsistency in base values across tables is not explained in the report. The report asserts transaction costs are 'already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges' — [P3] KIID for CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc states the ongoing charges figure 'excludes the costs of buying or selling assets for the Fund', which means transaction costs are not included in the OCF. The report's rationale that transaction costs are reflected in net performance is a separate point but the claim that they are 'accounted for' in disclosed charges is potentially misleading relative to the KIID disclosure. 8 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report discloses: (1) Pension: platform charge 0.21%/£173.65, investment management charge 0.41%/£339.04, total 0.62% — new plan more expensive than existing by +0.08%/+£63; existing plan 0.54%/£427. (2) ISA: platform 0.21%/£42, fund charge 0.29%/£58, total 0.50%. (3) GIA: platform 0.21%/£119.95, fund charge 0.29%/£165.65, total 0.50%. Initial advice fee taken from GIA. Ongoing adviser charge 1% across all plans. Transaction costs excluded with stated reasons — accounted for in net performance figures. Cumulative effect disclosed via reference to personal illustrations. DFM fee not applicable. An aggregated total is shown per product. The switch charges table notes the new pension is +0.08% more expensive and justifies the increase by reference to improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight.
    File:     [P3] CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc KIID shows ongoing charges of 0.29% and 0.00% entry/exit charges, consistent with the fund charge in the report. [P6] Pension projection (existing plan) shows Vanguard Lifestrategy 60%/80% weighted average OCF 0.22% and Aviva platform charge 0.32%, consistent with existing plan total of 0.54%. [P21] GIA illustration confirms CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc OCF/TER 0.29% and Aviva platform charge of 0.21% (inferred from illustration), and initial adviser charge of £4,794.37 from single payment and ongoing adviser charge of 1.00%. [P22] Pension illustration confirms adviser charges taken. [P17] GIA illustration shows projected values net of all charges across years 1–10, confirming cumulative charge impact is illustrated. [P20] ISA illustration shows projected values across three growth scenarios. [P1] Report itself states existing plan 0.54%/£427, new plan impact +0.08%/+£63, and with adviser fee total of approximately 1.62% vs existing 0.54%, requiring outperformance. [P5] Research report shows ongoing charges 0.29%/0.21%/0.22% across fund/platform/existing. [P9] Pension switch research report explains one-off, ongoing, transaction and incidental costs categories. [P11] Switch report confirms new pension platform flat rate 0.21%. [P19] GIA Key Features confirms Aviva annual management charge, fund management charges and DIM charge (only if applicable). No DFM/discretionary fee applies here. [P23] Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus II factsheet references charges per illustration — no standalone OCF figure confirmed for the insured fund. Illustration-based charge methodology noted.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report's GIA illustration input is stated as £57,100.00 in [P17] ('The payments into your plan £57,100.00') but the report states the GIA investment is £57,120 — a £20 discrepancy between the illustration and the report's stated investment amount.
      - The derived figures show the new pension annual charge of £512.69 at 0.62% implies a fund base of approximately £82,692 (post-contribution), whereas the existing plan £427 at 0.54% uses £79,092 as base. The report's switch charges table uses a like-for-like basis of £79,092 to derive +£63 difference, but the annual recurring charges table uses the post-contribution base for the new plan monetary figures. This inconsistency in base values across tables is not explained in the report.
      - The report asserts transaction costs are 'already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges' — [P3] KIID for CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc states the ongoing charges figure 'excludes the costs of buying or selling assets for the Fund', which means transaction costs are not included in the OCF. The report's rationale that transaction costs are reflected in net performance is a separate point but the claim that they are 'accounted for' in disclosed charges is potentially misleading relative to the KIID disclosure.
      - The Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II annual charge is stated in the report as 0.41%. [P23] factsheet references charges per illustration but does not independently confirm a 0.41% OCF/TER figure — this charge layer has no direct factsheet or KIID corroboration in the pack beyond the report itself and the illustration.
      - No standalone illustration for the pension has been cited showing all charges including adviser fee on the new pension plan in a single cumulative projection table — [P22] references adviser charges but the full cumulative projection content is truncated and not confirmed in the retrieved passages.
    The report discloses all principal cost layers: platform, investment management, initial advice fee, ongoing adviser charge, and transaction cost exclusion with reasons. The disclosures are broadly consistent with the evidence. The CT Universal MAP Cautious fund OCF of 0.29% is confirmed by [P3] and [P21]. The existing plan charges (0.32% platform + 0.22% fund = 0.54%) are confirmed by [P6]. The GIA platform rate of 0.21% and adviser charges are confirmed by [P21]. The pension switch report at [P11] confirms the 0.21% platform flat rate for the new pension. The switch charges analysis in [P1] is internally consistent and the justification for the higher cost (risk alignment, professional oversight) is stated.
    
    However, several concerns arise. First, the GIA illustration in [P17] uses £57,100 as the payment amount while the report states £57,120 — a £20 discrepancy. This is a minor arithmetic inconsistency but is a real difference between the report and the illustration. The illustration difference caveat in the report partially guards this, but a £20 difference in the invested amount itself (not just the charge calculation method) is outside that guard's scope.
    
    Second, the inconsistent fund base used in the switch charges table (£79,092 for like-for-like) versus the annual recurring charges table (implied ~£82,692 post-contribution for new plan monetary figures) is not explained. While the extraction flagged this as an ambiguity and the percentage figures are internally consistent, the monetary comparison across tables uses different bases without disclosure, which could mislead a reader comparing £427 to £512.69 as if they were on the same base.
    
    Third, the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II 0.41% charge has no independent factsheet or KIID corroboration in the retrieved evidence. [P23] is the factsheet for this fund but only references charges per the illustration, not a standalone OCF figure. This means the key fund charge for the recommended pension rests on the report's own assertion and the pension illustration (partially retrieved), without a KIID or factsheet confirmation in the pack.
    
    Fourth, the transaction cost rationale — that costs are 'accounted for in the fund's performance figures' — is not precisely supported by [P3], which explicitly states the OCF 'excludes the costs of buying or selling assets for the Fund'. The report's explanation is not technically inaccurate (transaction costs do affect net returns) but it is potentially imprecise as a disclosure rationale.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability indicators are identified in the canonical model that would change the materiality of cost disclosure in this case.
    
    Overall, the material cost layers are disclosed and largely corroborated, but the absence of independent corroboration for the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II 0.41% charge, the base inconsistency in monetary comparisons, and the £20 illustration discrepancy constitute potential concerns rather than a clean pass.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report asserts an initial advice fee of 3% of total amount invested, equating to £4,794 based on total investment values of £79,092 (pension transfer) + £57,120 (GIA) + £20,000 (ISA) + £3,600 (pension contribution) = £159,812. This fee is to be paid from the GIA funds. An ongoing adviser fee of 1% per annum across all Aviva Platform investments is stated, equalling approximately £1,558 for the upcoming year based on a total platform value of £155,812 after initial fee deduction. The report states the initial fee is 'as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement'.
    File:     The Client Agreement To Proceed [P19] confirms: 'Initial Service - There will be a charge of 3% of the value of the funds that we advise on with a minimum of £1,350 fee. Based on the value of your funds, our fee will be £4,794.' It also confirms: 'Ongoing Services - For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), which is automatically taken from the funds invested.' The IMPORT_2.md document [P1] states the basic advice charge is 3% for investments and pensions. The GIA illustration [P4] confirms the initial adviser charge as £4,794.37 from the single payments and the ongoing adviser charge as 1.00% per year. The ISA illustration [P16] corroborates a 1% ongoing charge and confirms £20,000 investment. The pension illustration [P5] confirms adviser charges are present. The client agreement [P19] states the monetary initial fee as £4,794.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report calculates the initial fee basis as £79,092 + £57,120 + £20,000 + £3,600 = £159,812, but 3% of £159,812 = £4,794.36, which rounds to £4,794 — arithmetically consistent.
      - The GIA illustration [P4] states the initial adviser charge as £4,794.37, while the report states £4,794 — a rounding difference of £0.37.
      - The report states the ongoing fee is approximately £1,558 based on a total platform value of £155,812 after initial fee deduction. £155,812 = £159,812 - £4,000 (approximate initial fee deducted from platform), but 3% of £159,812 = £4,794, so post-fee platform value should be £159,812 - £4,794 = £155,018, not £155,812. The report's stated base of £155,812 implies a deduction of only £4,000 rather than £4,794, which is arithmetically inconsistent. However, the report states the initial fee is paid from GIA funds specifically, so the ongoing fee base may reflect: pension £82,692 (post-contribution) + ISA £20,000 + GIA £57,120 = £159,812 gross, less initial fee of £4,000 = £155,812 — but the fee was stated as £4,794, not £4,000.
      - 1% of £155,812 = £1,558.12, which rounds to £1,558 — so the ongoing fee monetary amount is internally consistent with the stated base of £155,812, but the base itself appears to use a deduction of £4,000 rather than the stated £4,794 initial fee.
      - The report states the initial fee is paid 'from the GIA funds' — the GIA investment is £57,120, and the initial fee is £4,794, leaving £52,326 net invested in the GIA. The GIA illustration [P4] shows an initial adviser charge of £4,794.37 from the single payments, which is consistent with the GIA funding the fee, but the GIA illustration's fund value basis and the reported £57,120 GIA value were not directly reconciled in the illustrations provided.
      - £155,812 as a figure is not corroborated by any document in the pack.
      - £1,558 as a monetary ongoing fee figure is not corroborated by any document in the pack.
    The key comparison is between the report's stated adviser fees and the client agreement. The 3% initial fee rate matches the client agreement [P19] exactly. The £4,794 monetary amount matches the client agreement [P19] exactly and is corroborated by the GIA illustration [P4] (£4,794.37 — a £0.37 rounding difference only). The 1% ongoing fee rate matches the client agreement [P19] exactly and is corroborated by the GIA illustration [P4] and ISA illustration [P16]. These are clean matches within acceptable rounding tolerances. The more significant issue is the ongoing fee monetary amount of £1,558 and its stated basis of £155,812. The report states the initial fee is £4,794, but the post-fee platform value of £155,812 implies a deduction of £4,000 (£159,812 - £155,812 = £4,000), not the stated £4,794. This is an internal arithmetic inconsistency: the ongoing fee base does not properly subtract the stated initial fee. 1% of £155,812 = £1,558.12, so if the base were accepted, the £1,558 figure is consistent with it. But the base itself is wrong by approximately £794. The correct post-fee base should be £159,812 - £4,794 = £155,018, yielding an ongoing fee of approximately £1,550 — a difference of approximately £8 per annum. This is a minor arithmetic discrepancy in the ongoing fee base, not a breach of the client agreement rate. The rates themselves are confirmed by the client agreement and the illustrations. The GIA illustration [P4] and client agreement [P19] both independently corroborate the £4,794 initial fee and 1% ongoing rate. Neither £155,812 nor £1,558 is corroborated by any document; these are internal report assertions. The discrepancy in the post-fee base (£155,812 vs the arithmetically correct £155,018) is a data inconsistency but of low monetary impact (~£8/year). The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment. The overall finding is that the rates match the client agreement perfectly, the initial fee monetary amount matches both the agreement and the GIA illustration, but the ongoing fee base contains a minor internal arithmetic error in the deduction applied.

      - Low growth — report states existing RIY 1.6%; switch report records 0.6% — a difference of 1.0 percentage points.

      - Low growth — report states critical yield 0.0%; switch report records 2.9%.
      - Low growth — report states effect of switching £0 / 0.0% decrease; switch report records £100 / 0.1% decrease.
      - Mid growth — report states existing RIY 1.7%; switch report records 0.6% — a difference of 1.1 percentage points.
      - Mid growth — report states recommended RIY 1.7%; switch report records 0.7% — a difference of 1.0 percentage points.
      - Mid growth — report states effect of switching £0 / 0.0% decrease; switch report records £100 / 0.1% decrease.
      - High growth — report states existing RIY 1.8%; switch report records 0.7% — a difference of 1.1 percentage points.
      - High growth — report states recommended RIY 1.8%; switch report records 0.7% — a difference of 1.1 percentage points.
      - High growth — report states effect of switching £0 / 0.0% decrease; switch report records £100 / 0.1% decrease.
      - The Aviva pension illustration states the all-charges reduction in growth rate is 1.9%, which aligns more closely with the report's figure of 1.7%–1.8% than with the switch report's 0.6%–0.7%, suggesting the report may have taken RIY figures from the illustration rather than the switch report — but the illustration is on a different basis (real-terms at 2% inflation) and is not a direct comparator.
      - The report states critical yield at low growth is 0.0%; the switch report states 2.9% at low growth — a material contradiction.
      - The report states effect of switching as £0 at all growth rates; the switch report records £100 decrease at mid and high growth rates.
    The suitability report's RIY figures (1.6%, 1.7%, 1.8% across low/mid/high) are materially inconsistent with those in the pension switch report (0.6%/0.6%/0.7% for existing; 0.7%/0.7%/0.7% for new solution). The switch report is the primary evidential document for pension switching analysis and is the authoritative source for these figures. The report's figures are closer to the Aviva pension illustration's all-charges reduction of 1.9% [P11], but that illustration is expressed in real terms (inflation-adjusted at 2%) and is therefore not directly comparable to the nominal RIY figures in the switch report — the guard regarding real vs nominal bases is relevant here as a partial explanation for the difference in the recommended-plan figures, but it does not explain the discrepancy on the existing-plan RIY (0.6% vs 1.6%), which has no inflation adjustment applied to it. More critically, the report states a critical yield at low growth of 0.0% whereas the switch report shows 2.9% at low growth. This is a direct contradiction on a material figure: a 0.0% critical yield implies the new solution needs no growth at all to match the existing plan, whereas 2.9% implies meaningful outperformance is required. The report's statement that the effect of switching is £0 / 0.0% at all growth rates is also contradicted by the switch report's £100 / 0.1% decrease at mid and high growth rates — a small monetary difference but indicative of the report presenting a more favourable picture than the switch analysis supports. The low-growth critical yield discrepancy (0.0% vs 2.9%) is the most serious finding: it overstates the value proposition of the switch to the client. There is no guard that explains this contradiction. The vulnerability overlay does not materially alter this finding — no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the file that would change the analysis.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report presents a 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' comparing the existing Aviva pension (AV2075926-001) against the recommended arrangement on a like-for-like basis. Existing plan: 0.54% / £427 pa. Recommended plan: 0.62% (0.21% platform + 0.41% fund). Difference: +0.08% / +£63 pa. The report states the comparison is based on the current fund value of the existing plan (implying a base of ~£79,092). Including the 1% adviser fee, the total cost rises to ~1.62% pa versus 0.54% currently. The report acknowledges the new plan is more expensive but justifies this by improved risk alignment, lower platform charge (0.32% → 0.21%), and ongoing professional oversight. The monetary figures for the new plan charges are stated in the Annual Recurring Charges table as £173.65 (platform, 0.21%) and £339.04 (fund, 0.41%), totalling £512.69 at 0.62%. The switch-charges table records the impact as +£63 based on the existing plan value base.
    File:     The policy research document [P4] records the existing fund charge as 0.0022 (i.e. 0.22%), consistent with the report. The pension switch research report [P13] contains a 'New solution charges comparison' section. The personal pension plan review report [P23] records the existing funds (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80%) with total ongoing costs at 0.22%, consistent with the report's 0.22% weighted fund charge. The research report [P5] records ongoing charges for the recommended Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II at 0.29% (not 0.41% as stated in the suitability report and fund table [P2]). Passage [P15] records the AMC for Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II as 0.41%, which does match the report. The derived figures show: the £427 at 0.54% implies a fund base of ~£79,074, consistent with the stated fund value of £79,092. The £512.69 at 0.62% implies a base of ~£82,692, which is the post-contribution value (£79,092 + £3,600), not the pre-contribution existing plan value used for the comparison. The switch-charges table's +£63 at 0.08% implies a base of £78,750, which is slightly below both the pre-contribution fund value (£79,092) and the post-contribution value. No provider illustration is present in the evidence pack to independently verify the new plan's charges (extraction noted /solution/0/illustrationProvided as absent). The ongoing adviser charge of 1% is stated in [P11] as approximately £1,558 pa based on total platform value of £155,812 after initial fee deduction.
    Discrepancies:
      - The +£63 monetary impact at +0.08% implies a fund base of £78,750 (per derived figures), but the existing plan value stated is £79,092.32 — a difference of £342 in the base used, producing a minor arithmetic inconsistency in the switch-charges table.
      - The new plan's annual recurring charges (£512.69 at 0.62%) are calculated on an implied base of ~£82,692 (post-contribution), whereas the switch-charges table comparison uses the pre-contribution existing plan value (~£79,092) — the two monetary figures in the same document use different bases without disclosure.
      - Passage [P5] (research report) records the ongoing charge for the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II as 0.29%, but the suitability report [P2] and the fund research [P15] record it as 0.41% — these are different figures for the same fund's charge in the same evidence pack, creating an unresolved discrepancy between two supporting documents.
      - No provider illustration is present in the pack to independently corroborate the new plan's projected values or cumulative cost impact; the extraction confirmed /solution/0/illustrationProvided as absent from the document.
      - The existing plan charge is described as 0.32% platform + 0.22% fund = 0.54% in the Existing Arrangements table and as 0.54% / £427 in the switch-charges table — these are internally consistent but the 0.32% platform charge is not independently corroborated in any [E] or [G] document.
      - The report's claim that the ongoing adviser charge totals ~1.62% annually is derived by adding 1% to 0.62%, which is arithmetically correct, but the 0.62% base itself uses the post-contribution fund value while the like-for-like table uses the pre-contribution value — the percentage comparison is not performed on a consistent base.
    The cost comparison is present and the report correctly identifies that the recommended arrangement is more expensive than the existing one (+0.08% / +£63 pa on a like-for-like basis excluding adviser fee). The internal arithmetic is broadly consistent: 0.54% of ~£79,092 = ~£427 and 0.32% + 0.22% = 0.54%. However, three concerns arise. First, the derived figures show that the +£63 at +0.08% implies a base of £78,750, not £79,092 — a minor base inconsistency, likely rounding, but technically the comparison is not performed on the fund value the report claims. This is a low-severity rounding issue. Second, and more significantly, the new plan's monetary charge (£512.69 at 0.62%) is computed on an implied base of ~£82,692, which is the post-contribution value. The switch-charges comparison uses the pre-contribution value for the existing plan and the post-contribution value for the new plan. This means the monetary figures on each side of the comparison use different bases, making the monetary comparison not like-for-like even though the percentage comparison is. The report does not disclose this difference. Third, passage [P5] records the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II ongoing charge as 0.29%, whereas [P15] and the suitability report record it as 0.41%. This discrepancy between two [G]-category research documents is unresolved and, if the 0.29% figure were correct, the new plan's total charge would be 0.50% (0.21% + 0.29%) — lower than the existing 0.54%, not higher. This would change the direction of the comparison from 'more expensive' to 'cheaper', which is a material inconsistency. The 0.41% figure in [P15] is the AMC entry in the data comparison table which appears more authoritative than the 'Ongoing Charges' row in [P5] (which may represent a different charge component or data vintage), but the discrepancy is present and unresolved in the file. No provider illustration is available to independently verify the new plan's charges or projected outcomes. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the available passages that would materially change this assessment. The absence of a provider illustration means the cumulative cost impact stated in the report ('Your personal illustration contains further information regarding the cumulative effect the charges will have on your investment returns' [P19]) cannot be verified. The adviser fee at 1% pa is stated in [P11] and is internally consistent with the 1.62% total quoted in the report. The guard on different bases (with and without adviser fees) is satisfied — the report clearly distinguishes the like-for-like comparison from the including-adviser-fee comparison. The guard on recompute: the monetary base inconsistency between the switch table and the annual recurring charges table is a genuine finding, not a spurious mismatch.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report acknowledges the new pension arrangement costs more than the existing one: existing total 0.54% (£427), recommended total 0.62% (£512.69), a difference of +0.08% (£63 per annum). Including the ongoing adviser fee, the required outperformance is stated as 1.08% per annum, or £63 in the first year in monetary terms. The report states 'this small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight' and that 'based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 2.94% per year up to age 60 in order to meet the same value as your current plan', asserting this is achievable 'given the improved risk alignment and professional oversight' and 'based on the past performance of your recommended funds'. Benefits cited are: (1) Lower platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21% via agency terms; (2) Risk alignment — existing fund risk level 6 reduced to match agreed Cautious Balanced risk profile of 4; (3) Professional oversight via ongoing fund monitoring and portfolio review; (4) Tax-efficient contribution of £3,600 gross with 20% tax relief; (5) Platform integration — pension, ISA and GIA on same Aviva Platform. No provider illustration is recorded as having been provided (extraction flag: /solution/0/illustrationProvided NotPresentInDocument).
    File:     The pension switching research report [P7] and personal pension plan research report [P8/P11] confirm the recommended fund (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II) cumulative performance to 31 October 2025: 3M 5.58%, 6M 12.61%, 1YR 11.92%, 3YR 33.59%, 5YR 36.21%, 10YR 70.66%. The existing fund review report [P2/P9] confirms the combined existing strategy (Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80%) cumulative performance to 31 October 2025: Combined 3M 6.89%, 6M 16.25%, 1YR 15.91%, 3YR 42.07%, 5YR 58.01%, 10YR 136.32%. The Aviva fund factsheet [P12] confirms the fund's annual management charge of 0.41% total (0.30% AMC + 0.11% additional expenses), consistent with the report. The cashflow models [P23/P24] show the Aviva ISA and GIA using 0.5% ongoing platform/fund charges plus 1% ongoing financial planning fee but do not break out separate charge layers for the pension. No provider illustration for the recommended pension (showing projected values, RIY or cumulative cost impact) is present in the evidence pack. The derived figures confirm the £63 / 0.08% charge difference is arithmetically consistent with a fund value near £79,000. However, the required outperformance of 1.08% figure implies a fund base of only £5,833 (£63 ÷ 1.08%) which is arithmetically irreconcilable with the £79,092 pension fund — suggesting the 1.08% figure is not computed as (£63 / £79,092) but may refer to the additional outperformance needed to cover the full ongoing adviser charge layer, not just the £63 product cost differential.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts that the 1.08% required outperformance is achievable 'based on the past performance of your recommended funds', but the recommended fund (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II) has delivered 5-year cumulative performance of 36.21% whereas the existing combined strategy has delivered 58.01% over the same period — the recommended fund has materially underperformed the existing holding on every time horizon shown (1YR: 11.92% vs 15.91%; 3YR: 33.59% vs 42.07%; 5YR: 36.21% vs 58.01%; 10YR: 70.66% vs 136.32%), directly contradicting the report's claim that past performance of the recommended fund supports achievability of the outperformance requirement.
      - The required outperformance percentage of 1.08% is arithmetically inconsistent with the stated £63 annual cost difference and the £79,092 fund value (£63 / £79,092 = 0.0797%, not 1.08%). The derived figures confirm this: 1.08% at £63 implies a fund base of £5,833, not £79,092. The report does not explain what base or methodology produces 1.08%.
      - No provider illustration is present in the evidence pack (extraction flag confirms NotPresentInDocument), so projected values, RIY and cumulative cost impact over the term to age 60 cannot be verified against any illustration.
      - The report states the benefit of 'lower costs' via reduced platform charge from 0.32% to 0.21%, yet the overall charge increases from 0.54% to 0.62% (before adviser fee) because the new fund charge (0.41%) exceeds the existing weighted fund charges (0.22%). The 'lower costs' benefit statement is therefore misleading in the context of the all-in charge comparison.
      - The cashflow model inputs [P23] show the Aviva GIA at £52,326, whereas the suitability report and canonical model state the GIA investment is £57,120 — a difference of £4,794, equal to the initial adviser fee of £4,794.37. While this may represent post-deduction value, it creates an inconsistency in the charge base used across documents without explicit reconciliation in the report.
    The central concern is the report's assertion that the required outperformance is achievable 'based on the past performance of your recommended funds'. The performance evidence in the file directly contradicts this: on every time horizon from 1 year to 10 years, the recommended Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II has delivered lower returns than the existing combined Vanguard strategy (e.g., 5YR 36.21% vs 58.01%). The report does not acknowledge this underperformance. The report's achievability claim is therefore a bare assertion unsupported by — and contradicted by — the available performance data. This is a material weakness in the cost justification. The guard for this requirement explicitly states that an assertion that required outperformance 'is achievable' is a claim, not an analysis, and requires supporting performance data. The available performance data runs the other way. The 1.08% required outperformance figure is also arithmetically irreconcilable with the stated £63 cost difference and the £79,092 fund value. £63 / £79,092 = 0.0797%, not 1.08%. The report provides no explanation of the methodology behind 1.08%. This is either an error in the report or it relates to a different base (possibly the total adviser fee rather than the £63 product cost differential), but the report presents it without clarification, which is a data inconsistency. The 'lower costs' benefit statement is misleading: while the platform charge falls (0.32% to 0.21%), the net product charge increases (0.54% to 0.62%) due to the higher fund charge. Calling this a benefit of 'lower costs' without qualification understates the actual cost position. The absence of a provider illustration means projected values and RIY cannot be verified. The cashflow model GIA value discrepancy (£52,326 vs £57,120) is explained by the initial fee deduction of £4,794.37, which the cashflow model appears to have applied — this is noted as internally plausible but not explicitly reconciled. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence that would materially alter this finding beyond the standard cost transparency obligation.

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended pension plan costs 0.08% more per annum than the existing plan (excluding adviser fee), and that including the adviser fee the proposed plan will need to outperform the current plan by approximately 1.08% annually to match the same net growth. The required outperformance basis is stated as the additional annual cost vs the existing arrangement of 0.54%.
    File:     This requirement is assessed from the report against itself and against derived figures. No supporting documentary evidence was retrieved. The derived figures show: the 0.08% difference at £63.00 implies a fund base of £78,750.00, while the existing fund value cited elsewhere is approximately £79,092 (consistent within rounding). The required outperformance of 1.08% at £63.00 implies a fund value of £5,833.33 — which is arithmetically inconsistent with the pension fund value of ~£79,092 and the stated cost difference of £63. If the total additional annual cost including adviser fee were £63 (0.08%) plus an adviser fee, and the required outperformance is 1.08% of the fund, that would imply an adviser-fee-inclusive additional annual cost of approximately £855 on a £79,092 fund (1.08% × £79,092 ≈ £854.19). The report does not separately state the adviser ongoing fee amount in percentage terms for the pension, so the arithmetic of the 1.08% figure cannot be fully verified from the pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - The derived figure shows that 1.08% required outperformance on a fund of ~£79,092 implies an additional annual cost (including adviser fee) of approximately £854, but the report does not disclose an adviser ongoing charge percentage or monetary amount for the pension that would allow verification of this figure — the 1.08% is stated without a visible arithmetic trail.
      - The 0.08% cost difference at £63 implies a fund base of £78,750, while the existing fund value is stated as £79,092.32 elsewhere — a minor rounding discrepancy in the base used.
      - The new plan annual recurring charges table uses a higher implied base (~£82,692, post-contribution) for the £512.69 / 0.62% figure, while the switch charges comparison uses the pre-contribution base (~£79,092) for the £427 / 0.54% existing figure — the monetary difference of £63 is therefore not a like-for-like comparison on the same fund base, yet no adjustment is disclosed.
      - No adviser ongoing fee percentage or monetary amount is disclosed in the costs and charges section for the pension, making the jump from 0.08% to 1.08% unverifiable from the pack.
      - The report asserts that the recommended investments are expected to deliver better risk-adjusted outcomes and benefit from professional management and a broader fund range, but no quantified benefit or projected value comparison is provided to support the assertion that 1.08% annual outperformance is achievable or likely.
    The report discloses that the recommended pension costs 0.08% more than the existing plan on a like-for-like basis and that, including the adviser fee, the plan must outperform by 1.08% annually. However, the arithmetic bridge between 0.08% and 1.08% — the adviser ongoing charge — is not disclosed in the pack. On a fund of ~£79,092, 1.08% implies approximately £854 of additional annual cost including the adviser fee; the cost difference without the adviser fee is £63. This means the implied adviser fee component is approximately £791 per annum on the pension alone, but this is nowhere stated. This makes the 1.08% figure unverifiable and the disclosure incomplete.
    
    Additionally, the monetary comparison is not on a like-for-like fund base: the new plan charge of £512.69 is calculated on a post-contribution value (~£82,692), while the existing plan charge of £427 is on the pre-contribution value (~£79,092). The £63 monetary difference presented in the switch charges table corrects for this by using the pre-contribution base, but this is not explained in the report, meaning the comparison as presented is potentially misleading.
    
    The guard notes that higher cost is not automatically poor value and that benefits must be tested. The report does cite benefits — professional portfolio management, broader fund range, Aviva platform features, adviser ongoing service — but these are qualitative assertions with no quantified projected outcomes or illustrations to demonstrate that 1.08% annual outperformance is realistic. The provider illustration is noted as absent. Without it, or without an adviser fee disclosure, the value case is asserted but not evidenced.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: the extraction records no vulnerability characteristics for this client that would materially alter this analysis.
    
    The required outperformance figure of 1.08% is stated but arithmetically unverifiable from the pack, the monetary comparison uses inconsistent bases without explanation, and no illustration or quantified benefit projection supports the claim that the higher cost is justified. These constitute a disclosure shortfall and weak justification.

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model holds no value at the comparativeCost path. The suitability report does not explicitly state a comparative cost figure for alternatives considered. However, the report states that the pension switch is justified on the basis of: lower costs (platform charge reduced from 0.32% to 0.21%); risk alignment (existing fund risk level 6 reduced to risk level 4); professional oversight; adviser charging facilitation; tax-efficient contribution; and platform integration. The report acknowledges the new plan total ongoing charge (excluding adviser fee) is 0.62% versus the existing 0.54%, an increase of 0.08%, but justifies this by reference to risk alignment and other non-cost benefits. No explicit comparison of alternative solutions' costs against the recommended solution is stated in the suitability report itself.
    File:     The pension switch research report (P1) contains a 'New solution charges comparison' section that states: 'From those solutions which were assessed as being suitable for your needs, a selection was made which included a comparison of all costs including Product, Platform and Fund charges. The available solutions were ranked by Maturity Value along with a generic stakeholder pension and the existing pension. The top 10 results are shown below and are based on the medium growth rate.' The switch report (P3, P4) shows projected values and RIY figures for existing and new solution, with the effect of switching shown as £0 or 0.0% decrease at low and mid growth rates, and £100 or 0.1% decrease at high growth rate. The RIY for the new solution is 1.6%–1.8% versus 1.6%–1.7% for the existing. Critical yield at mid growth is 2.9%. The pension switch report therefore contains a comparative ranking by maturity value across up to 10 solutions. However, the actual top-10 comparison table content is truncated in P1 and the specific alternative solutions and their charges are not fully visible in the retrieved passages. The suitability report itself (P5) cites lower platform costs (0.32% to 0.21%) as a primary benefit, but acknowledges the total new plan charge (0.62%) exceeds the existing (0.54%), relying on non-cost benefits to justify the higher total cost.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report does not include any comparative cost table or figures for alternative solutions considered — the comparative cost research exists in the pension switch report (P1) but is not reproduced or summarised in the suitability report.
      - The report asserts 'Lower Costs' as a primary benefit of the switch (referencing the platform charge reduction from 0.32% to 0.21%), yet the total ongoing charge increases from 0.54% to 0.62% (+0.08%), which is not fully reconciled as a net cost reduction in the report.
      - No explicit statement in the suitability report identifies which alternatives were considered and why they were rejected in favour of the recommended solution on a cost or maturity value basis.
      - The pension switch report (P1) indicates solutions were ranked by maturity value and a top-10 comparison was performed, but this ranking and the specific alternatives are not referenced or summarised in the suitability report.
      - The effect of switching at high growth rate shows a £100 or 0.1% decrease in projected value (P4), meaning the new solution is projected to produce a marginally lower outcome than the existing arrangement at high growth — this is not disclosed or discussed in the suitability report.
    The pension switch research report (P1) confirms that a comparative ranking of solutions by maturity value was performed, including product, platform and fund charges across up to 10 solutions. This is the evidence that alternatives were assessed on cost. However, the specific alternative solutions and their charges in the top-10 table are truncated and not fully visible in the retrieved passages, so the detail of which alternatives were considered and why rejected cannot be fully verified. The suitability report itself does not reproduce or summarise this comparative analysis. The report's claim of 'Lower Costs' is partially misleading: while the platform charge is reduced (0.32% to 0.21%), the total ongoing charge rises from 0.54% to 0.62%. The report does acknowledge the increase is offset by non-cost benefits (risk alignment, oversight, platform integration), which is a legitimate approach under the guard — a cheaper option rejected for evidenced non-cost reasons is a valid outcome. However, the report's framing of 'Lower Costs' as a headline benefit without clearly explaining the net cost increase is a weak justification. Further, the switch report (P4) discloses that at the high growth rate the new solution projects £100 less than the existing arrangement, yet the suitability report does not acknowledge this. The critical yield at mid growth is 2.9% (P3), which is above the low growth rate (0% critical yield at low growth) — the report does not discuss this in the context of whether the switch represents value. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability adjustments are identified in the evidence as materially affecting this cost/charges assessment. Overall, the comparative cost research exists in supporting documents but is not adequately surfaced or reconciled in the suitability report, and the 'Lower Costs' claim is not accurate on a total charge basis without qualification.

  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states, under the Critical Yield section, the following RIY and critical yield figures at age 60: Low growth — existing RIY 1.6%, recommended RIY 1.6%, critical yield 0.0%, effect of switching £0 / 0.0% decrease. Mid growth — existing RIY 1.7%, recommended RIY 1.7%, critical yield 2.9%, effect of switching £0 / 0.0% decrease. High growth — existing RIY 1.8%, recommended RIY 1.7%, critical yield 5.9%, effect of switching £0 / 0.0% decrease.
    File:     The pension switch report [P4] shows: Low growth — existing RIY 1.6%, new solution RIY 1.6%, critical yield 0.0%, effect of switching £0 or 0.0% decrease. Mid growth — existing RIY 1.7%, new solution RIY 1.7%, critical yield 2.9%, effect of switching £0 or 0.0% decrease. High growth — existing RIY 1.8%, new solution RIY 1.7%, critical yield 5.9%, effect of switching £0 or 0.0% decrease. Passage [P3] cross-confirms the mid critical yield 2.9% and high critical yield 5.9%. The Aviva pension illustration [P6] states a product-and-investment-charges RIY of 0.6% and an all-charges RIY of 1.9% (not the same figures, but this illustration covers a different scenario — new solution only, including adviser charges, different term). The pension projection illustrations [P7][P8] show a product-and-investment RIY of 0.6% with no adviser charges facilitated. The effect-of-switching figures in [P3] show £100 or 0.1% decrease at both mid and high growth, contrasting with the report's £0 / 0.0% decrease.
    Discrepancies:
      - [P3] records the effect of switching at mid growth as '£100 or 0.1% decrease' whereas the suitability report states '£0 or 0.0% decrease'.
      - [P3] records the effect of switching at high growth as '£100 or 0.1% decrease' whereas the suitability report states '£0 or 0.0% decrease'.
      - [P4] records the effect of switching at low growth as '£0 or 0.0% decrease', consistent with the report — no discrepancy here.
      - The Aviva pension illustration [P6] states an all-charges RIY of 1.9%, which differs from the report's stated RIY of 1.7% for the recommended solution at high growth and 1.6%/1.7% at low/mid — but this illustration uses different assumptions (includes adviser charges, different term), so the figures are not directly comparable under the growth-rate guard.
      - The pension projection illustrations [P7][P8] state a product-and-investment RIY of 0.6%, materially lower than the 1.6%–1.8% figures in the report — but these projections exclude adviser charges and cover a different retirement age (65/67), so the guard on different retirement ages applies.
    The core RIY and critical yield figures stated in the suitability report are corroborated by [P4] across all three growth scenarios: low (1.6%/1.6%, CY 0.0%), mid (1.7%/1.7%, CY 2.9%), high (1.8%/1.7%, CY 5.9%). These figures match precisely. However, there is a genuine discrepancy on the effect-of-switching figures. The report states '£0 or 0.0% decrease' for both mid and high growth scenarios, but [P3] — the pension switch report on file — records '£100 or 0.1% decrease' for both mid and high growth. [P4] confirms £0 for low growth. The switch report [P4] also shows £0 for mid and high growth (consistent with the suitability report), but [P3] is a separate passage from the same document and shows £100/0.1% for mid and high. On inspection, [P3] and [P4] are both passages from the pension switch report: [P4] appears to be the main comparison table showing £0 for all scenarios, while [P3] appears to show a different section (possibly a summary) with £100/0.1% for mid and high. The primary comparison table in [P4] is consistent with the report. The £100/0.1% figure in [P3] represents a minor rounding difference (£100 on a ~£83,300–£93,400 projected fund, i.e. approximately 0.1%) which the severity guidance classifies as a low-severity rounding difference. The all-charges RIY of 1.9% in [P6] and the 0.6% in [P7]/[P8] are not comparable to the switch analysis RIY figures because [P6] includes adviser charges at a different term and [P7]/[P8] use a different retirement age (65/67 vs 60) and exclude adviser charges — the retirement-age guard explains this difference. The report's RIY and critical yield figures are therefore substantively corroborated. The only discrepancy is a rounding difference of £100/0.1% on effect of switching between [P3] (summary section) and [P4] (main table), with the main table matching the report. No vulnerability factors in the file alter this assessment.
