  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report states an ongoing adviser charge of 1% per annum across all Aviva Platform investments, reviewed annually. The services attached to this charge are: (1) reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss; (2) reviewing ongoing suitability of the investment portfolio; (3) market updates and legislative changes; (4) access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time for ad-hoc advice; (5) access to full holistic financial planning; (6) ongoing professional fund monitoring and portfolio review services; and (7) adviser charges to be deducted directly from the platform. The charge is described as applying across all Aviva Platform investments.
    File:     The Client Agreement To Proceed [P12] confirms an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), automatically taken from the funds invested. The Important Information document [P2] describes an ongoing service including an annual review meeting and recommendations. The client agreement [P4] states that ongoing advice and review services will be provided as described in the 'Important Information about our Services' document. No minimum fee or cap on the ongoing charge is mentioned in the suitability report. The client agreement [P12] records the initial fee as £4,794 (3% of funds). The client agreement [P4] states fees are set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation. The Important Information document [P2] describes an annual review meeting plus recommendations as the core of the ongoing service but does not explicitly enumerate all seven services listed in the report.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report does not mention the minimum ongoing fee of £100 stated in the client agreement [P12], which could be material depending on portfolio value movements.
      - The client agreement [P4] states that the fees are set out in the suitability report — the report does state 1% p.a., which is consistent with [P12]. No numerical discrepancy on the percentage rate itself.
      - The suitability report lists seven distinct ongoing services but the client agreement and Important Information document do not explicitly enumerate all seven of these services, meaning some service descriptions (e.g. holistic financial planning, ad-hoc advice access) are asserted only in the suitability report and not independently corroborated in the case file evidence.
      - The suitability report does not state the £100 minimum fee threshold, which is a material term in the client agreement that the client should be aware of.
    The 1% ongoing adviser charge rate is consistent between the suitability report and the client agreement [P12], which is positive. The client agreement [P4] confirms ongoing advice and review services will be provided. However, the suitability report asserts a detailed service proposition of seven elements, while the supporting evidence (client agreement and Important Information document) only partially corroborates this. The Important Information document [P2] describes an annual review meeting and recommendations, which aligns with the risk review and portfolio suitability review services. However, services such as 'access to full holistic financial planning', 'ad-hoc advice access at any time', and 'market and legislative updates' are stated only in the report (an [I] category document) and are not independently evidenced in the case file — the report cannot corroborate itself. The omission of the £100 minimum fee from the suitability report is a disclosure shortfall: a client whose portfolio fell significantly could find the effective percentage charge materially higher, and this term is absent from what the client receives in the suitability report. The services listed are not client-specific (they are generic service descriptions) but their absence from any supporting document beyond the suitability report means the service proposition is partially unsupported. The vulnerability overlay does not materially affect this finding — no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would elevate or reduce the concern here. Overall: the 1% rate is corroborated, but the full service proposition is only partially supported, and the £100 minimum fee is silently omitted from the report.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report acknowledges that the recommended pension switch results in a higher ongoing charge (0.62% vs 0.54% existing, +£63/year) and relies on five stated benefits to justify this: (1) Lower Costs — platform charge reduced from 0.32% to 0.21% via agency terms; (2) Risk Alignment — existing funds at risk level 6 are too high for the agreed Cautious Balanced profile of 4, and switching to the recommended fund aligns to risk level 4; (3) Professional Oversight — access to ongoing fund monitoring and portfolio review; (4) Tax-Efficient Contribution — £3,600 gross pension contribution utilising annual allowance with 20% tax relief; (5) Platform Integration — pension, ISA and GIA consolidated on Aviva Platform for administrative convenience. The report also states the cost justification: 'this small increase in charges is justified by the improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight', and provides a critical yield statement that the plan must grow at 2.94% per year (matching the mid-growth rate) to match the current plan's value, which the report asserts is achievable given improved risk alignment and professional oversight.
    File:     The pension switching research report [P5] confirms the agreed risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) and that the investment of £79,092 is being risk-rated at level 4. The suitability report itself [P21] provides a performance and volatility comparison showing the existing strategy (risk level 6 blended) against the recommended Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II and CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund, confirming the recommended fund demonstrates lower volatility consistent with risk level 4. The existing arrangements section [P22] confirms the existing pension holds Vanguard LifeStrategy 60% and 80% Equity funds at a blended risk level of 6, with no penalties or exit costs. The client report [P17] confirms the closing fund value of £79,092.32. The ongoing adviser charge section [P23] states the ongoing fee is 1% per annum across all Aviva Platform investments, approximately £1,558 for the year based on a total platform value of £155,812. The research reports [P1], [P2], [P3] confirm the Aviva Platform was selected and its features. The fact find [P24] confirms the client's objective of a minimum retirement income of £2,200 net per month, the desire to review and improve the pension, and interest in investment advice. No provider illustration is present in the pack (extraction notes /solution/0/illustrationProvided as NotPresentInDocument). No independent evidence of the agency terms platform charge rate of 0.21% appears in the retrieved passages — this figure appears only in the suitability report. No evidence in the pack independently confirms the critical yield of 2.94% or the mid-growth rate used. The 'Professional Oversight' benefit is generic and not client-specific. The existing plan already sits on the Aviva Platform, so Platform Integration is already available to the client without the switch.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report claims 'Lower Costs' as a benefit (platform charge reduced from 0.32% to 0.21%), but the net overall charge increases from 0.54% to 0.62% (+0.08%, +£63/year) — the report itself acknowledges this increase in the Like for Like section, creating an internal tension in presenting 'Lower Costs' as a top-level benefit heading when total costs rise.
      - The 'Platform Integration' benefit (pension, ISA, GIA on Aviva Platform) is stated as a justification for the higher cost, but the existing arrangement is already on the Aviva Platform (Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd Pension Portfolio SIPP) — this benefit is therefore already available to the client and cannot justify additional cost per the guard.
      - The critical yield of 2.94% is stated as equal to the mid-growth rate, with the report asserting achievability based on past performance and improved risk alignment — no provider illustration or independent projection is in the pack to corroborate this figure or the mid-growth rate used.
      - The 'Professional Oversight' benefit is described in generic terms and is flagged as not client-specific in the canonical model — no service proposition document or client agreement excerpt in the pack independently evidences what specific oversight services will be provided.
      - No independent evidence of the agency terms platform charge of 0.21% appears in the retrieved passages — the charge reduction claim is self-asserted in the report only.
    The report faces a structural tension in its cost justification: it leads with 'Lower Costs' as the first stated benefit, yet the total ongoing charge rises from 0.54% to 0.62%. The report does address this transparently in the 'Like for Like' section, acknowledging the small increase and relying on risk alignment and professional oversight to justify it. However, presenting 'Lower Costs' as a benefit heading when total charges increase is potentially misleading and at minimum creates a disclosure shortfall — a client reading the benefit list may not immediately appreciate that, taken as a whole, charges are higher.
    
    The 'Platform Integration' benefit is particularly weak. The existing pension is already on the Aviva Platform (Aviva Life & Pensions UK Ltd). The guard explicitly states that a benefit the existing plan already provides cannot justify the extra cost. Consolidation convenience is cited, but the pension itself is already on the same platform — only the ISA and GIA are new additions, and those are new products being established regardless of whether the pension switch occurs. This benefit therefore cannot legitimately justify the pension switch cost premium.
    
    Risk alignment is the strongest benefit and is corroborated by the research reports [P5] and the performance/volatility analysis in [P21], which show the existing funds carry higher risk than the agreed profile. This is a genuine, client-specific benefit.
    
    The critical yield of 2.94% equals the stated mid-growth rate — while this appears benign (the plan only needs to match the growth rate assumed), no provider illustration is in the pack to independently confirm this figure. The assertion of achievability based on 'past performance' of the recommended funds introduces a forward-looking claim not independently evidenced in the pack.
    
    The 'Professional Oversight' benefit is generic. The ongoing service description in [P23] lists standard services, but this appears only in the suitability report (category [I]) and cannot self-corroborate. No separate service proposition document is in the pack.
    
    The agency terms platform charge of 0.21% is stated only in the report — no fee schedule, client agreement or provider document in the pack independently confirms this rate.
    
    On balance: risk alignment is genuinely supported; the 'Lower Costs' framing alongside a rising total charge is a disclosure shortfall; 'Platform Integration' relies on a benefit the existing arrangement already provides; and the critical yield achievability claim and the 0.21% agency terms rate are unsupported by independent evidence. These are Potential Concerns, though the risk alignment benefit is substantive enough that this is not a high-severity finding.

  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report acknowledges the recommendation is more expensive: the new pension costs 0.62% vs the existing 0.54%, a difference of +0.08% (£63 more per year). Including the 1.00% ongoing adviser fee, the new plan must outperform the existing by approximately 1.08% annually to match the same net growth. Critical yield projections (before adviser fee) show: Low 0.00% / Mid 2.9% / High 5.9%. RIY figures for the new plan are 1.6% (low), 1.7% (mid), 1.8% (high). Projected values at age 60 in real terms: Low £74,000, Mid £83,300, High £93,400. The report presents these under a 'Critical Yield' section within a 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' and an 'Including Adviser Fee' section.
    File:     The pension switch report [P7] provides a comparison table (before adviser charges) showing: Low — existing £74,000 / new £74,000, RIY both 1.6%, critical yield 0.0%, effect £0/0.0% decrease. Mid — existing £83,300 / new £83,300, RIY 1.7%/1.7%, critical yield 2.9%, effect £0/0.0% decrease. High — existing £93,400 / new £93,400, RIY existing 1.8%/new 1.7%, effect £0/0.0% decrease, critical yield 5.9%. A separate table [P2] shows the comparison after adviser charges: Mid — existing £86,800 / new £86,700, effect £100/0.1% decrease; High — existing £97,400 / new £97,300, effect £100/0.1% decrease. RIY for new solution at mid is 0.7% and at high is 0.7%. The switch report [P15] records the recommendation to switch. The Aviva pension illustration [P5] confirms projections in real terms at three growth rates. The existing plan fund value at switch is £79,092.32 [P7].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states RIY at high growth for the new plan as 1.8%, but the switch report [P7] records the new solution RIY at high growth as 1.7% — a 0.1% difference. The existing plan RIY at high is 1.8% in [P7]; the report appears to have quoted the existing plan figure for the new plan at high growth.
      - The report asserts a required outperformance of 1.08% annually including the adviser fee; this figure does not appear in any retrieved passage and is not corroborated by any non-[I] source — only the suitability report itself states it.
      - The charge difference of +0.08% and the £63 monetary figure do not appear in any retrieved passage and are not corroborated by any non-[I] source. The nearest figure in the switch report passages is 0.1% (rounding of 0.08%), but the exact 0.08% figure is unmatched.
      - The switch report [P2] post-adviser-fee comparison shows projected values higher than the pre-adviser-fee comparison (e.g., mid existing £86,800 vs £83,300 pre-adviser). These use different bases (different contribution assumptions or growth periods), which is not explained in the retrieved passages.
      - The report's RIY figures (1.6%, 1.7%, 1.8% at low/mid/high) match the pre-adviser-fee switch report table for the new solution at low and mid, but at high the report states 1.8% while [P7] shows 1.7% for the new solution (1.8% is the existing plan's high-growth RIY).
    The primary discrepancy is the RIY at high growth: the report asserts 1.8% for the new plan, but the switch report [P7] records 1.7% for the new solution at high growth (with 1.8% being the existing plan's figure at that rate). This appears to be the report quoting the wrong column — the existing plan's RIY rather than the new solution's RIY at high growth. This is a data inconsistency between the suitability report and the supporting switch report, though it is of low practical significance (0.1% difference) and does not change the direction of the analysis. The required outperformance of 1.08% and the +0.08% charge difference are stated only in the suitability report [I] with no corroboration from any external document. However, the charge arithmetic in the derived figures confirms 0.08% / £63 is arithmetically consistent with the fund values in the switch report, and the 1.08% figure equals 0.08% plus the 1.00% adviser fee — which is arithmetically derivable from the report's own figures. The guard on required outperformance under 0.5% being within noise applies to the pre-adviser-fee 0.08%, but the 1.08% including adviser fee is material and above the noise threshold. The switch report itself does not carry the 1.08% figure, so the report's value-for-money justification at that level rests solely on its own internal assertion. The critical yield figures (0.0%, 2.9%, 5.9%) and projections (£74,000, £83,300, £93,400) are corroborated by [P7]. The effect of switching before adviser charges is shown as nil (£0/0.0% decrease at all rates) in [P7], which is internally consistent with the critical yield figures equalling the growth rates used. The post-adviser-fee comparison in [P2] shows a small £100/0.1% decrease at mid and high, which is consistent with the 1% adviser charge being applied to the new solution. No vulnerability factors in the file appear to affect this specific cost-and-charges analysis. Overall: the RIY mismatch at high growth is a genuine data inconsistency of low severity; the absence of external corroboration for the 1.08% outperformance figure is a disclosure shortfall of moderate concern given the materiality of the adviser fee to the value assessment.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 6 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). The report does not mention the specific risk categories applicable to the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II as disclosed in the factsheet (e.g. property illiquidity risk [H], high yield bond risk [I], reinsured fund insolvency risk [J], alternative investment fund liquidity risk [K], foreign exchange risk [B], emerging markets risk [C], smaller companies risk [D], fixed interest/interest rate risk [E], derivatives risk, specialist fund concentration risk) — none of these product-specific fund risks appear in the suitability report. The report does not mention that in certain circumstances Aviva may delay payments, transfers or switching for up to one month or longer (e.g. property funds), as disclosed in the pension key features document. The report does not address the income drawdown risks disclosed in the pension key features: fund depletion if high income is taken, reduced legacy/annuity purchase capacity, potential overpayment of tax on income drawings. 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report discloses the following material disadvantages and limitations: (1) Higher charges — the recommended fund strategy is 0.08% more expensive than the current plan, requiring outperformance of £63 in the first year on a like-for-like basis; (2) Including the 1% adviser fee, total cost rises to approximately 1.62% vs the current 0.54%, requiring approximately 1.08% annual outperformance to match net growth; (3) Lower historical returns on recommended funds, though with less severe drawdowns; (4) Access delay of 10–15 working days for disinvestment; (5) No guarantee of outperformance or greater pension fund at retirement; (6) CGT exposure on GIA gains above £3,000 annual exemption (18% basic/24% higher rate); (7) Dividend tax exposure above £500 in the GIA; (8) Past performance is not a guarantee of future returns; (9) Transaction costs excluded from quoted charges (variable); (10) Charge calculation methodology may differ from illustration; (11) Earnings uncertainty for pension contribution (client recently retired, short working period); (12) Beneficiary nomination uncertainty — Expression of Wish form posted out.
    File:     Aviva Pension Key Features documents confirm: investment value can go down as well as up; complex decision to transfer with charges, fund range, valuable benefits and tax implications to consider; no guarantee of higher benefits after transfer; ongoing advice recommended; disinvestment delays possible in certain market conditions (up to one month or longer for property/illiquid assets); tax treatment may change; income drawdown risks including depletion of fund if high income taken, reduced legacy, and possible overpayment of tax. The Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II factsheet discloses specific fund risks including property illiquidity, high yield bond default risk, reinsured fund insolvency risk, alternative investment fund liquidity risk, foreign exchange risk, emerging markets risk, smaller companies risk, fixed interest/interest rate risk, derivatives default risk, and specialist fund concentration risk. The Aviva GIA Key Features confirms investment value can go down as well as up; overseas investment currency risk; emerging market risk; small company liquidity risk. The Aviva ISA Key Features confirms value can go down as well as up; no guarantee transfer is in client's best interests; additional permitted subscription rules on death. The policy research file confirms the existing plan fund value of £79,092.32 with no guaranteed benefits noted. The fact find confirms total monthly household expenditure of £2,206.67 and emergency fund of £10,000 in place.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not mention the specific risk categories applicable to the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II as disclosed in the factsheet (e.g. property illiquidity risk [H], high yield bond risk [I], reinsured fund insolvency risk [J], alternative investment fund liquidity risk [K], foreign exchange risk [B], emerging markets risk [C], smaller companies risk [D], fixed interest/interest rate risk [E], derivatives risk, specialist fund concentration risk) — none of these product-specific fund risks appear in the suitability report.
      - The report does not mention that in certain circumstances Aviva may delay payments, transfers or switching for up to one month or longer (e.g. property funds), as disclosed in the pension key features document.
      - The report does not address the income drawdown risks disclosed in the pension key features: fund depletion if high income is taken, reduced legacy/annuity purchase capacity, potential overpayment of tax on income drawings.
      - The report does not explicitly state what benefits or features the client is giving up by switching from the existing Aviva plan (e.g. any loss of FSCS coverage structure, loss of existing investment history), though no guaranteed benefits are identified in the research.
      - The report characterises fund-specific risk only in generic terms (higher charges, historical performance comparison, no guarantee of outperformance) without referencing the specific risk categories applicable to the recommended fund as set out in the product literature.
      - The report does not mention currency/foreign exchange risk applicable to the recommended funds despite this being disclosed in the fund factsheet.
      - The report does not mention the alternative investment fund liquidity risk [K] applicable to the recommended Aviva Multi-Asset Plus Fund II.
    The report covers the most financially material disadvantages for this case — higher charges (quantified in both like-for-like and all-in terms), the required outperformance threshold, access delays, no guarantee of outperformance, CGT and dividend tax consequences for the GIA, past performance caveat, earnings uncertainty, and the beneficiary nomination gap. These are client-specific and well-presented.
    
    However, the product literature for the recommended Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II (passages P5 and P6) discloses a range of specific risk categories that are applicable to the fund: foreign exchange risk [B], high yield bond risk [I], reinsured fund insolvency risk [J], alternative investment fund liquidity risk [K], fixed interest/interest rate risk [E], derivatives risk, and specialist fund concentration risk. None of these appear in the suitability report in any form — not under a disadvantages section, not in an appendix, and not referenced to product literature. The report does not delegate risk disclosure to product literature either; it simply omits the product-specific fund risk warnings entirely.
    
    The pension key features (P3, P4) also disclose that Aviva may delay payments/transfers for up to one month or longer in certain market conditions, and discloses income drawdown risks (fund depletion, reduced legacy, tax overpayment). The report mentions only a 10–15 working day administration delay but does not address the broader liquidity deferral risk or the income drawdown risk warnings relevant to the client who may crystallise benefits.
    
    The false-positive guard that disadvantages may be spread across several sections has been applied: no passage anywhere in the report addresses these product-specific fund risks.
    
    The omission of fund-specific risk categories is not individually severe but is collectively material — these are standard disclosures from the product's own factsheet that the report does not mention. The income drawdown risks are relevant because the client's objective is retirement income and the pension switch is central to the case.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would heighten the disclosure concern for this client.
    
    The charge arithmetic is internally consistent with the derived figures (0.08% / £63 difference; 1.08% including adviser fee). No discrepancy on the figures used.
    
    Overall: the report provides good coverage of the financially quantifiable disadvantages but omits product-specific fund risk disclosures that appear in the supporting literature. This constitutes a disclosure shortfall — not a complete absence of risk disclosure, but a gap in product-specific content. Severity is Moderate (risks stated in generic terms with nothing product-specific from the fund factsheet).

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states the following product-specific and investment-specific risks: (1) PensionContributionExcessTaxCharge — if the client contributes more than earned in this tax year, a tax charge applies and HMRC will treat the excess as additional income taxed at marginal rate; (2) MinimumPensionAge — benefits can be drawn from age 55 (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028); (3) PensionIHTLegislativeChange — from 6 April 2027 most unused DC pensions and death benefits will be included in taxable estate for IHT; (4) DeathBenefitsTaxTreatment — pre-75 death benefits paid free of tax, post-75 at beneficiary's marginal rate; (5) CancellationRisk — previous provider not obliged to take the plan back; (6) CancellationMarketLoss — fall in value before cancellation notice received will be deducted; (7) MarketRiseDisadvantage and MarketFallAdvantage during switch; (8) CashInflationRisk — long-term cash cannot keep pace with inflation; (9) VariableInterestRateRisk — Moneybox ISA rate circa 4.5% but variable; (10) MarketRiskDuringSwitch — stock market could rise or fall while transactions pending; (11) CapitalRisk — unit prices and value of pension/ISA/GIA can fall as well as rise, no guarantee of return of original capital; (12) PastPerformanceWarning — past performance no guarantee of future returns. Capital-at-risk wording is applied to the pension and (by inference) ISA and GIA. Tax consequences of excess pension contributions are stated. IHT legislative change is noted. No explicit reference to the product-specific risk warnings (A–K) assigned to the recommended funds in the product literature, nor to derivative risk, fixed interest risk, credit risk, emerging markets risk, foreign exchange risk, or liquidity/suspension risk identified in the fund factsheets and KIIDs.
    File:     Product literature in the pack identifies the following product-specific and fund-specific risks: For the Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II (pension fund): risk warnings A (General — value not guaranteed, can go down as well as up), B (Foreign Exchange Risk), C (Emerging Markets), E (Fixed Interest), F (Derivatives); the KIID for CT Universal MAP Cautious C Acc identifies Derivative Risk (losses can be greater than initial investment), Fixed Interest Securities Risk, Credit Risk, Smaller Companies Risk, Emerging Markets Risk, Liquidity Risk, and a 5-year minimum recommended holding period ('this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years'). The Aviva ISA Key Features document identifies suspension/deferral risk (inability to sell or switch if fund manager suspends dealing), property liquidity risk, and the possibility that ISA tax treatment may change in future. The Aviva GIA Key Features identifies emerging markets risk, smaller company risk, overseas/currency risk, and the impact of charges on growth. The Aviva Pension Key Features identifies that transfer value may not get higher benefits, value can go down as well as up, and transfer complexity. Pension projections provide lower/medium/higher growth scenarios including a real-terms lower scenario where fund value falls in today's money terms. The pension review report (P15) flags that the existing holdings' 10-year volatility maps to risk level 6, outside the client's agreed risk level of 4.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not mention Derivative Risk for either recommended fund (Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II or CT Universal MAP Cautious), despite both fund factsheets/KIIDs identifying derivatives exposure — the CT KIID explicitly warns that derivative losses can be greater than the initial investment.
      - The report does not mention Fixed Interest Securities Risk (interest rate sensitivity of bond-holding funds) for either recommended fund, despite both factsheets/KIIDs identifying this risk.
      - The report does not mention Emerging Markets Risk for either recommended fund, despite the Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II factsheet flagging risk warning C (Emerging Markets) and the CT KIID flagging Emerging Markets Risk.
      - The report does not mention Foreign Exchange Risk for the Aviva Multi-Asset Plus II fund, despite the factsheet flagging risk warning B (Foreign Exchange Risk).
      - The report does not mention Credit Risk identified in the CT Universal MAP Cautious KIID.
      - The report does not mention Liquidity Risk (ability to buy/sell at favourable price may be affected) identified in the CT Universal MAP Cautious KIID.
      - The report does not mention the 5-year minimum recommended holding period flagged in the CT Universal MAP Cautious KIID ('this fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years'), which is relevant given the client intends to draw benefits before or around retirement.
      - The report does not mention dealing suspension/deferral risk for the ISA or GIA (inability to sell or switch funds if the fund manager suspends dealing), identified in the Aviva ISA Key Features.
      - The report does not mention the possibility that ISA tax treatment may change in future, identified in the Aviva ISA Key Features.
      - The report does not mention the impact of charges being taken from investment versus income on growth, identified in the Aviva GIA Key Features.
      - The capital-at-risk warning for the GIA is stated as inferred (confidence 0.85) rather than explicitly quoted, suggesting it may not be explicitly stated in the report for that product.
      - No loss scenario or illustration of what the client could receive back at the lower growth rate is referenced or cited in the body of the suitability report, despite pension projections at lower/medium/higher growth rates being present in the file (P14, P16).
    The report provides a reasonable baseline of general capital-at-risk, past performance, and pension-specific warnings (IHT, contribution tax charge, minimum pension age, death benefits, cancellation risk, and switch market risk). These satisfy the general investment risk disclosure requirement. However, applying the guard that standard capital-at-risk wording satisfies the general warning but not product-specific risks, a significant set of product-specific risks identified in the fund factsheets and KIIDs are entirely absent from the report. Both recommended funds carry derivatives exposure, fixed interest rate sensitivity, and emerging markets exposure — risks that are material and flagged by the product issuers. The CT Universal MAP Cautious KIID additionally warns that derivative losses can exceed the initial investment and that the fund is unsuitable for investors with a sub-5-year horizon, which is potentially relevant given the client's retirement timeline. The report does not mention any of these fund-specific risk categories. The Aviva ISA Key Features identifies fund suspension risk and the possibility that ISA tax treatment could change, neither of which appears in the report. No loss scenario illustration is cited in the body of the report — while projections exist in the file (P14, P16), the suitability report does not reference them or quote a lower-growth outcome. The capital-at-risk warning for the GIA is extracted at lower confidence as an inference, suggesting it may not be explicitly stated for that wrapper. The disclosure gap is material: the omitted risks are not peripheral but are named risk warnings assigned by the product providers themselves. The report does not delegate to product literature explicitly, so this is not a case of explicit delegation — it simply omits these risks. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to alter this finding: no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the canonical model that would heighten or reduce the risk disclosure requirement, and the extraction notes the vulnerability assessment outcome as absent. The finding stands on the omission of product-specific risks alone.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report has a dedicated section headed 'Other Considerations of Switching' within the main body. It states: unit prices and pension fund values can fall as well as rise; no guarantee of return of original capital; a switch does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement; no guarantee the new provider's funds will outperform existing ones. Tax consequences are addressed in dedicated sections covering GIA taxation (dividends, interest, capital gains), ISA tax treatment, pension allowances (lump sum allowance £268,275; lump sum and death benefit allowance £1,073,100), and pension contribution rules. The report notes the MPAA has not been triggered. Capacity for loss is described as Medium-High with a dedicated section. Cashflow modelling is included with a market-crash scenario. The pension switch is supported by a charges comparison showing the new plan costs 0.08% more (£63 per annum) than the existing plan. Risk warnings appear both in the dedicated switching section and in the fund recommendation section. The cancellation notice and 30-day right to cancel are disclosed. Death benefit tax treatment (tax-free before age 75; marginal rate after age 75) is disclosed. The report notes the new plan is not a stakeholder pension and explains why. Vulnerability: no formal vulnerability assessment outcome is recorded.
    File:     The pension switch report [P9][P10][P11] confirms: effect of switching is a £100 or 0.1% decrease at mid and high growth rates (adviser charge scenario), and £0 or 0.0% decrease without adviser charges. Critical yield figures range from 0.0% (low growth, no adviser charge) to 5.9% (high growth, with adviser charge). Reduction in yield rises from 0.6%/0.7% (existing) to 0.7%/0.7% (new). The switch report records no benefits lost on switching ('No benefits were listed for the existing products') [P11]. The GIA illustration [P13] shows projected values declining in real terms in the 'after all charges' column relative to before-charges projections, demonstrating loss scenarios. The cashflow market-crash scenario [P14] is present in the file. The switch report records an initial adviser charge of £0.00 [P11]. The suitability report risk warnings in [P3] cover capital loss and switching risk. Tax sections in [P1][P2] cover GIA, ISA and pension taxation. The capacity for loss and knowledge/experience sections are in [P6]. No vulnerability assessment outcome is recorded in any document.
    Discrepancies:
      - The pension switch report records that no benefits are lost on switching ('No benefits were listed for the existing products') [P11], but the suitability report does not explicitly state this — it is silent on whether any guarantees, safeguarded benefits or other protections attached to the existing Aviva plan are foregone. The switch report's finding that no benefits are lost is present only in a supporting document, not surfaced in the body of the suitability report.
      - The switch report shows a critical yield of 5.9% at the high growth rate scenario [P9][P11], meaning the new solution must grow at 5.9% to match the existing pension at that growth rate. This figure is not referenced in the suitability report body.
      - The switch report shows an effect of switching of minus £100 (0.1% decrease) at mid and high growth rates [P9][P11]. The suitability report acknowledges higher charges (+0.08%, +£63 p.a.) but does not translate this into a projected capital loss figure for the client.
      - The GIA is subject to capital gains tax above the £3,000 annual exemption, with rates of 18% (basic rate) and 24% (higher/additional rate) per [P1]. The report discloses CGT rates in a generic tax section but does not apply these specifically to the client's GIA investment of £57,120 or model a scenario where gains are realised.
      - The report states 'your current earnings are sufficient to support the recommended contribution level' while also stating the client has stopped working — an internal tension noted in the extraction. This uncertainty about the contribution basis is not disclosed as a risk or limitation to the client in the disadvantages/risks section.
      - No vulnerability assessment outcome is recorded anywhere in the file; the extraction flags this as absent. The report does not consider whether any vulnerability characteristics affect the presentation or comprehension of risk disclosures.
      - The report does not quantify or illustrate a specific downside/loss scenario for the ISA or GIA investments (e.g. what the portfolio would be worth in a market downturn), beyond the general 'can fall as well as rise' statement and the cashflow market-crash chart.
    The report has a dedicated switching risk section in the main body, not confined to an appendix, which satisfies the prominence requirement for the pension switch risks. Generic investment risk warnings (capital loss, no guarantee of outperformance) are present [P3]. Tax consequences for the GIA, ISA and pension are addressed in substantive named sections [P1][P2]. The cancellation right and death benefit taxation are disclosed [P5]. Cashflow modelling including a market-crash scenario is referenced [P14][P8]. On the face of it, the report covers the principal categories of risk.
    
    However, several gaps remain material:
    
    1. Critical yield and projected capital loss from switching: The switch report quantifies the effect of switching as a £100 (0.1%) decrease in projected fund value at mid and high growth rates [P9][P11], and records a critical yield of 5.9% at the high growth rate [P9][P11]. The suitability report states the new plan costs £63 p.a. more but does not translate this into a projected capital shortfall figure or reference the critical yield. For a pension switch, the critical yield and projected capital impact are material consumer harm figures that a client would need to weigh the recommendation. Their absence from the report body — while present in the switch tool output — is a disclosure shortfall.
    
    2. Benefits lost on switching: The switch report records 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' [P11]. This is a finding that could reassure the client. Its absence from the suitability report means the client is not explicitly told whether any guarantees or protections are being surrendered.
    
    3. GIA tax — client-specific application: The report discloses CGT rates generically [P1] but does not apply them to the client's specific £57,120 GIA holding, where gains above £3,000 will be subject to 18%/24% CGT. Given the client has little investment experience [P6] and the GIA is a new product type for them, a client-specific illustration of the tax consequence would be proportionate.
    
    4. Contribution basis risk: The report acknowledges uncertainty about the client's earnings yet simultaneously asserts sufficiency. This risk — that the £3,600 gross pension contribution may exceed the client's relevant UK earnings and therefore be invalid — is not disclosed as a limitation in the risk section.
    
    5. Vulnerability: No formal vulnerability assessment outcome is recorded. The extraction flags this as absent from the document. The client's stated low investment knowledge [P6] (no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares) is recorded but the report does not address whether this affected how risks were communicated or whether any adjustments to the presentation were made. Under the vulnerability overlay, this is noted but assessed as a secondary concern to CHK-010.
    
    The most material issues are (1) the absence of the critical yield and capital impact figures from the switch in the suitability report body, and (2) the absence of a clear statement that no benefits are lost. These are not explained by any guard: the switch report data exists in the file and the suitability report simply does not surface it. The remaining gaps (GIA tax application, contribution basis risk) are moderate rather than high in isolation. Overall this is a Potential Concern at Moderate severity — the core risk structure is present, but quantified loss data from the switch tool and explicit confirmation of benefits lost/not lost are absent from the report.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report asserts that no safeguarded features, guaranteed annuity rates (GARs) or minimum income guarantees (MIGs) were identified on the existing plan, and therefore no features or guarantees are lost. It notes the existing platform offers 'Good platform with comprehensive facilities', 'Full flexi-access drawdown and UFPLS available', 'Over 6,900 funds available', and 'Allows Servicing Adviser: Yes'. The existing plan is described as having no transfer penalty (£0.00) and no life cover attached.
    File:     Policy research [P2] confirms: Guaranteed annuity rates (GAR) = No; Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP) = No; Guaranteed growth rates = No. The LOA response [P13] confirms: 'No annuity options', 'No guaranteed annuity rates', 'No guaranteed minimum pension', '25% Tax free cash, 75% Taxable flexible drawdown available', 'No Terminal bonuses', 'There is no Market Value Adjustment/Early Transfer Charges applicable to this plan.' The pension switch report [P5] confirms transfer penalty = £0.00. The policy research [P4] records the existing plan can be moved by Origo (Yes), no life cover attached, partial transfers allowed, projections provided. The switch report [P10] identifies the existing product as a Personal Pension Plan (not a SIPP) switching to a SIPP (Pension Portfolio - Choice on Aviva Platform). The LOA [P13] notes the existing plan has over 3,000 funds with more than 110 fund managers and offers full and partial encashment from age 55 and flexi-access drawdown. The report states over 6,900 funds on the existing plan, while the LOA states 'over 3000 funds' — these differ. The new plan is a SIPP on the Aviva Platform; the existing plan is a Personal Pension Plan with no platform, so the client is moving from a direct personal pension to a platform-based SIPP.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states 'Over 6,900 funds available' on the existing plan, but the LOA response states 'We have over 3000 funds with more than 110 fund managers' — a direct numerical discrepancy between 6,900+ and 3,000+.
      - The report does not explicitly address that the client is moving from a non-platform Personal Pension Plan to a platform-based SIPP, and does not discuss whether any features specific to the existing Personal Pension Plan product type are lost in that transition (e.g., any product-type distinctions).
      - The report states 'no features or guarantees are lost' but does not address the fund range reduction (from 6,900+ to whatever the new platform offers) as a potential feature difference, nor does it reconcile the fund count discrepancy with the LOA.
    The core assertion — no GARs, no GMPs, no safeguarded benefits — is well corroborated by both the policy research [P2] and the LOA [P13]. The LOA confirms no guaranteed annuity rates, no GMP, no MVA/early transfer charges, and no terminal bonuses, all consistent with the report. The transfer penalty of £0.00 is confirmed by [P5]. These are the most material protections in a pension switch context, and their absence is properly documented and evidenced. However, there is a direct discrepancy on the fund range figure: the report states 'Over 6,900 funds available' on the existing plan, while the LOA [P13] — the provider's own response — states 'We have over 3000 funds with more than 110 fund managers.' The guard requires checking whether a feature described in generic product literature is actually carried by this policy; the LOA is the policy-specific response and gives 3,000+. The report's 6,900+ figure is therefore inconsistent with the provider's own statement about this specific plan. While the fund range is not a safeguarded benefit or guarantee, it is an 'other feature' of the existing arrangement that the report itself chose to disclose — and it has disclosed it inaccurately. This is a data inconsistency rather than a missing disclosure of a material guarantee. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics have been identified in the pack that would heighten concern here beyond the standard requirement. The discrepancy on fund numbers is a factual inaccuracy in the report about a feature it chose to record, but it does not represent a material protection being given up that was omitted. The more significant features (GARs, GMPs, MVA, life cover) are all correctly stated as absent. The fund count error is a data quality/inconsistency issue of low-to-moderate significance — the direction of the error (overstating the existing plan's fund range) could slightly understate the relative attractiveness of the existing plan's fund range, but this is marginal. The transition from Personal Pension Plan to SIPP is noted in the switch report but not explicitly addressed in the 'features being given up' section of the report; however, there are no specific features of the Personal Pension Plan product type identified in the evidence as being lost in this transition that the report omits.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report discloses two layers of cost increase: (1) on a like-for-like basis, the recommended fund strategy is 0.08% more expensive than the current plan, requiring the proposed plan to outperform by £63 in the first year; this is justified by 'improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight'. (2) Including the 1% annual adviser fee, total cost rises to approximately 1.62% versus the existing 0.54%, requiring outperformance of approximately 1.08% annually. The report states that based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, the recommended plan would need to grow at 2.94% per year to age 60 to match the current plan's value, and that this is 'achievable given the improved risk alignment and professional oversight'. Three scenario projections are given: low (0.00%) £74,000; mid (2.94%) £83,300; high (5.88%) £93,400 at age 60. Disadvantages disclosed include no guarantee of outperformance, no guarantee of a greater fund at retirement, CGT exposure on GIA gains above £3,000 annual exemption, dividend tax exposure above £500, and a past performance warning.
    File:     The pension switch report [P4] shows: at mid growth (2.94%) the existing pension projects £86,800 and new solution £86,700 (effect of switching: £100 or 0.1% decrease); at high growth (5.88%) existing £97,400, new solution £97,300 (£100 or 0.1% decrease). The switch report including adviser charges [P10] shows: at low (0.00%) both existing and new £74,000 (£0 or 0.0% decrease); at mid (2.94%) both £83,300 (£0 or 0.0% decrease); at high (5.88%) both £93,400 (£0 or 0.0% decrease). The pension switch report [P5] confirms existing plan fund value £79,092.32, transfer penalty £0.00, no ongoing contributions. [P21] (report itself) shows the Pension Switch Charges Only table: existing plan 0.54% / £427; impact +0.08% / +£63. The GIA illustration [P1] and ISA illustration [P2] show effect-of-charges tables. The pension projection age 67 [P11] shows a separate projection to age 67. Fund research [P16] shows 5-year cumulative returns: Aviva Insured Funds Multi-asset Plus Fund II 36.21% vs CT Universal MAP Cautious 31.28% and Vanguard LifeStrategy 40% 21.49%.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report's suitability report states the mid-growth projected value at age 60 for the new plan is £83,300 (in the Critical Yield section). The pension switch report [P4] (which includes adviser charges) also shows £83,300 for the new solution at mid growth — these are consistent. However [P4] (without adviser charges, like-for-like) shows the existing pension at mid growth projects to £86,800 and new solution at £86,700, a £100 decrease. The report does not disclose this £100 like-for-like decrease in projected maturity value; it only discloses the monetary charge differential of £63.
      - The report asserts the critical yield (mid growth) requires the new plan to grow at 2.94% per year — matching the mid growth rate itself. [P4] confirms the critical yield is 2.9% (new solution), consistent with the report. However [P10] (including adviser charges table) shows the critical yield at mid growth is 2.9%, while [P4] (like-for-like, without adviser charges) also shows 2.9%. The report conflates or does not clearly distinguish which critical yield figure applies with and without the adviser fee.
      - The report states outperformance required is approximately 1.08% annually when including the 1% adviser fee (1.62% minus 0.54%). The derived figures confirm: £63 at 0.08% implies a fund base of ~£78,750 and the required outperformance figure of 1.08% at £63 implies a fund of £5,833 — the arithmetic of 1.08% outperformance on the full fund value is not arithmetically derived from the charge differential alone, suggesting the 1.08% figure is simply 1.62% minus 0.54%, which is a percentage-point subtraction rather than a yield calculation on projected values. This is not a contradiction but a methodological point the report does not explain.
      - The justification for the cost increase rests on 'improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight' — this is the same phrase repeated for both the like-for-like justification and the critical yield achievability assertion. No independent evidence in the file directly corroborates that the recommended fund's expected return will exceed the critical yield; the fund research [P16] shows 5-year cumulative return of 36.21% for Aviva Multi-asset Plus Fund II vs the existing Vanguard funds, which provides some historical support but is not cited by the report in its cost justification.
    The report makes two cost disclosures that are broadly present and arithmetically consistent with the switch report evidence. The like-for-like charge differential of +0.08% / +£63 is confirmed by [P21] (report itself) and consistent with [P4]/[P10]. The total cost including adviser fee of 1.62% versus 0.54% is internally stated and the 1.08% outperformance requirement follows arithmetically from those figures. The three projection scenarios (£74,000 / £83,300 / £93,400) match [P10] exactly. The critical yield of 2.94% (mid) is confirmed by [P4] and [P10]. On this basis the core disclosures are present and consistent with the supporting evidence. The discrepancy worth noting is that [P4] (the like-for-like switch comparison without adviser charges) shows a small projected maturity value decrease of £100 at mid and high growth rates when comparing existing to new on a like-for-like basis. The report does not disclose this projected value reduction; it only mentions the £63 annual charge differential. However this is a very minor figure (0.1% of projected value) and is explained by the slightly higher like-for-like charge base. The guard applies: a justification that repeats the benefit without connecting it to the cost is weak but is a justification; the 'improved risk alignment and ongoing professional oversight' reasoning appears twice and is not independently quantified or linked to a specific expected return premium over the critical yield. The fund research in [P16] provides historical performance data that could have been cited but was not. The absence of a more specific connection between the benefit claimed and the cost incurred makes the justification weak rather than absent. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability flags in the evidence materially affect this specific disclosure requirement. Overall: the report explains the increased costs to the client at two levels, discloses the required outperformance, provides scenario projections, and gives the critical yield. The justification is present but repetitive and generic rather than quantitatively connected to the cost. This is a weak justification concern at low-to-moderate severity rather than a missing disclosure.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report discloses: (1) higher charges of 0.08% / £63 first-year outperformance requirement; (2) total cost including 1% adviser fee of 1.62% vs 0.54%, requiring 1.08% annual outperformance; (3) recommended funds had lower historical returns but less severe losses than existing funds; (4) access delay of 10-15 working days; (5) transaction costs excluded from quoted charges; (6) charge calculation may differ from illustration; (7) earnings uncertainty and tax charge risk if contributions exceed earnings; (8) pension age 55 rising to 57 from April 2028; (9) IHT legislative change from April 2027 for unused DC pensions; (10) death benefit tax treatment pre/post age 75; (11) cancellation risk — previous provider not obliged to take plan back; (12) cancellation market loss risk; (13) nomination uncertainty with Expression of Wish form posted out.
    File:     The pension switch report (Defaqto) shows: at mid growth rate the new solution projects £86,700 vs existing £86,800 (£100 or 0.1% decrease effect of switching); at high growth rate £97,300 vs £97,400 (£100 decrease); at low growth rate both £74,000 (£0 decrease). The existing pension has no listed benefits that would be lost on switching ('No benefits were listed for the existing products'). The existing plan has no transfer penalty (£0.00) and no Market Value Adjustment/Early Transfer Charges. The Aviva fund factsheet (P2/P6) assigns risk warnings A (general — value not guaranteed, can go down as well as up), B (foreign exchange), C (emerging markets), E (fixed interest), F (derivatives) to the recommended Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II. The CT Universal MAP Cautious KIID (P4) identifies derivative risk, fixed interest securities risk, credit risk, smaller companies risk, emerging markets risk, and liquidity risk for the GIA/ISA fund. The KIID also states the fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years.' The Aviva Key Features document (P10) notes that if death benefits are paid more than two years after death, income tax may apply, and that a lump sum paid to a trust or personal representatives attracts a 45% charge. Research reports confirm individual discrete performance data including a -9.65% combined loss year in 2022 for the existing holdings.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II factsheet assigns specific risk warnings: B (Foreign Exchange Risk), C (Emerging Markets), E (Fixed Interest), F (Derivatives) — none of these fund-specific risk categories are named or referenced in the suitability report's risk disclosure section.
      - The CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund KIID (used for the GIA and ISA) identifies liquidity risk, credit risk, smaller companies risk, derivative risk, fixed interest securities risk and emerging markets risk — none of these are disclosed in the report in connection with the GIA or ISA recommendations.
      - The CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund KIID states the fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' — the report does not flag this minimum recommended holding period for the GIA or ISA.
      - The Aviva Key Features document notes that a lump sum death benefit paid to a trust or personal representatives attracts a 45% tax charge, and that income tax may apply if benefits are paid more than two years after death — these nuances are not disclosed in the report's death benefits section, which covers only the broad pre/post-75 treatment.
      - The pension switch report records 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' — the report does not explicitly state that no valuable guarantees or benefits are being surrendered, though this absence is consistent with the switch report finding.
      - The report's risk disclosures relate primarily to the pension recommendation; no product-specific investment risks are disclosed for the GIA or ISA wrapper recommendations.
    The report contains a substantive risk disclosure section covering the most financially material items: the higher charges (both ex-adviser and including adviser fee), the outperformance requirement, historical return comparison, access delays, earnings/contribution uncertainty, pension access age change, IHT legislative change, death benefit tax treatment, cancellation risks and nomination uncertainty. These are client-specific and prominent.
    
    However, the product literature in the file identifies fund-specific risks for both recommended funds that are entirely absent from the report. For the pension (Aviva Insured Funds Multi-Asset Plus Fund II), the factsheet assigns risk warnings B, C, E and F — foreign exchange, emerging markets, fixed interest and derivatives risks — and the general investment risk (A: value not guaranteed). For the GIA and ISA (CT Universal MAP Cautious Fund), the KIID identifies derivative, fixed interest, credit, smaller companies, emerging markets and liquidity risks. None of these named, fund-specific risk categories appear anywhere in the report.
    
    The 'A — General' risk warning from the Aviva factsheet (value not guaranteed, can go down as well as up) is the most basic investment risk disclosure. While a court might expect this to be implicit, COBS 4.2.1R requires fair, clear and not misleading disclosure, and COBS 9.4.7R requires disadvantages and risks of the recommendation to be explained. The absence of any investment risk disclosure for the GIA and ISA is more serious: those products receive no fund-specific risk commentary at all in the report.
    
    The CT Universal MAP Cautious KIID's statement that the fund 'may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw their money within 5 years' is a material limitation. The GIA is funded from inheritance earmarked partly for near-term renovations (£40,000 set aside), and the client is recently retired — the time horizon for potential access is relevant. This holding period warning is absent from the report.
    
    The death benefits section omits the 45% charge applicable to lump sum benefits paid to trusts or personal representatives, and the income tax risk where benefits are paid more than two years after death, both noted in the Aviva Key Features document.
    
    The generic 'guard' that product literature generic market-risk wording need not be repeated does not excuse the complete absence of fund-specific risk categories for the GIA and ISA, where the report provides no investment risk disclosure at all for those products. The pension report's risk section is more complete but still omits named fund risk categories from the factsheet.
    
    This is a disclosure shortfall: the report does not repeat or reference the fund-specific risks identified in the product literature for the GIA/ISA, and omits the minimum holding period warning that is material to a recently retired client. The severity is moderate: generic risks are present and the most financially material pension-specific risks are disclosed, but there are identifiable gaps in fund-specific investment risk disclosure for two of the three recommended products.

CHK-010 — Vulnerability, Support Needs and Foreseeable Harm Overlay
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 4 raised a concern (G10.1, G10.3, G10.4, G10.5). The suitability report infers thirdPartySupport.isPresent = false; this is consistent with the fact find recording no one else present at the meeting, but the basis for the inferred PoA grant is unsupported by any evidence.   [G10.1] Details of additional support and needs, including third-party support
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The canonical model records that both clients have a Power of Attorney granted (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted = true for both C1 and C2). No third-party support is recorded as being exercised during the advice process (thirdPartySupport.isPresent = false, inferred at confidence 0.7).     File:     The fact find [P7] records 'Accessible format requirement: No Requirement' and 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference'. The risk profile [P14] records no additional notes on investment target or agreed risk level [P11]. The fact find [P1] records very low investment knowledge and experience (no understanding of stocks and shares, very little of pension planning). No Power of Attorney document, LPA record, or third-party authority document is present in the pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model asserts isPowerOfAttorneyGranted = true for both clients, but no Power of Attorney or LPA document, and no reference to any such instrument, appears anywhere in the case file evidence.
      - The suitability report infers thirdPartySupport.isPresent = false; this is consistent with the fact find recording no one else present at the meeting, but the basis for the inferred PoA grant is unsupported by any evidence.
      - No vulnerability assessment outcome is recorded in the report (confirmed absent by extraction as NotPresentInDocument); no vulnerability assessment document or section appears in the file either.
      - The client presents FG21/1 drivers (very low investment knowledge and understanding per [P1], recently retired life event, low financial capability indicators) but neither the report nor any other file document records a structured vulnerability or capability assessment.
      - No support needs, communication adjustments or accessible format requirements are recorded in the report; the fact find records 'No Requirement' for accessible format and 'No Preference' for delivery method, which is consistent but represents a minimal evidential basis for a client with very low investment knowledge.
    The most material issue is the canonical model's assertion that a Power of Attorney has been granted by both clients. This is a specific factual claim with regulatory significance under FCA FG21/1 (third-party authority is one of the named indicators). No LPA document, no reference to a PoA in the fact find, and no mention in the client agreement or risk profile supports this assertion. The extraction note acknowledges the PoA 'is noted as existing but is not indicated as being exercised', suggesting the report itself references it, but the supporting file contains nothing to corroborate the grant. The claim is therefore unsupported by evidence, which is a Potential Concern in its own right.
    
    The guard distinguishes a recommendation to put a PoA in place (future planning) from evidence of a current instrument. The canonical model asserts current grant (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted = true), not a recommendation — so the guard does not apply here.
    
    The second concern is the complete absence of any vulnerability assessment. The client exhibits clear FG21/1 drivers: very low investment knowledge (no understanding of stocks and shares, very little of pension planning per [P1]), a recent life event (retirement), and receipt of an inheritance — all recognised FG21/1 triggers. Neither the report nor any file document records a structured vulnerability or consumer duty capability assessment. The extraction confirms assessmentOutcome is NotPresentInDocument. This is not an extraction failure: the evidence pack itself contains no such record.
    
    The accessible format and delivery preferences in the fact find [P7] are recorded as 'No Requirement' and 'No Preference', which is internally consistent with the report's silence on adjustments, but given the low-capability profile this minimal record does not demonstrate that the question was meaningfully explored.
    
    The thirdPartySupport.isPresent = false inference is consistent with the fact find showing no one else at the meeting [P20], but this relates to the meeting attendance point, not to the PoA grant point, which remains unsupported.
    
    Overall: two distinct concerns — unsupported PoA assertion and absent vulnerability assessment in a case with clear FG21/1 drivers — make this a Potential Concern at High severity.

  [G10.3] Adjustments made to support client comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model holds no value at the vulnerability/perClient[]/adjustmentsMade[] or vulnerability/perClient[]/characteristics[] paths. The extraction confirms these paths are absent from the document (NotPresentInDocument).     The case presents a clear low-capability FG21/1 driver: the client is explicitly described as having very little investment knowledge and not finding financial matters easy to understand [P12]. The ATRQ being sent for self-completion without any noted comprehension support is an additional concern given the stated low financial literacy.   [G10.4] Vulnerability has a material impact on the recommendation (overlay)
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report records one foreseeable harm: the client has limited investment knowledge and may not fully understand the risks of the recommended products (SIPP and GIA). The stated mitigation is that a suitability report with product literature, risk warnings and ongoing adviser oversight was provided, and that investment pathway options were considered and discounted with explanation. The report describes the client as having 'very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing', no understanding of unit trusts, stocks and shares or EIS, but fair understanding of cash and Cash ISAs. The capacity-for-loss section notes Medium-High capacity and explains why. No formal vulnerability assessment outcome is recorded (/vulnerability/assessmentOutcome is absent — confirmed as NotPresentInDocument by the extraction). No impact-on-recommendation mapping is recorded (/vulnerability/perClient[]/impactOnRecommendation[] is absent). No mention of communication adjustments, accessible formats, third-party authority, Power of Attorney or trusted contacts beyond the fact-find's standard fields. The report does not connect the low-knowledge finding to any specific adjustment in the recommendation structure, product choice or communication method.
    File:     The risk-profile questionnaire (P2) records the client's own self-assessment: 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares and EIS; 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and income in retirement. The fact-find (P14) records: Accessible format requirement — No Requirement; Preferred delivery method — No Preference. No vulnerability indicators (health, cognitive, bereavement, financial stress) are recorded anywhere in the fact-find or adviser notes. The adviser notes (P3, P4) are purely commercial — they discuss investment amounts, ISA allowances and pension contributions, with no reference to vulnerability assessment or any support need. The capacity-for-loss questionnaire (P1) records 'No additional notes were recorded'. The agreed risk-level section (P12) records 'No additional notes were recorded' against both investment target and agreed risk level. The risk-profile report (P7, P15) describes the client as a Cautious Balanced investor who 'may have limited or moderate experience of investments and does not find financial matters particularly easy to understand' — consistent with low knowledge but not a formal vulnerability finding. No FG21/1 vulnerability assessment document exists in the pack. No Power of Attorney, trusted contact or third-party authority document is present.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts a foreseeable harm arising from limited investment knowledge and lists mitigation (suitability report, product literature, risk warnings, ongoing oversight) but does not record any formal vulnerability assessment outcome anywhere, and no such assessment exists in the file.
      - The report identifies limited knowledge as a foreseeable harm but does not record how this characteristic materially affected the recommendation — the specific product choice (SIPP, ISA, GIA), fund selection or communication approach is not linked back to the knowledge gap.
      - The report provides no account of whether communication or format adjustments were made in response to the knowledge gap; the fact-find records 'No Requirement' and 'No Preference' but neither the report nor the file explains how the adviser satisfied themselves these defaults were appropriate given the self-reported very low investment knowledge.
      - No FG21/1 vulnerability assessment process is documented anywhere in the case file — the extraction confirms /vulnerability/assessmentOutcome is NotPresentInDocument and no corroborating document exists.
      - The report is silent on Power of Attorney, trusted contacts and third-party involvement; the file contains no such documents or references to them having been considered.
      - The capacity-for-loss notes section records 'No additional notes were recorded', yet the suitability report makes detailed assertions about Medium-High capacity for loss — these assertions are made solely in the report with no supporting discussion notes in the file.
    The trigger is satisfied: the canonical model flags hasVulnerabilityIndicators = true and the extraction recorded a foreseeable harm centred on limited investment knowledge, corroborated by the risk-profile questionnaire (P2) which records 'No understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares and 'Very little understanding / knowledge' of pension planning. The knowledge gap is therefore a genuine, evidenced characteristic. The guards note that age alone is not vulnerability and that a genuine absence of vulnerability, if assessed, is not a finding. Neither guard applies here: the issue is not that vulnerability is absent but that (a) no formal vulnerability assessment was conducted or documented — /vulnerability/assessmentOutcome is confirmed absent and no FG21/1 assessment document exists in the pack — and (b) the identified characteristic (very low investment knowledge across the very products being recommended) does not visibly influence the recommendation reasoning. The report lists a foreseeable harm and generic mitigation but does not explain how the product choice, fund selection, or the decision to recommend a SIPP and GIA to a client with 'No understanding / knowledge' of unit trusts and GIAs was shaped by that finding. The mitigation stated (suitability report, product literature, risk warnings) is standard and could apply to any case; it does not address whether simpler or more structured products were considered or why they were discounted for this client. The fact-find (P14) records no accessible format requirement and no preferred delivery method, but neither the report nor any adviser note explains how these defaults were arrived at in the context of very low financial literacy. The capacity-for-loss notes (P1) record 'No additional notes were recorded', leaving the report's Medium-High capacity assertion unsupported by any discussion note. Taken together: a material FG21/1 driver (low capability / low financial knowledge) is documented in the file and partially acknowledged in the report, but the vulnerability assessment process was never completed or documented, and the report does not demonstrate that the characteristic materially influenced the recommendation. This is a compliance concern and weak justification at moderate severity — the characteristic does not appear to make the recommendation unsuitable on its face, but the absence of a documented assessment and the failure to connect the finding to the recommendation mean the duty of care obligation under PRIN 2A.5 and FG21/1 cannot be verified.

  [G10.5] Vulnerabilities recorded in the file and in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model records hasVulnerability as 'No' for client one (Anthony Barnes) and 'NotAssessed' for client two (Yvette). No vulnerability categories, characteristics or assessment date are present in the extracted model. The extraction's own report confirms /vulnerability/assessmentOutcome is absent because it is 'NotPresentInDocument', meaning the report itself does not contain a formal vulnerability assessment outcome. The report records the meeting type as telephone, notes knowledge and experience limitations (very little understanding of investing), and records capacity for loss as Medium-High, but carries no documented vulnerability screen, no communication adjustments, and no support needs.
    File:     The fact find (P1) records 'Accessible format requirement: No Requirement' and 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference'. The meeting was conducted by telephone (P7). No vulnerability flag, health indicator, or support-need marker appears in any fact-find field. The adviser notes (P8, P9) record that the client is retired, receives DB income, and was working part-time; there is no mention of vulnerability, health issues, cognitive concerns, or third-party authority. The ATRQ was not completed at the meeting — the client was sent an email version to complete in his own time (P1, P3). No Power of Attorney, trusted contact, or representative is referenced anywhere in the file. No FG21/1 driver (health event, bereavement, life event beyond retirement, low financial resilience, or low capability flag) is recorded in the supporting documents beyond the noted low investment knowledge, which the report does acknowledge.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report records hasVulnerability as 'No' for Anthony Barnes. The file does not positively contradict this — no vulnerability is flagged — but no formal vulnerability assessment or screening record exists anywhere in the pack to support the 'No' conclusion: it is an unsubstantiated assertion.
      - The canonical model records hasVulnerability as 'NotAssessed' for the second client (Yvette). The file contains no vulnerability assessment for Yvette either. The report's own extraction confirms /vulnerability/assessmentOutcome is NotPresentInDocument, meaning even the 'No' for Anthony is not backed by a documented assessment outcome in the report.
      - The ATRQ was not completed at the meeting and was sent for self-completion (P1, P3). This is a low-capability indicator under FG21/1 (client required assistance or deferral for a standard questionnaire) that is not acknowledged in the report's vulnerability or support-needs section.
      - The report acknowledges the client has 'very little understanding or knowledge when it comes to investing' (P18). Low financial capability is a FG21/1 driver. No adjustments, additional explanation measures, or support steps are recorded in the report in response to this characteristic.
      - No accessible-format or communication adjustment is documented in the report, though the fact find records 'No Requirement' and 'No Preference' — these defaults are not positively evidenced as having been checked with the client in the context of vulnerability screening.
    The file does not record any positive vulnerability indicator for Anthony Barnes or Yvette beyond the low investment knowledge noted in both the fact find and the suitability report. The fact find records 'No Requirement' for accessible formats and 'No Preference' for delivery method, which is consistent with — though not independently corroborative of — the report's 'No' vulnerability conclusion for Anthony. However, the report's 'No' finding is itself unsubstantiated: there is no vulnerability screening record, no assessment outcome, and no documented process by which the conclusion was reached. The extraction confirms /vulnerability/assessmentOutcome is NotPresentInDocument. Under FG21/1, a concluded 'No vulnerability' still requires an evidenced assessment; here the file holds only defaults and blanks. Two specific drivers are present in the file that the report does not address in a vulnerability or support context: (1) the ATRQ was deferred because the client did not have time to complete it at the meeting — a process indicator of potential engagement or capability concern that warranted at least a note; (2) the client's acknowledged very low investment knowledge is a FG21/1 low-capability driver, and the report does not record any adjusted explanation, additional support step, or confirmation that the client's understanding was checked before proceeding. The 'NotAssessed' status for Yvette is unexplained and unremedied. The age-guard applies: the client's age (55) is not itself a vulnerability indicator. The 'resolved or historic' guard does not apply as no historic vulnerability is recorded. None of the discrepancies is explained by the available guards, though the magnitude is moderate rather than high — no health event, bereavement, or materially distressed financial position is recorded. The primary concern is the absence of a documented vulnerability screen and the unaddressed low-capability indicator.
